Ah! I see. Each edge is between a capital and a non-adjacent city. Since the edge between a capital and a city is undirected, we should avoid double-counting when we have two capitals.

So in the algorithm, for each capital, we consider all cities that are not adjacent to it. But if we do this for each capital, we might count the same edge twice (e.g., capital 1 to city 4 and capital 4 to city 1).

However, since the edge is between city 4 and city 1, and both are capitals, we only want to count it once.

But actually, in this case, city 4 is a capital, and city 1 is a capital, so the edge (1,4) should be counted only once.

In our iteration, when we go through capital 1, we consider (1,4) because city 4 is not adjacent to city 1.
When we go through capital 4, we consider (4,1) because city 1 is not adjacent to city 4.

But (1,4) and (4,1) are the same edge.

So we are double-counting edges between two capitals that are not adjacent.

For any edge (i,j) where both i and j are capitals and not adjacent, we would count it twice.

Therefore, we need to modify our approach.

Better approach:
1. Start with the sum of all cyclic edges
2. Add the sum of all edges (cap_city, other_city) where other_city is not adjacent to cap_city
3. But since edges between two capitals are counted twice (once from each capital), we need to subtract the sum of edges between two capitals that are not adjacent

Let me verify with example 2:
- Cyclic sum = 49
- Add edges from capitals to non-adjacent cities:
  - From capital 1: non-adjacent cities are 3 and 4 → (1,3):6, (1,4):6 → sum=12
  - From capital 4: non-adjacent cities are 1 and 2 → (4,1):6, (4,2):10 → sum=16
  - Total before correction = 49 + 12 + 16 = 77
- Subtract the double-counted edges between two capitals that are not adjacent
- Which edges between two capitals are not adjacent?
  - Capital 1 and 4: are they adjacent? In cycle 1-2-3-4-5-1, 1 is adjacent to 2 and 5, 4 is adjacent to 3 and 5. So 1 and 4 are not adjacent.
  - So edge (1,4) should be counted only once, but we counted it twice (once in each capital's iteration)
- The price of edge (1,4) is 3*2 = 6
- So we need to subtract 6 (since we counted it twice but should count it once)
- Total = 77 - 6 = 71 ✓

This matches!

So the algorithm is:

1. Calculate the sum of all cyclic edges: sum_{i=1}^{n-1} c_i * c_{i+1} + c_n * c_1
2. For each capital city, for each other city that is not adjacent to it, add c_cap * c_other
3. For each pair of capitals (i,j) where i < j and they are not adjacent, subtract c_i * c_j (since it was counted twice)

But step 2 might be inefficient (O(n*k)) and step 3 might be O(k^2).

We can optimize:

For step 2: we can loop through each capital and add contributions to non-adjacent cities.

For step 3: we need to find pairs of capitals that are not adjacent.

In a cycle of n cities, two cities i and j are adjacent if |i-j| = 1 or (i=1 and j=n) or (j=1 and i=n). For capital cities, they are not adjacent if |i-j| > 1 and not (i=1 and j=n) etc.

Actually, for two distinct indices i and j in a cycle, they are adjacent if the minimum distance between them is 1.

We can compute the sum of products for all pairs of capitals that are not adjacent.

Total sum of products for all pairs of capitals = sum_{i<j} c_i * c_j

Sum of products for adjacent pairs of capitals = sum over pairs of capitals that are adjacent (in the cycle)

Then, the sum for non-adjacent pairs = total - adjacent pairs

Then, we need to subtract this from the sum we got in step 2.

Actually, in our algorithm, when we go through each capital, and add c_cap * c_other for non-adjacent other city, we are counting each non-adjacent edge twice (once from each capital).

For edges between two capitals that are not adjacent, the price is c_i * c_j and it is counted twice in the sum from step 2.

For edges between a capital and a non-capital that are not adjacent, the price is c_cap * c_other and it is counted once (only from the capital side).

So the only double-counting is for edges between two capitals that are not adjacent.

Therefore, the final answer is:
- cyclic_sum + (sum of c_cap * c_other for all capitals and non-adjacent other cities) - (sum of c_i * c_j for all pairs of capitals that are not adjacent)

Let me write it clearly:

Let `total = 0`
- total += sum of cyclic edges (adjacent pairs)
- total += sum over each capital city x of (sum over all cities y ≠ x such that (x,y) is not an adjacent pair of c_x * c_y)
- total -= sum over all pairs of distinct capitals (i,j) such that (i,j) is not adjacent of (c_i * c_j)

The last term is the sum over all pairs of capitals (i,j) where i < j and they are not adjacent, of c_i * c_j.

We can compute this as:
- total_pairs = sum_{i<j, i,j in capitals} c_i * c_j
- adjacent_pairs_sum = sum over pairs of capitals (i,j) that are adjacent in the cycle of c_i * c_j
- non_adjacent_pairs_sum = total_pairs - adjacent_pairs_sum

Then subtract non_adjacent_pairs_sum from total.

But actually, in our sum in step 2, we counted each non-adjacent capital pair twice (once from each capital), so we need to subtract the amount of double-counting, which is exactly non_adjacent_pairs_sum.

So algorithm:

1. Read inputs
2. Compute cyclic_sum = sum of c[i]*c[i+1] for i=0 to n-2 + c[n-1]*c[0]
3. Compute capital_sum = 0
   - For each capital city x (with index i):
     - For each city y (index j) where j ≠ i and (i,j) is not adjacent:
       - capital_sum += c[i] * c[j]
4. Compute capital_pairs_sum = sum of products for all pairs of distinct capitals (i,j) with i < j
5. Compute adjacent_capital_pairs_sum = sum of products for pairs of capitals that are adjacent in the cycle
6. non_adjacent_capital_pairs_sum = capital_pairs_sum - adjacent_capital_pairs_sum
7. result = cyclic_sum + capital_sum - non_adjacent_capital_pairs_sum

Let me verify with example 1:
- n=4, k=1, capitals=[3] (index 2), c=[2,3,1,2]
- cyclic_sum = (2*3) + (3*1) + (1*2) + (2*2) = 6+3+2+4 = 15
- capital_sum: only capital at index 2
  - non-adjacent cities: cities not adjacent to 2
  - adjacent to 2: city 1 (index 1) and city 3 (index 3) 
  - so only city at index 0 (city 1) is not adjacent to 2? 
  - index 0: city 1, is it adjacent to 2? Index 2 is adjacent to 1 and 3. 
  - Index 0 is not directly adjacent to index 2? In cycle 0-1-2-3-0, index 2 is adjacent to 1 and 3.
  - So index 0 is not adjacent to 2.
  - So add c[2] * c[0] = 1 * 2 = 2
  - capital_sum = 2
- capital_pairs_sum: only one capital, so 0
- adjacent_capital_pairs_sum: 0
- non_adjacent_capital_pairs_sum: 0
- result = 15 + 2 - 0 = 17 ✓

Example 2:
- n=5, k=2, capitals=[1,4] (indices 0 and 3), c=[3,5,2,2,4]
- cyclic_sum = (3*5) + (5*2) + (2*2) + (2*4) + (4*3) = 15+10+4+8+12 = 49
- capital_sum:
  - capital at 0 (city 1):
    - adjacent: city 1 (index 1) and city 5 (index 4)
    - non-adjacent: indices 2,3
    - add c[0]*c[2] = 3*2 = 6, c[0]*c[3] = 3*2 = 6 → sum = 12
  - capital at 3 (city 4):
    - adjacent: city 3 (index 2) and city 5 (index 4)
    - non-adjacent: indices 0,1
    - add c[3]*c[0] = 2*3 = 6, c[3]*c[1] = 2*5 = 10 → sum = 16
  - capital_sum = 12 + 16 = 28
- capital_pairs_sum: pairs of capitals (0,3): c[0]*c[3] = 3*2 = 6
- adjacent_capital_pairs_sum: are capitals 0 and 3 adjacent in cycle?
  - 0 is adjacent to 1 and 4
  - 3 is adjacent to 2 and 4
  - 0 and 3 are not adjacent (since 0-1-2-3 and 4-0, so 0 and 3 are separated by 1,2 or by 4,3)
  - not adjacent, so adjacent_capital_pairs_sum = 0
- non_adjacent_capital_pairs_sum = 6 - 0 = 6
- result = 49 + 28 - 6 = 71 ✓

Perfect!

Now for the implementation, note that the adjacency check for city i and city j is that they are adjacent if |i-j| = 1 or (i=0 and j=n-1) or (j=0 and i=n-1).

In 0-indexed, cities i and j are adjacent if:
- |i-j| = 1, or
- (i=0 and j=n-1), or
- (i=n-1 and j=0)

For the capital_sum, for a capital at index i, we need to exclude cities j where |i-j| == 1 or (i==0 and j==n-1) or (i==n-1 and j==0).

We can implement this.

For the capital_pairs_sum, we need to sum over all pairs of capitals, and for each pair, if they are not adjacent, add their product.

Let me code accordingly.

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Convert to 0-indexed
capitals = [x-1 for x in capitals]

# Step 1: compute cyclic sum
cyclic_sum = 0
for i in range(n-1):
    cyclic_sum += c[i] * c[i+1]
cyclic_sum += c[n-1] * c[0]

# Step 2: compute capital_sum - sum of c[i]*c[j] for each capital i and non-adjacent city j
capital_sum = 0
for cap_idx in capitals:
    # Find all cities that are not adjacent to cap_idx
    for j in range(n):
        if j == cap_idx:
            continue
        # Check if j is adjacent to cap_idx
        if abs(cap_idx - j) == 1:
            continue
        if (cap_idx == 0 and j == n-1) or (cap_idx == n-1 and j == 0):
            continue
        capital_sum += c[cap_idx] * c[j]

# Step 3: compute sum of products for all pairs of distinct capitals
total_capital_pairs = 0
adjacent_capital_pairs = 0

# Sum over all pairs of distinct capitals
for i in range(len(capitals)):
    for j in range(i+1, len(capitals)):
        idx1, idx2 = capitals[i], capitals[j]
        # Check if they are adjacent in the cycle
        if abs(idx1 - idx2) == 1:
            adjacent_capital_pairs += c[idx1] * c[idx2]
        elif (idx1 == 0 and idx2 == n-1) or (idx1 == n-1 and idx2 == 0):
            adjacent_capital_pairs += c[idx1] * c[idx2]
        else:
            # They are not adjacent, so include in total_capital_pairs
            total_capital_pairs += c[idx1] * c[idx2]

# The sum of products for non-adjacent capital pairs
non_adjacent_capital_pairs = total_capital_pairs - adjacent_capital_pairs

# Final result
result = cyclic_sum + capital_sum - non_adjacent_capital_pairs
print(result)
```

Wait, in the step 3, I computed `total_capital_pairs` as including all pairs, and `adjacent_capital_pairs` as the pairs that are adjacent. But actually, in the `total_capital_pairs`, I am only including non-adjacent pairs, and in adjacent, I am including adjacent ones.

Actually, I want:
- `total_capital_pairs` = sum over all pairs of distinct capitals of c[i]*c[j]
- `adjacent_capital_pairs` = sum over pairs of distinct capitals that are adjacent of c[i]*c[j]
- `non_adjacent_capital_pairs` = total_capital_pairs - adjacent_capital_pairs

Yes, that's correct.

And in the loop, I compute `total_capital_pairs` only for non-adjacent pairs, which is wrong.

I should compute `total_capital_pairs` as sum over all pairs of distinct capitals of c[i]*c[j].

So:

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Convert to 0-indexed
capitals = [x-1 for x in capitals]

# Step 1: compute cyclic sum
cyclic_sum = 0
for i in range(n-1):
    cyclic_sum += c[i] * c[i+1]
cyclic_sum += c[n-1] * c[0]

# Step 2: compute capital_sum - sum of c[i]*c[j] for each capital i and non-adjacent city j
capital_sum = 0
for cap_idx in capitals:
    # Find all cities that are not adjacent to cap_idx
    for j in range(n):
        if j == cap_idx:
            continue
        # Check if j is adjacent to cap_idx
        if abs(cap_idx - j) == 1:
            continue
        if (cap_idx == 0 and j == n-1) or (cap_idx == n-1 and j == 0):
            continue
        capital_sum += c[cap_idx] * c[j]

# Step 3: compute sum of products for all pairs of distinct capitals
total_capital_pairs = 0
adjacent_capital_pairs = 0

# Sum over all pairs of distinct capitals
for i in range(len(capitals)):
    for j in range(i+1, len(capitals)):
        idx1, idx2 = capitals[i], capitals[j]
        # Check if they are adjacent in the cycle
        if abs(idx1 - idx2) == 1:
            adjacent_capital_pairs += c[idx1] * c[idx2]
        elif (idx1 == 0 and idx2 == n-1) or (idx1 == n-1 and idx2 == 0):
            adjacent_capital_pairs += c[idx1] * c[idx2]
        else:
            # This pair is not adjacent, so it will be in total_capital_pairs
            total_capital_pairs += c[idx1] * c[idx2]

# This is actually wrong - total_capital_pairs should be the sum over all pairs
# So I need to compute total_capital_pairs as sum over all pairs (i,j) with i<j
total_capital_pairs = 0
adjacent_capital_pairs = 0
for i in range(len(capitals)):
    for j in range(i+1, len(capitals)):
        idx1, idx2 = capitals[i], capitals[j]
        if abs(idx1 - idx2) == 1 or (idx1 == 0 and idx2 == n-1) or (idx1 == n-1 and idx2 == 0):
            adjacent_capital_pairs += c[idx1] * c[idx2]
        else:
            total_capital_pairs += c[idx1] * c[idx2]

# Actually, total_capital_pairs is only the non-adjacent pairs in the capitals
# So non_adjacent_capital_pairs = total_capital_pairs
non_adjacent_capital_pairs = total_capital_pairs

result = cyclic_sum + capital_sum - non_adjacent_capital_pairs
print(result)
```

This should work.

But let me test with the examples.

Example 1: k=1, so no pairs of capitals → total_capital_pairs = 0 → result = 15 + 2 - 0 = 17 ✓

Example 2: k=2, capital_indices=[0,3]
- Pairs of capitals: only (0,3)
- |0-3| = 3 ≠ 1, and (0,3) is not (0,n-1) since n-1=4, so (0,3) is not adjacent.
- So total_capital_pairs += 3*2 = 6
- adjacent_capital_pairs = 0
- non_adjacent_capital_pairs = 6
- result = 49 + 28 - 6 = 71 ✓

Perfect!

Now, the time complexity is O(n*k + k^2). Since n,k ≤ 100,000, k^2 could be up to 10^10, which is too slow.

We need to optimize.

How can we optimize?

For capital_sum: we are doing O(n*k) which in worst case k=100,000, n=100,000 → 10^10, which is too slow in Python.

For the capital pairs: we are doing O(k^2), which in worst case k=100,000 → 10^10.

We need to optimize both.

First, for capital_sum: we want to compute sum over each capital i of (sum of c[j] for j not adjacent to i and j not in capitals) times c[i].

But actually, for a capital at index i, we want:
- sum over j not adjacent to i of (c[i] * c[j]) = c[i] * (sum of c[j] for j not adjacent to i)

So we can precompute the sum of all c[j] for all j, then subtract the adjacent cities and the capital cities.

But note: the cities adjacent to i are specific ones.

For a capital at i, the adjacent cities are:
- j such that |i-j| == 1 or (i==0 and j==n-1) or (i==n-1 and j==0)

So the sum of c[j] for j adjacent to i can be computed directly.

Also, we need to exclude j that are capitals (since we are only adding to non-capital cities? Wait no - in the problem, the capital connects to every other city, including other capitals).

In our earlier analysis, we are connecting to every other city except adjacent ones, and we are not excluding other capitals.

In the capital_sum, we are including edges to other capitals that are not adjacent.

For example, in example 2, we have capital at 0 and 3, and we add edge (0,3) because it's not adjacent.

So we should not exclude other capitals.

Therefore, for a capital at i, the sum of c[j] for j not adjacent to i is:
- total_sum - sum of c[j] for j adjacent to i

But this includes other capitals.

We can compute this way.

Let total_sum = sum(c)

Then for a capital at i, adjacent_sum_i = sum of c[j] for j adjacent to i

Then the sum of c[j] for j not adjacent to i = total_sum - adjacent_sum_i

Then contribution of capital i to capital_sum is c[i] * (total_sum - adjacent_sum_i)

But wait - this includes edges to other capitals that are not adjacent, which is correct.

So capital_sum = sum over each capital i of c[i] * (total_sum - adjacent_sum_i)

This is O(k), which is much better.

Now for the capital pairs: we need to compute the sum of c[i]*c[j] for all pairs of distinct capitals that are not adjacent.

We can compute:
- total_capital_pairs_sum = sum over all pairs of distinct capitals of c[i]*c[j]
- adjacent_capital_pairs_sum = sum over pairs of distinct capitals that are adjacent of c[i]*c[j]

Then non_adjacent_capital_pairs = total_capital_pairs_sum - adjacent_capital_pairs_sum

We can compute total_capital_pairs_sum efficiently:
- Let S = sum of c[i] for i in capitals
- Then total_capital_pairs_sum = S*(S-1)//2 - (sum of c[i]^2 for i in capitals) + ? 

Actually, standard formula:
For a set of numbers a1,a2,...,am, the sum of products of all pairs is:
sum_{i<j} a_i * a_j = (sum a_i)^2 / 2 - (sum a_i^2) / 2

More precisely: (sum a_i)^2 = sum a_i^2 + 2 * sum_{i<j} a_i a_j
So sum_{i<j} a_i a_j = (sum a_i)^2 - sum a_i^2) / 2

Yes.

So:
- S = sum of c[i] for i in capitals
- S_sq = sum of c[i]^2 for i in capitals
- total_capital_pairs_sum = (S*S - S_sq) // 2

Then, adjacent_capital_pairs_sum: sum of c[i]*c[j] for adjacent pairs of capitals.

We can precompute the adjacency between capital indices.

A pair of capitals (i,j) is adjacent if they are adjacent in the cycle.

We can iterate over all pairs of capitals and check if they are adjacent, but that's O(k^2).

We can do better: for each capital, we can check its two adjacent positions (in the cycle).

For a capital at index i, the adjacent positions are:
- (i-1) % n and (i+1) % n

But we care about whether there is a capital at a neighboring position.

So we can:
- Create a set of capital indices for O(1) lookup
- For each capital at i, check if there is a capital at (i-1) % n or (i+1) % n
- If yes, add c[i] * c[neighbor] to adjacent_capital_pairs_sum

But we have to avoid double counting - each edge should be counted once.

So for each capital i, if there is a capital at j such that (i,j) are adjacent, then we add c[i]*c[j], but we only want to count each edge once.

We can iterate through each capital, and for each neighbor in the cycle, if that neighbor is also a capital, then add c[i]*c[neighbor].

Since each edge is between two capitals, and we are going to each capital and its neighbors, we will count each edge twice (once from each end).

So we should divide by 2.

Therefore:
- adjacent_capital_pairs_sum = 0
- For each capital i:
  - neighbor1 = (i-1) % n
  - neighbor2 = (i+1) % n
  - if neighbor1 in capital_set and neighbor1 != i:
    - adjacent_capital_pairs_sum += c[i] * c[neighbor1]
  - if neighbor2 in capital_set and neighbor2 != i:
    - adjacent_capital_pairs_sum += c[i] * c[neighbor2]
- Then adjacent_capital_pairs_sum //= 2

This is O(k), which is good.

Now, the overall complexity is O(k), which is acceptable.

Let me verify with examples.

Example 1: k=1, capital=[2] (index 2)
- total_sum = c[2] = 1
- S = 1, S_sq = 1, total_capital_pairs_sum = (1*1 - 1)//2 = 0
- adjacent_capital_pairs_sum: for capital at 2, neighbors: (1) and (3)
- no capitals at 1 or 3 (capitals=[2]), so adjacent_capital_pairs_sum = 0
- non_adjacent_capital_pairs = 0
- capital_sum: for capital at 2, adjacent_sum = c[1] + c[3] = 3 + 2 = 5
- total_sum = 2+3+1+2 = 8
- contribution = c[2] * (8 - 5) = 1 * 3 = 3
- But expected was 2 in example 1.

Wait, this is not matching.

What's the adjacent_sum for index 2?
- (2-1) % 4 = 1, (2+1) % 4 = 3
- c[1] = 3, c[3] = 2 → adjacent_sum = 3+2 = 5
- total_sum = 2+3+1+2 = 8
- contribution to capital_sum = 1 * (8 - 5) = 3

But we expected to add only c[2]*c[0] = 1*2 = 2

Why did we get 3?

Because in the adjacent_sum, we are including all adjacent cities, and then subtracting them from the total_sum.

But the cities adjacent to 2 are 1 and 3.

So sum of c[j] for j adjacent to 2 is 3+2=5.

Total sum is 8.

So sum for j not adjacent is 8 - 5 = 3.

Then c[2] * 3 = 3.

But we are supposed to add only the non-adjacent cities, which are city 0 (index 0) with c[0]=2.

So why is the sum 3?

Because the cities not adjacent to 2 are city 0 (index 0) and that's it? 
- Adjacent: 1 and 3
- So non-adjacent: 0
- Only one city, c[0]=2

So the sum should be 2.

But we calculated total_sum - adjacent_sum = 8 - 5 = 3.

8 - 5 = 3, but 3 should be the sum of c[j] for j not adjacent, which is only c[0]=2.

This is wrong.

The total_sum is the sum of all c[j] for j in 0 to n-1, which is 8.

adjacent_sum is the sum of c[j] for j adjacent to 2, which is c[1] + c[3] = 3+2=5.

So the sum of c[j] for j not adjacent to 2 should be 8 - 5 = 3, but that includes city 0 and possibly others? 

Cities: 0,1,2,3
- city 2 is excluded in this sum? 
- adjacent_sum is for j such that j is adjacent to 2, which are 1 and 3.
- So sum of c[j] for j not adjacent to 2 and j ≠ 2: only j=0.

Sum of c[0] = 2.

But 8 - 5 = 3, which is not 2.

Where is the error?

total_sum = sum of c[0] + c[1] + c[2] + c[3] = 2+3+1+2 = 8
adjacent_sum = c[1] + c[3] = 3+2 = 5
8 - 5 = 3

But 3 is not the sum of non-adjacent cities. What cities are not adjacent to 2?
- city 0: not adjacent
- city 1: adjacent
- city 3: adjacent
- city 2: is it adjacent to itself? No.

So only city 0 is not adjacent.

Sum of c[j] for j not adjacent to 2 is c[0] = 2.

But 8 - 5 = 3.

The issue is that we are subtracting only the adjacent cities, and 8 - 5 = 3, which is the sum of all c[j] for j not in {1,3}, which is c[0] + c[2] = 2 + 1 = 3.

And we are including city 2 itself.

In the capital_sum, we should only consider j ≠ i, and j not adjacent to i.

So we need to exclude city i as well.

Therefore, for a capital at i, the sum of c[j] for j not adjacent to i and j ≠ i should be:
total_sum - c[i] - adjacent_sum_i

Because:
- total_sum includes c[i] and all other cities
- adjacent_sum_i includes only the adjacent cities (which are not i)
- so total_sum - c[i] - adjacent_sum_i = sum of c[j] for j not adjacent to i and j ≠ i

Yes.

In example 1:
- total_sum = 8
- c[2] = 1
- adjacent_sum_i = 3+2 = 5
- sum of c[j] for j not adjacent to 2 and j ≠ 2 = 8 - 1 - 5 = 2
- then contribution = c[2] * 2 = 1 * 2 = 2 ✓

Perfect.

Similarly in example 2:
- capital indices: 0 and 3
- total_sum = 3+5+2+2+4 = 16
- for capital at 0:
  - adjacent: (0-1)%5=4, (0+1)%5=1
  - c[4]=4, c[1]=5 → adjacent_sum = 4+5=9
  - sum of c[j] for j not adjacent to 0 and j≠0 = 16 - c[0] - 9 = 16 - 3 - 9 = 4
  - contribution = 3 * 4 = 12
- for capital at 3:
  - adjacent: (2) and (4)
  - c[2]=2, c[4]=4 → adjacent_sum = 2+4=6
  - sum for j not adjacent to 3 and j≠3 = 16 - 2 - 6 = 8
  - contribution = 2 * 8 = 16
- total capital_sum = 12 + 16 = 28 ✓

Matches.

So now the algorithm:

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Convert to 0-indexed
capitals = [x-1 for x in capitals]

# Step 1: compute cyclic sum
cyclic_sum = 0
for i in range(n-1):
    cyclic_sum += c[i] * c[i+1]
cyclic_sum += c[n-1] * c[0]

# Step 2: compute capital_sum
total_sum = sum(c)
capital_sum = 0
capital_set = set(capitals)

for cap_idx in capitals:
    # Compute adjacent_sum for cap_idx
    adjacent_sum = 0
    # neighbors: (cap_idx-1) % n and (cap_idx+1) % n
    for neighbor in [(cap_idx-1) % n, (cap_idx+1) % n]:
        if neighbor in capital_set:
            # But we don't need to add if it's itself, but neighbor won't be cap_idx
            adjacent_sum += c[neighbor]
    
    # Sum of c[j] for j not adjacent to cap_idx and j != cap_idx
    sum_not_adjacent = total_sum - c[cap_idx] - adjacent_sum
    capital_sum += c[cap_idx] * sum_not_adjacent

# Step 3: compute sum of products for all pairs of distinct capitals that are not adjacent
S = sum(c[cap_idx] for cap_idx in capitals)
S_sq = sum(c[cap_idx] * c[cap_idx] for cap_idx in capitals)
total_capital_pairs_sum = (S*S - S_sq) // 2

adjacent_capital_pairs_sum = 0
# For each capital, check its two neighbors
for cap_idx in capitals:
    # neighbors: (cap_idx-1) % n and (cap_idx+1) % n
    for neighbor in [(cap_idx-1) % n, (cap_idx+1) % n]:
        if neighbor in capital_set and neighbor != cap_idx:
            # add c[cap_idx] * c[neighbor]
            adjacent_capital_pairs_sum += c[cap_idx] * c[neighbor]

# Since each edge is counted twice (once from each end), divide by 2
adjacent_capital_pairs_sum //= 2

non_adjacent_capital_pairs_sum = total_capital_pairs_sum - adjacent_capital_pairs_sum

result = cyclic_sum + capital_sum - non_adjacent_capital_pairs_sum
print(result)
```

Let me test with examples.

Example 1:
- cyclic_sum = 15
- capital_sum = 2
- S = 1, S_sq = 1, total_capital_pairs_sum = (1-1)//2 = 0
- adjacent_capital_pairs_sum: for cap_idx=2, neighbors: 1 and 3
  - 1 in capitals? no (capitals=[2]), 3 in capitals? no → 0
  - after //2 = 0
- non_adjacent_capital_pairs_sum = 0
- result = 15 + 2 - 0 = 17 ✓

    - 4 in capitals? yes (capitals=[0,3]), so add c[0]*c[4] = 3*4 = 12

  - cap_idx=3: neighbors 2 and 4
    - 2 in capitals? no
    - 4 in capitals? yes → add c[3]*c[4] = 2*4 = 8
  - total = 12+8 = 20, then //2 = 10
- non_adjacent_capital_pairs_sum = 6 - 10 = -4? 

This is negative!

In example 2, the capital pair is (0,3). Are they adjacent?
- 0 and 3: in cycle 0-1-2-3-4-0, 0 is adjacent to 1 and 4, 3 is adjacent to 2 and 4.
- So 0 and 3 are not adjacent (no edge between them).
- So adjacent_capital_pairs_sum should be 0.

But in our loop:
- For cap_idx=0, neighbor 4: is 4 in capitals? capitals are [0,3], 4 is not in it → no
- For cap_idx=0, neighbor 1: not in capitals → no
- For cap_idx=3, neighbor 2: not in capitals → no
- For cap_idx=3, neighbor 4: not in capitals → no
- So adjacent_capital_pairs_sum = 0
- After //2 = 0
- non_adjacent_capital_pairs_sum = 6 - 0 = 6
- result = 49 + 28 - 6 = 71 ✓

I made a mistake in the capital_set: capitals are [1,4] in input, which becomes [0,3] in 0-indexed.

- 4 in input is index 3 in 0-indexed? 1-based: city 1 is index 0, city 4 is index 3.

Yes.

So in the loop, for cap_idx=0: neighbors are (4) and (1)
- 4 is index 4, not in capitals (capitals are 0,3) → not in
- 1 is index 1, not in

For cap_idx=3: neighbors are (2) and (4)
- 2 and 4 not in capitals

So adjacent_capital_pairs_sum = 0.

Correct.

I think there's a typo in the manual calculation: when I said 4 is in capitals, it's not.

So the code is correct.

Let me write the final code.

Note: The adjacent_capital_pairs_sum might be large, but we are only adding when the neighbor is in capital_set.

Final code:

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Convert to 0-indexed
capitals = [x-1 for x in capitals]

# Step 1: compute cyclic sum
cyclic_sum = 0
for i in range(n-1):
    cyclic_sum += c[i] * c[i+1]
cyclic_sum += c[n-1] * c[0]

# Step 2: compute capital_sum
total_sum = sum(c)
capital_sum = 0
capital_set = set(capitals)

for cap_idx in capitals:
    # Compute adjacent_sum for cap_idx
    adjacent_sum = 0
    # neighbors: (cap_idx-1) % n and (cap_idx+1) % n
    for neighbor in [(cap_idx-1) % n, (cap_idx+1) % n]:
        # Only if neighbor is in the capital_set and not itself
        if neighbor in capital_set:
            adjacent_sum += c[neighbor]
    
    # Sum of c[j] for j not adjacent to cap_idx and j != cap_idx
    sum_not_adjacent = total_sum - c[cap_idx] - adjacent_sum
    capital_sum += c[cap_idx] * sum_not_adjacent

# Step 3: compute sum of products for all pairs of distinct capitals that are not adjacent
S = sum(c[cap_idx] for cap_idx in capitals)
S_sq = sum(c[cap_idx] * c[cap_idx] for cap_idx in capitals)
total_capital_pairs_sum = (S * S - S_sq) // 2

adjacent_capital_pairs_sum = 0
# For each capital, check its two neighbors
for cap_idx in capitals:
    for neighbor in [(cap_idx-1) % n, (cap_idx+1) % n]:
        if neighbor in capital_set and neighbor != cap_idx:
            adjacent_capital_pairs_sum += c[cap_idx] * c[neighbor]

# Since each edge is counted twice, divide by 2
adjacent_capital_pairs_sum //= 2

non_adjacent_capital_pairs_sum = total_capital_pairs_sum - adjacent_capital_pairs_sum

result = cyclic_sum + capital_sum - non_adjacent_capital_pairs_sum
print(result)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Little Mishka is a great traveller and she visited many countries. After thinking about where to travel this time, she chose XXX — beautiful, but little-known northern country.

Here are some interesting facts about XXX:

  1. XXX consists of n cities, k of whose (just imagine!) are capital cities. 
  2. All of cities in the country are beautiful, but each is beautiful in its own way. Beauty value of i-th city equals to ci. 
  3. All the cities are consecutively connected by the roads, including 1-st and n-th city, forming a cyclic route 1 — 2 — ... — n — 1. Formally, for every 1 ≤ i < n there is a road between i-th and i + 1-th city, and another one between 1-st and n-th city. 
  4. Each capital city is connected with each other city directly by the roads. Formally, if city x is a capital city, then for every 1 ≤ i ≤ n, i ≠ x, there is a road between cities x and i. 
  5. There is at most one road between any two cities. 
  6. Price of passing a road directly depends on beauty values of cities it connects. Thus if there is a road between cities i and j, price of passing it equals ci·cj.



Mishka started to gather her things for a trip, but didn't still decide which route to follow and thus she asked you to help her determine summary price of passing each of the roads in XXX. Formally, for every pair of cities a and b (a < b), such that there is a road between a and b you are to find sum of products ca·cb. Will you help her?

Input

The first line of the input contains two integers n and k (3 ≤ n ≤ 100 000, 1 ≤ k ≤ n) — the number of cities in XXX and the number of capital cities among them.

The second line of the input contains n integers c1, c2, ..., cn (1 ≤ ci ≤ 10 000) — beauty values of the cities.

The third line of the input contains k distinct integers id1, id2, ..., idk (1 ≤ idi ≤ n) — indices of capital cities. Indices are given in ascending order.

Output

Print the only integer — summary price of passing each of the roads in XXX.

Examples

Input

4 1
2 3 1 2
3


Output

17

Input

5 2
3 5 2 2 4
1 4


Output

71

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` cities arranged in a cycle: 1-2-...-n-1
2. There are `k` capital cities
3. For each capital city `x`, there's a direct road to every other city (including the cycle connections)
4. The price of a road between cities `i` and `j` is `c_i * c_j`
5. We need to find the sum of prices for all roads

## Analysis

Let me break down what roads exist:

1. **Cycle roads**: For each consecutive pair (i, i+1) where i < n, and (n, 1)
   - These are guaranteed to exist
   - Price for road (i, i+1) = `c_i * c_{i+1}`
   - Price for road (n, 1) = `c_n * c_1`

2. **Capital roads**: For each capital city `x`, there's a direct road to every other city `i ≠ x`
   - But we need to be careful - these roads might be duplicated with cycle roads
   - The problem states "at most one road between any two cities", so if a capital city already has a direct road to another city via the cycle, we only count it once

Then: "Each capital city is connected with each other city directly by the roads" - this adds direct roads.

So total roads = cycle roads + capital roads, but we need to avoid double counting.

However, the cycle roads already connect consecutive cities. For capital cities, they connect to every other city directly.

But if a capital city is already connected through the cycle to another city, do we add the direct road? The problem says "at most one road", so we should only count each pair once.

But the problem description says "for every pair of cities a and b (a < b), such that there is a road between a and b" - so we sum over all existing roads, not counting duplicates.

So we need to find all pairs (a,b) where a < b and there's a road between them.

The roads exist in two types:
1. Consecutive pairs in the cycle: (1,2), (2,3), ..., (n-1,n), (n,1)
2. Direct connections from each capital city to every other city

But we must avoid counting duplicates. A direct connection from capital x to city y will be counted if y > x, and we only count it once.

Actually, the key insight is that the cycle already has roads for consecutive cities. The capital roads add additional direct roads between a capital city and every other city, including non-consecutive ones.

So the total sum is:
- Sum of all cycle road prices (consecutive pairs and (n,1))
- Plus sum of all direct capital connections (from each capital to every other city) but only for pairs where the capital city is less than the other city, OR we need to be careful about ordering

Wait, no - the problem asks for sum of products for every pair (a,b) where a < b and there's a road between them.

- For each consecutive pair (i, i+1) where i < n+1 (with n+1 meaning 1), we have road (i, i+1)
- For each capital city x, and for each city y where y ≠ x, there is a direct road between x and y

- Capital roads from city 3: (3,1)=1*2=2, (3,2)=1*3=3, (3,4)=1*2=2
- But (3,2) and (3,4) are already in the cycle? (3,2) is the same as (2,3), and (3,4) is the same as (3,4)

I think I'm overcomplicating. Let me re-read: "Each capital city is connected with each other city directly by the roads. Formally, if city x is a capital city, then for every 1 ≤ i ≤ n, i ≠ x, there is a road between cities x and i."

Actually, this seems incorrect. Looking at the cycle structure, a direct road between capital x and city y exists, but if y is consecutive to x in the cycle, that road is already present.

The key is that the cycle already defines roads between consecutive cities. When we add capital roads, we're adding direct roads between capital city x and every other city y.

- Sum of all cycle road prices for consecutive pairs

- The cycle provides edges: (i, i+1) for i=1 to n-1, and (n,1)
- The capitals provide edges: (x,j) for each capital x and every j ≠ x

The final set of edges is the union of these two sets.

So the total sum is the sum of c_i*c_j for all pairs (i,j) with i < j that are in either the cycle or have a capital connection.

But this would be very complex to compute directly.

Alternative approach: the total sum is:
Sum over all cycle edges + sum over all capital edges that are not already in the cycle.

But we need to avoid double counting.

Actually, let's think differently. For each capital city x, it connects directly to every other city y ≠ x. 
So for each capital x, the sum of prices for roads from x to other cities is: sum_{y≠x} c_x * c_y = c_x * (sum_{y≠x} c_y)

The total sum from all capital cities would be: sum_{x in capitals} c_x * (sum_{y≠x} c_y)

However, this counts all pairs (x,y) where x is capital and y≠x.

But some of these pairs might already be covered by the cycle edges.

For example, if y is adjacent to x in the cycle, then the edge (x,y) is already present.

So we need to subtract the sum of prices for the edges that are both in the cycle and in the capital connections.

The edges that are both in cycle and in capital connections are exactly the edges between a capital city and its neighbors in the cycle.

For a capital city x, it has two neighbors in the cycle: (x-1,x) and (x,x+1), with wraparound. The edges (x-1,x) and (x,x+1) are already present in the cycle.

So if we compute the sum of all capital connections and add the cycle connections, we would be double-counting the edges between a capital and its neighbors.

But actually, in the capital connections, we're adding direct roads to all cities, and in the cycle we have direct roads to neighbors. Since the problem says "at most one road", we should only count the edge once.

Therefore, the total sum is:
- Sum of all cycle edge prices
- Plus sum of all capital connection prices to non-adjacent cities

For a capital city x:
- Total connections: sum of c_x * c_y for all y ≠ x
- Minus the connections to adjacent cities in the cycle
- The adjacent cities: (x-1), (x+1) with wraparound

So for each capital x, the contribution to the total sum from its non-cycle connections is: c_x * (sum of c_y for all y ≠ x and y not adjacent to x)

Actually, let me reconsider the problem statement. It says "for every pair of cities a and b (a < b), such that there is a road between a and b you are to find sum of products ca·cb."

So we need to count each road exactly once.

The roads exist if:
1. (i, i+1) for i=1 to n-1, and (n,1) - the cycle edges
2. (x,y) for each capital x and each y ≠ x - the capital edges

But with the constraint that there's at most one road between any two cities.

So the total sum is the sum of c_i*c_j for all pairs (i,j) with i < j that are connected either by cycle or by capital connection.

To compute this, I can:
1. Add the sum of all cycle edge prices
2. Add the sum of all capital edge prices for pairs that are not consecutive (i.e., not adjacent in the cycle)

For a capital city x, it connects to every city y ≠ x. The connections to neighbors (x-1 and x+1) are already covered by the cycle. So we only need to add the connections to non-adjacent cities.

So for each capital city x, the contribution is: c_x * (sum of c_y for y ≠ x and y not adjacent to x)

The sum of c_y for y ≠ x and y not adjacent to x = (total sum of all c_y) - c_x - (c_{x-1} + c_{x+1}) where indices are modulo n.

To avoid double-counting, we can ensure that for a pair (x,y), we only count it when x < y.

So the algorithm:
1. Calculate sum of all cycle edge prices
2. For each capital city x:
   a. For each city y such that y > x and y is not adjacent to x, add c_x * c_y
   b. For each city y such that y < x and y is not adjacent to x, add c_x * c_y (but this would be counted when processing y if y<x and y is capital)

Wait, if x is capital and y is also capital and y < x, then when we process capital y, we will also add the edge (y,x) when y < x and x not adjacent to y.

So to avoid double-counting, we should only count the edge (min(x,y), max(x,y)) once.

Since we want each pair (a,b) with a < b counted once, we can do:

For each capital city x, for each city y such that y > x and y is not adjacent to x, add c_x * c_y.

But this will miss edges where y < x.

The only way is to count every pair (x,y) where x is capital, y is not adjacent to x, and x < y, and add c_x * c_y.

For the edge between a capital and a non-capital city with x < y, and not adjacent, we count it once.

For the edge between two capitals with x < y and not adjacent, we count it once.

In the second example:
- Capital cities: 1,4
- Edges to count:
  - (1,3): 1<3, not adjacent to 1 → count, value = 3*2 = 6
  - (1,4): 1<4, not adjacent to 1 → count, value = 3*2 = 6
  - (2,4): 2<4, not adjacent to 4 → count, value = 5*2 = 10
- Total capital edge sum = 6+6+10 = 22
- Cycle edge sum = 49
- Total = 49 + 22 = 71 ✓

Perfect! This matches.

So the algorithm is:
1. Read input
2. Calculate sum of all cycle edges: 
   - (1,2) + (2,3) + ... + (n-1,n) + (n,1)
3. Calculate sum of all capital edge prices for pairs (x,y) where:
   - x is a capital city
   - y is a city such that y > x and y is not adjacent to x (in the cycle)
   - Add c_x * c_y for each such pair
4. Return total sum

For adjacent in the cycle: for a city x, its neighbors are (x-1) and (x+1) with wraparound.

In programming terms, for city i (0-indexed), neighbors are (i-1) % n and (i+1) % n.

For a capital city x (0-indexed), it is adjacent to (x-1) % n and (x+1) % n.

For city x, we want to consider all cities y where y > x and y is not (x-1)%n or (x+1)%n.

But we need to be careful with indexing.

Let me implement:

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Convert to 0-indexed
capitals = [x-1 for x in capitals]

# Calculate cycle edge sum
cycle_sum = 0
for i in range(n-1):
    cycle_sum += c[i] * c[i+1]
cycle_sum += c[n-1] * c[0]  # (n,1)

# Calculate capital edge sum
capital_sum = 0
for x in capitals:
    # For each capital city x, consider cities y such that y > x and y is not adjacent to x
    for y in range(x+1, n):
        # Check if y is adjacent to x in the cycle
        # x is adjacent to (x-1) % n and (x+1) % n
        neighbors = [(x-1) % n, (x+1) % n]
        if y not in neighbors:
            capital_sum += c[x] * c[y]

total = cycle_sum + capital_sum
print(total)
```

Let me search for a different insight.

Perhaps the capital roads are in addition to the cycle, and the total sum is:
- Sum of cycle edges
- Plus sum over each capital city x of (c_x * (sum of c_y for all y not adjacent to x))

And then we don't subtract anything because the problem says "at most one road", but in the sum, if a road is both in cycle and in capital, we only have one road, so we should not double-count.

In the first example:
- Cycle sum = 15
- For capital city 3 (index 2): adjacent to 1 (index 1) and 3 (index 3)
- cities not adjacent: 0 (index 0)
- sum of c_y for y not adjacent to x: c[0] = 2
- contribution: c[2] * 2 = 1 * 2 = 2
- total = 15 + 2 = 17 ✓

In example 2:
- Cycle sum = 49
- For capital city 1 (index 0): adjacent to 4 (index 4) and 1 (index 1) -> (0-1)%5 = 4, (0+1)%5 = 1
- not adjacent: cities 2,3,4 in indices: 2,3,4
- sum of c_y: c[2]+c[3]+c[4] = 2+2+4 = 8

- For capital city 4 (index 3): adjacent to 2 (index 2) and 4 (index 4) -> (3-1)%5 = 2, (3+1)%5 = 4

Still not right.

I see the issue: in this approach, for capital city 0, we are adding edges to cities 2,3,4, which includes (0,2), (0,3), (0,4)
For capital city 3, we are adding edges to cities 0,1, which includes (3,0) = (0,3)

So edge (0,3) is counted twice.

So we need to avoid double-counting.

The only way is to count each edge only once.

After some thought, I think the intended solution is:

Total sum = sum of cycle edges + sum over each capital city x of ( c_x * (sum of c_y for all y not adjacent to x) ) - sum over each pair of capitals (x,y) with x < y and not adjacent in cycle of c_x * c_y

Because when we do the sum over each capital city of (c_x * sum of c_y for not adjacent), we are counting each edge between a capital and a non-adjacent city, and for edges between two capitals that are not adjacent, they are counted twice (once for each capital), so we need to subtract once.

In example 1:
- cycle sum = 15
- for capital x=2: adjacent to 1,3 -> not adjacent: only 0
- sum of c_y for not adjacent: c[0] = 2
- contribution: 1 * 2 = 2
- no pair of capitals (only one capital)
- total = 15 + 2 = 17 ✓

In example 2:
- cycle sum = 49
- for capital x=0: adjacent to 4,1 -> not adjacent: 2,3,4
- sum of c_y: c[2]+c[3]+c[4] = 2+2+4 = 8
- contribution: 3 * 8 = 24
- for capital x=3: adjacent to 2,4 -> not adjacent: 0,1
- sum of c_y: c[0]+c[1] = 3+5 = 8
- contribution: 2 * 8 = 16

I think I have a fundamental mistake.

Perhaps the capital roads are only for direct connections, and they are added as is, and the cycle roads are only for consecutive pairs, and there is no double-counting in the problem, but the problem says "at most one road", so if a road exists in both, we only have one.

In the sum, we should not double-count.

After reading the problem again: "for every pair of cities a and b (a < b), such that there is a road between a and b"

So we sum over distinct pairs.

The roads are:
- All consecutive pairs in the cycle
- All pairs (x,y) where x is capital and y != x, but only if this pair is not already in the cycle

In other words, the total set of roads is the union.

So the sum is:
- sum of cycle edges
- plus sum of (c_x * c_y) for every pair (x,y) where x is capital, y != x, and (x,y) is not a consecutive pair in the cycle

And since a < b, we only count each such pair once.

For example 1:
- cycle edges: (0,1)=6, (1,2)=3, (2,3)=2, (3,0)=4 → sum = 15
- pairs (x,y) with x capital, y!=x, and not consecutive: 
  - capital x=2 (index 2), y=0: (0,2) is not consecutive? consecutive are (0,1), (1,2), (2,3), (3,0) — (0,2) is not consecutive, so add c[2]*c[0] = 1*2 = 2
  - (2,1): (1,2) is consecutive, so skip
  - (2,3): (2,3) is consecutive, so skip
- so only (0,2) with value 2
- total = 15 + 2 = 17 ✓

For example 2:
- cycle edges: 49
- capital cities: 0 and 3 (0-indexed)
- for x=0 (capital):
  - y=1: (0,1) is consecutive, skip
  - y=2: (0,2) is not consecutive, add c[0]*c[2] = 3*2 = 6

  - y=0: (3,0) is consecutive (since (3,4) and (4,0)), so skip
  - y=1: (3,1) is not consecutive? (3,4), (4,0), (0,1), (1,2), (2,3) — (3,1) is not in the list, so add c[3]*c[1] = 2*5 = 10

Perfect! So the algorithm is:

1. Calculate sum of cycle edge prices
2. For each capital city x (0-indexed), for each city y (0-indexed) such that y != x and (x,y) is not a consecutive pair in the cycle, and we want to add c_x * c_y, but only once for each pair (min(x,y), max(x,y))

In the loop, when we add c_x * c_y, we are adding for a directed edge, but since the pair (x,y) and (y,x) are the same pair with a < b, and we are iterating over each capital, we might add the same pair twice if both x and y are capitals.

In example 2, for x=0, y=3: we add c0*c3
 for x=3, y=0: we would add c3*c0, which is the same.

So to avoid double-counting, we should only consider pairs where x < y.

For each capital city x, for each city y such that y > x and (x,y) is not a consecutive pair, add c_x * c_y.

In example 2:
- x=0: y > 0 and not adjacent to 0: y=2,3
  - (0,2): not adjacent to 0? 0 adjacent to 1,4. (0,2) is not consecutive, yes. add c[0]*c[2] = 3*2 = 6
  - (0,3): not adjacent, add c[0]*c[3] = 3*2 = 6
- x=3: y > 3: y=4, but (3,4) is consecutive, skip
- so only (0,2) and (0,3) = 6+6 = 12, not 22

We only consider y > x.

For the pair (3,1) with 1<3, when x=3, y=1<3, so not considered.

We need to consider only pairs with x < y.

In (3,1), min=1, max=3, so if we consider when x=1, but 1 is not a capital.

So only when x is capital and x < y.

In this case, for a pair (x,y) with x < y, and x is capital, and (x,y) is not consecutive, we add c_x * c_y.

In example 2, the pairs are:
- (0,2): x=0<2, not consecutive, add
- (0,3): x=0<3, not consecutive, add
- (0,1): x=0<1, but (0,1) is consecutive, skip
- (1,3): x=1 is not capital, skip
- (2,3): consecutive, skip
- (3,4): consecutive, skip
- (1,4): not consecutive? (1,4) is not in consecutive, but x=1 not capital
- (2,4): not consecutive, but x=2 not capital

So only (0,2) and (0,3) = 6+6 = 12, not 22.

We are missing (3,1) with 1<3, and x=3 capital, but in the pair (1,3) with 1<3, and x=3 is capital, so we should consider when x=3 and y=1 with 1<3, but then x> y in the condition x< y.

To have a pair (a,b) with a < b and a capital, we can have either a or b as the capital, but only if the capital is the one with the larger index or smaller.

Since the edge (x,y) exists because x is capital and y is not adjacent, and we want to count it once with a < b, we can do it when the capital is the one with the larger index in the pair, or when it is the one with the smaller index.

But if the capital is the one with the larger index, then when we consider x and y with x > y, we would have to ensure that x is capital and x > y.

So perhaps we should iterate over each capital city x, and for each city y such that y < x and (x,y) is not consecutive, add c_x * c_y, and also for y > x and not consecutive, add c_x * c_y.

But then for a pair between two capitals, if x and y are both capitals and x > y, then when we consider x and y with y < x, we add it once.

In example 2 with (0,3): when x=0, y=3>0, so we would add it when x=0, y=3>0.
 when x=3, y=0<3, so we add it when x=3, y=0<3.
 so twice.

So still double-counting.

The only way is to only count when x < y and x is capital, or when x > y and x is capital, but only if we ensure that for each pair, it is only counted once.

Since the edge exists because x is capital and y is not adjacent, and for a pair (a,b) with a < b, if a is capital and (a,b) is not consecutive, then we should count it.
 if b is capital and (a,b) is not consecutive, then we should count it.

So for each pair (a,b) with a < b, if either a is capital or b is capital, and (a,b) is not a consecutive pair, then add c_a * c_b.

In example 2:
- (0,1): a=0 capital, but (0,1) is consecutive, skip
- (0,2): a=0 capital, not consecutive, add 3*2=6
- (0,3): a=0 capital, not consecutive, add 3*2=6
- (0,4): a=0 capital, (0,4) is consecutive, skip

- (2,3): a=2 not capital, b=3 capital, (2,3) is consecutive, skip
- (2,4): a=2 not capital, b=4 not capital, skip
- (3,4): a=3 capital, (3,4) is consecutive, skip
- also (0,3) is already counted

Pairs: (0,2), (0,3), (1,3) = 6+6+10 = 22
 total = 49 + 22 = 71 ✓

Yes! This is the correct approach.

So the algorithm is:

1. Calculate sum of cycle edge prices
2. For each pair (a,b) with a < b:
   - if a is capital or b is capital, and (a,b) is not a consecutive pair in the cycle, then add c_a * c_b
3. Return total

But n up to 100,000, so we can't iterate over all pairs (a,b) with a < b (O(n^2)).

We need to optimize.

We can group by capital cities.

For each capital city x, we can consider the cities it connects to that are not adjacent and with y > x or y < x, but only once for each pair.

We can do: for each capital city x, for each city y such that y is not adjacent to x and y > x, add c_x * c_y
 and for each city y such that y < x and y is not adjacent to x, add c_x * c_y

But then for a pair between two capitals, it will be counted twice.

So we need to subtract the double-counted pairs.

The double-counted pairs are (x,y) where x and y are both capitals, x < y, and (x,y) is not consecutive in the cycle.

For such a pair, it will be counted once when we process x and y> x, and once when we process y and y> x is not, when we process y and y > x, we have y > x, so we would add when processing y and y > x would not include x.

When we process capital x with y > x, we add c_x * c_y
 when we process capital y with x < y, we add c_y * c_x = c_x * c_y (since multiplication is commutative)

So each such pair is counted twice.

Therefore, if we do:
- For each capital city x, for each city y not adjacent to x and y != x, add c_x * c_y
 then we have double-counted the pairs between two capitals that are not adjacent.

So we can do:
- First, compute the sum over all capital cities x and for all y not adjacent to x, of c_x * c_y
- Then subtract the sum over all pairs of capitals (x,y) with x < y and (x,y) is not consecutive in the cycle of c_x * c_y

In example 1:
- only one capital, so no pair of capitals
- sum = for x=2, y=0: c[2]*c[0] = 1*2 = 2
- total = 15 + 2 = 17 ✓

In example 2:
- First, for each capital x, sum over y not adjacent to x of c_x * c_y
- x=0: not adjacent to: 2,3 ( indices 2,3)
  - c[0]*c[2] = 3*2 = 6
  - c[0]*c[3] = 3*2 = 6
  - sum = 12
- x=3: not adjacent to: 0,1 ( indices 0,1)
  - c[3]*c[0] = 2*3 = 6
  - c[3]*c[1] = 2*5 = 10
  - sum = 16
- total = 12 + 16 = 28
- pairs of capitals with x< y and not consecutive: only (0,3) with 0<3, and (0,3) is not consecutive, so subtract c[0]*c[3] = 3*2 = 6
- so capital edge sum = 28 - 6 = 22
- total = 49 + 22 = 71 ✓

Perfect! 

So the final algorithm is:

1. Read n, k, c, capitals
2. Convert capitals to 0-indexed
3. Calculate cycle_sum = sum of c[i] * c[(i+1)%n] for i in range(n)
4. capital_edge_sum = 0
   for each capital city x in capitals:
      for each city y from 0 to n-1:
         if y == x: skip
         if y is adjacent to x (i.e., (y-1)%n == x or (y+1)%n == x): skip
         else: capital_edge_sum += c[x] * c[y]
5. pairs_between_capitals = 0
   for each pair of capitals (x,y) with x < y:
      if (x,y) is not a consecutive pair in the cycle (i.e., (x+1)%n != y and (y+1)%n != x and (x-1)%n != y and (y-1)%n != x):  
         then pairs_between_capitals += c[x] * c[y]
6. capital_edge_sum -= pairs_between_capitals
7. total = cycle_sum + capital_edge_sum
8. print(total)

But step 4 is O(n*k) and k can be up to n, so O(n^2), and n up to 100,000, so worst-case 10^10, which is too slow.

We need to optimize.

In step 4, we can avoid the inner loop by precomputing the adjacent cities.

For a capital city x, the cities not adjacent to it are: all cities y such that y is not (x-1)%n and not (x+1)%n.

So the sum over y not adjacent to x of c_y is: (total_sum of all c) - c[(x-1)%n] - c[(x+1)%n]

Then the sum over y not adjacent to x of c_x * c_y = c_x * ( total_sum - c[(x-1)%n] - c[(x+1)%n] )

So for each capital x, add c_x * ( total_sum - c[(x-1)%n] - c[(x+1)%n] )

In example 2: total_sum = 3+5+2+2+4 = 16
- x=0: adjacent to 4 and 1, c[4]=4, c[1]=5, so sum of c_y for not adjacent = 16 - 4 - 5 = 7, then c[0] * 7 = 3 * 7 = 21, but earlier we had 12, so mistake.

 not adjacent cities for x=0: should be 2,3, because 1 and 4 are adjacent.
 c[2]+c[3] = 2+2 = 4, not 7.

 total_sum = 16, minus c[4] and c[1] = 4+5 = 9, 16-9=7, but should be 4.

 total_sum - c[4] - c[1] = 16 - 4 - 5 = 7, not 4.

 For x=0: (x-1)%n = 4, (x+1)%n = 1, so we subtract c[4] and c[1] = 4+5=9, 16-9=7, but should be 4.

 unless the total_sum is only for not including x, but we are including everything.

 The sum of c_y for y not adjacent to x is: sum_{y not in { (x-1)%n, (x+1)%n }} c_y

 This is: (sum of c_y for all y) - c[(x-1)%n] - c[(x+1)%n]

 because we are removing only these two cities.

 the set of cities we are removing is {(x-1)%n, (x+1)%n}, which might include x or not.

 for x=0: (x-1)%n = 4, (x+1)%n = 1, both are different from 0, so we remove them.

 the sum of c_y for all y is for all cities, including x.

 we want sum for y not in { (x-1)%n, (x+1)%n } and y != x.

 so we should do: (sum of all c_y) - c[(x-1)%n] - c[(x+1)%n] - c[x] + c[x] if x is in the set, but it's not.

 since only (x-1)%n and (x+1)%n are removed, and x is not in that set, then the sum for not adjacent to x is: (sum of all c) - c[(x-1)%n] - c[(x+1)%n]

 but in example, this is 16 - 4 - 5 = 7, and the actual is 4, so not matching.

 unless the total_sum is only for the other cities.

 let total_sum_without_x = (total_sum of all c) - c[x]

 then the contribution is c[x] * that sum.

 in example 2 for x=0: (16 - 3 - 4 - 5) = 16-12=4, then 3*4 = 12

 perfect.

 so algorithm:

 total_sum = sum(c)

 cycle_sum = 0
 for i in range(n):
   cycle_sum += c[i] * c[(i+1) % n]

 capital_edge_sum = 0
 for x in capitals:
   adjacent1 = (x-1) % n
   adjacent2 = (x+1) % n
   sum_c_not_adjacent = total_sum - c[x] - c[adjacent1] - c[adjacent2]
   capital_edge_sum += c[x] * sum_c_not_adjacent

 # now subtract the pairs between two capitals that are not adjacent in the cycle and with min < max
 pairs_between_capitals = 0
 for i in range(len(capitals)):
   for j in range(i+1, len(capitals)):
     x = capitals[i]
     y = capitals[j]
     # check if (x,y) is not a consecutive pair in the cycle
     # (x,y) is consecutive if (x+1)%n == y or (y+1)%n == x or (x-1)%n == y or (y-1)%n == x
     # since it's a cycle, they are consecutive if either (x+1)%n == y or (y+1)%n == x
     if (x+1) % n == y or (y+1) % n == x:
         continue
     else:
         pairs_between_capitals += c[x] * c[y]

 capital_edge_sum -= pairs_between_capitals

 total = cycle_sum + capital_edge_sum
 print(total)

 The complexity: O(k^2) for the last loop, and k can be up to n, so worst-case O(n^2) = 10^10, which is too slow for n=100,000.

 We need to optimize the last loop.

 We need to compute: for each pair of capitals (x,y) with x < y, if (x,y) is not consecutive, then add c[x]*c[y]

 This is equivalent to: (sum of c[x] * c[y] for all pairs of capitals with x < y) - (sum of c[x] * c[y] for pairs of capitals that are consecutive)

 Let S = sum of c[x] * c[y] for all pairs of capitals with x < y

 This can be computed as: (sum_{x in capitals} c[x])^2 - sum_{x in capitals} c[x]^2) / 2

 then subtract the sum of c[x]*c[y] for consecutive pairs of capitals.

 In example 2: capitals = [0,3]
 S = (3*2) = 6
 consecutive pairs: (0,3) is not consecutive, so no subtraction, so pairs_between_capitals = 0 for consecutive, so only if not consecutive, then in this case (0,3) is not consecutive, so we add 3*2=6.

 in the example, (0,3) with 0<3, and (0+1)%5 =1 !=3, (3+1)%5=4 !=0, so not consecutive, so we add 6.

 so pairs_between_capitals = 6

 in example 1: only one capital, so no pairs, so 0.

 so we can compute:

 total_capitals_sum = sum of c[x] for x in capitals
 sum_of_squares = sum of c[x]^2 for x in capitals
 S = (total_capitals_sum * total_capitals_sum - sum_of_squares) // 2

 then for each consecutive pair of capitals in the list, if (x,y) is consecutive in the cycle, then we subtract c[x] * c[y]

 note: the consecutive in the cycle might be in any order, and we only need to consider each pair once.

 in the list of capitals, if two capitals are consecutive in the cycle, we subtract their c product.

 so we need to find all pairs of capitals that are adjacent in the cycle.

 we can do: for each capital x in capitals, check if (x+1)%n is in capitals or (x-1)%n is in capitals, and if so, then the pair (min(x, that_city), max(x, that_city)) is a consecutive pair.

 but we only want to subtract once per pair.

 so we can create a set of capitals, then for each capital x, if (x+1)%n in capitals_set and (x+1)%n > x, then include the pair (x, (x+1)%n) with value c[x]*c[(x+1)%n]
 similarly if (x+1)%n in capitals_set and (x+1)%n < x, then the pair (x, (x+1)%n) with x> (x+1)%n, so when we do min and max, it's the same.

 so we can iterate over each capital x, and if (x+1)%n in capitals_set, then let y = (x+1)%n, and if x < y, then we have a pair (x,y) that is consecutive, and we subtract c[x]*c[y]
 if x > y, then we will be processing when x=y when we do y, so to avoid double-counting, we only consider when x < y.

 similarly for (x-1)%n.

 so for each capital x, if (x+1)%n in capitals_set and (x+1)%n > x, then subtract c[x] * c[(x+1)%n]
 if (x-1)%n in capitals_set and (x-1)%n > x, then subtract c[x] * c[(x-1)%n]

 but in the cycle, if x and y are consecutive, then either (x+1)%n = y or (y+1)%n = x, and we can have x < y or x > y.

 if x < y, then (x+1)%n = y or (y+1)%n = x (which implies (x-1)%n = y if we have (y+1)%n = x)

 so for each capital x, if (x+1)%n is in capitals_set, then the pair (x, (x+1)%n) is consecutive, and we should subtract c[x]*c[(x+1)%n] only if x < (x+1)%n
 similarly for (x-1)%n: if (x-1)%n in capitals_set and x > (x-1)%n, then the pair (x-1, x) with x-1 < x, so we should subtract when we process x-1 and (x-1+1)%n = x, and if x > (x-1), then when we process x-1, we will check (x-1+1)%n = x, and if x > x-1, then we subtract.

 so to avoid double-counting, we can for each capital x, if (x+1)%n in capitals_set and x < (x+1)%n, then subtract c[x]*c[(x+1)%n]
 if (x-1)%n in capitals_set and x > (x-1)%n, then when we process x-1, we will have (x-1+1)%n = x, and x-1 < x, so we will subtract c[x-1]*c[x] when processing x-1.

 so only when x < (x+1)%n, we subtract.

 in example 2: capitals = [0,3]
 for x=0: (0+1)%5 =1 not in capitals, (0-1)%5=4 not in capitals
 for x=3: (3+1)%5=4 not in capitals, (3-1)%5=2 not in capitals
 so no subtraction, but we have a pair (0,3) that is not consecutive.

 in the cycle, are 0 and 3 consecutive? 0 adjacent to 1,4; 3 adjacent to 2,4. so not consecutive.

 so for the pair (0,3), it is not a consecutive pair, so we should add it in the pairs_between_capitals.

 in the above, we only subtract for consecutive pairs, and we don't have any consecutive, so pairs_between_capitals = 0, but we need to add the non-consecutive pairs.

 in the example, we need to add c[0]*c[3] = 6 because (0,3) is not consecutive.

 so in the algorithm, we should not subtract if it is not consecutive; we want to add for non-consecutive pairs.

 in the capital edge sum, we have to add for every pair of capitals (x,y) with x < y and not consecutive.

 so we can compute: S = (sum of c[x] for x in capitals)^2 - sum of c[x]^2 for x in capitals) // 2
 then subtract the sum of c[x]*c[y] for pairs of capitals that are consecutive in the cycle.

 then the pairs_between_capitals = S - (sum of c[x]*c[y] for consecutive pairs)

 in example 2: S = (3+2)^2 - (9+4) // 2 = 25 - 13 // 2 = 12//2 = 6
 then subtract for consecutive pairs: none, so pairs_between_capitals = 6
 then capital_edge_sum = 28 - 6 = 22
 total = 49 + 22 = 71 ✓

 in example 1: only one capital, S = 0, pairs_between_capitals = 0, so capital_edge_sum = 2 - 0 = 2, total = 15+2=17 ✓

 so the algorithm is:

 total_sum = sum(c)
 cycle_sum = 0
 for i in range(n):
   cycle_sum += c[i] * c[(i+1) % n]

 total_capitals_sum = sum(c[x] for x in capitals)
 sum_of_squares = sum(c[x] * c[x] for x in capitals)
 S = (total_capitals_sum * total_capitals_sum - sum_of_squares) // 2

 consecutive_pairs_sum = 0
 capitals_set = set(capitals)
 for x in capitals:
   if (x+1) % n in capitals_set and x < (x+1) % n:
      consecutive_pairs_sum += c[x] * c[(x+1) % n]
   if (x-1) % n in capitals_set and x > (x-1) % n:
      consecutive_pairs_sum += c[x] * c[(x-1) % n]

 pairs_between_capitals = S - consecutive_pairs_sum

 capital_edge_sum = 0
 for x in capitals:
   adjacent1 = (x-1) % n
   adjacent2 = (x+1) % n
   sum_c_not_adjacent = total_sum - c[x] - c[adjacent1] - c[adjacent2]
   capital_edge_sum += c[x] * sum_c_not_adjacent

 capital_edge_sum -= pairs_between_capitals

 total = cycle_sum + capital_edge_sum
 print(total)

 Complexity: O(k) which is acceptable.

 Let me test with example 1 and 2.

 Example 1: n=4, c=[2,3,1,2], capitals=[2] (0-indexed)
 total_sum = 2+3+1+2 = 8
 cycle_sum = 2*3 + 3*1 + 1*2 + 2*2 = 6+3+2+4 = 15
 total_capitals_sum = 1
 sum_of_squares = 1
 S = (1*1 - 1)//2 = 0
 consecutive_pairs_sum = 0 (only one capital)
 capital_edge_sum = for x=2: adjacent1 = (1), adjacent2 = (3) (0-indexed)
 sum_c_not_adjacent = 8 - c[2] - c[1] - c[3] = 8 - 1 - 3 - 2 = 2
 capital_edge_sum = 1 * 2 = 2
 then capital_edge_sum -= 0 = 2
 total = 15+2 = 17 ✓

 Example 2: n=5, c=[3,5,2,2,4], capitals=[0,3] (0-indexed)
 total_sum = 3+5+2+2+4 = 16
 cycle_sum = 3*5 + 5*2 + 2*2 + 2*4 + 4*3 = 15+10+4+8+12 = 49
 total_capitals_sum = 3+2 = 5
 sum_of_squares = 9+4 = 13
 S = (25-13)//2 = 12//2 = 6
 consecutive_pairs_sum: for x=0: (0+1)%5=1 not in {0,3}, (0-1)%5=4 not in {0,3} -> 0
 for x=3: (3+1)%5=4 not in, (3-1)%5=2 not in -> 0
 so consecutive_pairs_sum = 0
 pairs_between_capitals = 6 - 0 = 6
 capital_edge_sum = for x=0: (0-1)%5=4, (0+1)%5=1, sum_c_not_adjacent = 16 - 3 - 5 - 4 = 4, then 3*4 = 12
 for x=3: (3-1)%5=2, (3+1)%5=4, sum_c_not_adjacent = 16 - 2 - 2 - 4 = 8, then 2*8 = 16
 capital_edge_sum = 12+16 = 28
 then capital_edge_sum -= 6 = 22
 total = 49+22 = 71 ✓

 Perfect.

 So final code:

Note: in the for loop for consecutive_pairs_sum, when we have (x+1)%n in capitals_set and x < (x+1)%n, we add c[x] * c[(x+1)%n]
 when we have (x-1)%n in capitals_set and x > (x-1)%n, we add c[x] * c[(x-1)%n]

 But is (x-1)%n in capitals_set and x > (x-1)%n the same as (x-1)%n and (x-1)%n+1=x being in the set and (x-1)%n < x? yes.

 and in the cycle, if x and y are consecutive, then either (x+1)%n = y or (y+1)%n = x, and we will capture it when x is the smaller in the pair.

 for example, if we have cities 0 and 1, then when x=0, (0+1)%5=1, and 0<1, so we add c[0]*c[1]
 when x=1, (1+1)%5=2 not in, (1-1)%5=0, and 1>0, so we would add when x=1, (1-1)%5=0 and 1>0, so we add c[1]*c[0] — double-count.

 so we must only do it for the smaller index.

 in our condition, when we have (x+1)%n in capitals_set and x < (x+1)%n, we add for the pair (x, (x+1)%n)
 when we have (x-1)%n in capitals_set and x > (x-1)%n, then (x-1)%n < x, and the pair is (x-1, x), and when we process x-1, we will have (x-1+1)%n = x, and if x > x-1, then we will add when processing x-1, and x-1 < x, so we will be in the condition for x-1: (x-1+1)%n = x in capitals_set and x-1 < x, so we add.

 therefore, to avoid double-counting, we should only do it when we have (x+1)%n in capitals_set and x < (x+1)%n, and similarly for (x-1)%n only when we have it and x > (x-1)%n, but this would capture the same pair twice.

 actually, if two cities are consecutive, then either they are (x, x+1) or (x, x-1), and we will capture it when processing the smaller one in the pair.

 specifically, if x < y and (x+1)%n = y, then when processing x, we have (x+1)%n = y in capitals_set and x < y, so we add.
 if x < y and (y+1)%n = x, then (y+1)%n = x, which means (x-1)%n = y, and when processing y, we have (y-1)%n = x, and y > x, so we have x < y and (y-1)%n = x, and if we do for (x-1)%n in capitals_set and x > (x-1)%n, when x > (x-1)%n, which is true, then we add when processing y.

 but in this case, when we process y, we have (y-1)%n = x, and y > x, so we only add if we have the condition on the smaller index.

 in the condition "if (x-1)%n in capitals_set and x > (x-1)%n", when x is y, then (x-1)%n = x-1, and we have x > (x-1), so we add.

 but the pair is (x-1, x) with x-1 < x, and we would add when processing x-1: if (x-1+1)%n = x in capitals_set and (x-1) < x, then we add.

 so we will capture it when processing the smaller index in the pair.

 therefore, we can only do the condition for (x+1)%n when x < (x+1)%n, and for (x-1)%n when x > (x-1)%n, but this will capture each pair only once (when processing the smaller index).

 in the example of (0,1): when x=0, (0+1)%5=1 in capitals_set and 0<1, so add.
 when x=1, (1-1)%5=0 in capitals_set and 1>0, so we would add, but we only want to add once.

 so to avoid double-counting, we should only do it for one of them.

 we should only do it when the pair is (x, x+1) with x < x+1, and we add when processing x.

 so we can simply do: for each capital x, if (x+1) % n in capitals_set and x < (x+1) % n, then add c[x] * c[(x+1) % n]

 and similarly, if (x-1) % n in capitals_set and x > (x-1) % n, then the pair is (x-1, x) with x-1 < x, and we should add when processing x-1, so we only need to do it when x > (x-1) % n, but then when we process x-1, we will have (x-1+1)%n = x in capitals_set and (x-1) < x, so we will add when processing x-1.

 therefore, if we do only the (x+1) condition, we might miss the (x-1) pairs.

 when we have a pair (x, x-1), then in the cycle, they are adjacent if (x-1+1)%n = x, which is true, and if (x-1) < x, then when we process x-1, we will have (x-1+1)%n = x in capitals_set and (x-1) < x, so we add.

 so if we only do the condition for (x+1)%n and x < (x+1)%n, then we will capture all pairs that are (x, x+1) with x < x+1.

 in a cycle, every edge is between consecutive, so if two capitals are adjacent, they are either (x, x+1) or (x, x-1), and when we do (x+1)%n, if x+1 exists and x < x+1, then we capture (x, x+1) if it exists.
 for (x, x-1), we have (x-1+1)%n = x, and if x-1 < x, then when we process x-1, we will have (x-1+1)%n = x in capitals_set and x-1 < x, so we capture.

 therefore, by doing only: for each capital x, if (x+1) % n in capitals_set and x < (x+1) % n, then add, we will capture every consecutive pair in the cycle between two capitals.

 and we do it only once per pair.

 so we can do only that.

 in the example of (0,3) in example 2: (0+1)%5=1 not in, (3+1)%5=4 not in, so not captured, correct.

 in a cycle with capitals at 0 and 1: when x=0, (0+1)%5=1 in, and 0<1, so add c[0]*c[1]

 in a cycle with capitals at 1 and 0: when x=1, (1+1)%5=2 not in, when x=0, (0+1)%5=1 in and 0<1, so add.

 so for any pair of consecutive capitals, we will catch it when we process the smaller index.

 Therefore, we can use:

 consecutive_pairs_sum = 0
 capitals_set = set(capitals)
 for x in capitals:
   if (x+1) % n in capitals_set and x < (x+1) % n:
      consecutive_pairs_sum += c[x] * c[(x+1) % n]

 then pairs_between_capitals = S - consecutive_pairs_sum

 and the final code is as above.

 Let me write the code accordingly. 

Note: in the cycle, the consecutive pair between x and y is if they are adjacent, which is if (x+1)%n == y or (y+1)%n == x, and we capture it when the smaller is processed with the condition on the next.

 in code: (x+1) % n in capitals_set and x < (x+1) % n

 This will only happen when the smaller index has the next city in capitals_set.

 and since in a cycle, if two cities are adjacent, then either (x+1)%n = y or (y+1)%n = x, and in the pair (min(x,y), max(x,y)), if (min+1)%n = max, then when we process min, we will have (min+1)%n = max in capitals_set and min < max, so we add.

 if (min+1)%n != max, then (max+1)%n = min, which implies (min-1)%n = max, and then when we process max, (max+1)%n = min, and if max < min, then we would have max < min, but min < max, so not true.

 in a cycle, if two cities are adjacent, then they are either (x, x+1) or (x, x-1), and (x, x-1) with x-1 < x, then when we process x-1, (x-1+1)%n = x, and x-1 < x, so we will add when processing x-1.

 so in both cases, when we process the smaller index, we will have the next city in the capitals_set.

 therefore, the condition covers all adjacent pairs.

 So the code is:

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Convert to 0-indexed
capitals = [x-1 for x in capitals]

# Calculate cycle edge sum
cycle_sum = 0
for i in range(n):
    cycle_sum += c[i] * c[(i+1) % n]

# Total sum of all beauty values
total_sum = sum(c)

# Total sum and sum of squares of capital cities
total_capitals_sum = sum(c[x] for x in capitals)
sum_of_squares = sum(c[x] * c[x] for x in capitals)
S = (total_capitals_sum * total_capitals_sum - sum_of_squares) // 2

# Sum of c[x]*c[y] for consecutive pairs of capitals with x < y
consecutive_pairs_sum = 0
capitals_set = set(capitals)
for x in capitals:
    next_city = (x + 1) % n
    if next_city in capitals_set and x < next_city:
        consecutive_pairs_sum += c[x] * c[next_city]

# The sum of c[x]*c[y] for pairs of capitals with x < y and not consecutive
pairs_between_capitals = S - consecutive_pairs_sum

# Calculate the sum of c[x] * (sum of c_y for y not adjacent to x and y != x)
capital_edge_sum = 0
for x in capitals:
    adjacent1 = (x - 1) % n
    adjacent2 = (x + 1) % n
    # Sum of c_y for all y except x and its adjacent cities
    sum_c_not_adjacent = total_sum - c[x] - c[adjacent1] - c[adjacent2]
    capital_edge_sum += c[x] * sum_c_not_adjacent

# Subtract the pairs between two capitals that are not adjacent (because they were counted twice in the capital_edge_sum if both are capitals and not adjacent)
# Actually, in the capital_edge_sum, we have for each capital x and y not adjacent to x, we add c_x * c_y
# For a pair (x,y) with both x and y capitals and not adjacent, it is counted twice: once in x and once in y
# But in the pairs_between_capitals, we have the sum for all non-consecutive pairs of capitals
# So we need to subtract the non-consecutive pairs of capitals (because they were counted twice in capital_edge_sum)
# In the capital_edge_sum, when we do for x, we add for every y not adjacent to x, including when y is a capital and not adjacent.
# When we do for y, we add for every x not adjacent to y, including when x is a capital and not adjacent.
# So for a pair of capitals (x,y) with not adjacent, it is counted twice in capital_edge_sum.
# Therefore, we need to subtract it once to have only once.
# The value to subtract is the sum of c[x]*c[y] for all pairs of capitals (x,y) with x < y and not consecutive in the cycle.
# This is exactly pairs_between_capitals.
# In our earlier calculation, we defined pairs_between_capitals = S - consecutive_pairs_sum = sum for all pairs of capitals with x<y and not consecutive
# So we subtract pairs_between_capitals to make it count only once.
capital_edge_sum -= pairs_between_capitals

total = cycle_sum + capital_edge_sum
print(total)
```

Note: in the example with two capitals and not adjacent, when we add for x: we add for y, and when we add for y: we add for x, so twice. Then we subtract once to make it once.

Yes.

I'll now write the final code. 
We are done. 
The complexity is O(k), which is acceptable for k<=n<=100,000.

Let me box it. 
Note: the input indices are 1-indexed, so after reading, we convert to 0-indexed by subtracting 1.

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Convert to 0-indexed
capitals = [x - 1 for x in capitals]

# Calculate cycle edge sum: (i, i+1) for i=0 to n-1, and (n-1,0)
cycle_sum = 0
for i in range(n):
    cycle_sum += c[i] * c[(i + 1) % n]

# Total sum of all beauty values
total_sum = sum(c)

# Sum of c[x] for x in capitals and sum of c[x]^2 for x in capitals
total_capitals_sum = sum(c[x] for x in capitals)
sum_of_squares = sum(c[x] * c[x] for x in capitals)
# The sum of c[x] * c[y] for all pairs of distinct capitals with x < y
S = (total_capitals_sum * total_capitals_sum - sum_of_squares) // 2

# Sum of c[x] * c[y] for consecutive pairs of capitals (x,y) with x < y
consecutive_pairs_sum = 0
capitals_set = set(capitals)
for x in capitals:
    next_city = (x + 1) % n
    if next_city in capitals_set and x < next_city:
        consecutive_pairs_sum += c[x] * c[next_city]

# The sum of c[x] * c[y] for pairs of capitals with x < y and not consecutive in the cycle
pairs_between_capitals = S - consecutive_pairs_sum

# Calculate the sum of c[x] * (sum of c_y for y not adjacent to x and y != x)
capital_edge_sum = 0
for x in capitals:
    adjacent1 = (x - 1) % n
    adjacent2 = (x + 1) % n
    # Sum of c_y for all y except x and its adjacent cities
    sum_c_not_adjacent = total_sum - c[x] - c[adjacent1] - c[adjacent2]
    capital_edge_sum += c[x] * sum_c_not_adjacent

# Subtract the pairs between two capitals that are not adjacent (because they were counted twice)
capital_edge_sum -= pairs_between_capitals

total = cycle_sum + capital_edge_sum
print(total)
``` 
This should be the solution. 
I have tested with the given examples and it works. 
The complexity is O(k) which is acceptable. 
We are done. 
Note: in the condition for consecutive_pairs_sum, we only consider when x < (x+1)%n, which ensures we count each consecutive pair only once. 
And in the capital_edge_sum, each pair (x,y) with both x and y capitals and not adjacent is counted twice (once in x and once in y), so we subtract it once to have only once. 
And for non-consecutive pairs, we have the correct value. 
And for edges between a capital and a non-capital city, they are only in the capital_edge_sum when the capital is x and the other city is not adjacent, and they are counted once. 
So it should be correct.

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Convert to 0-indexed
capitals = [x - 1 for x in capitals]

# Calculate cycle edge sum
cycle_sum = 0
for i in range(n):
    cycle_sum += c[i] * c[(i + 1) % n]

# Total sum of all beauty values
total_sum = sum(c)

# Sum of c[x] for x in capitals and sum of c[x]^2 for x in capitals
total_capitals_sum = sum(c[x] for x in capitals)
sum_of_squares = sum(c[x] * c[x] for x in capitals)
# The sum of c[x] * c[y] for all pairs of distinct capitals with x < y
S = (total_capitals_sum * total_capitals_sum - sum_of_squares) // 2

# Sum of c[x] * c[y] for consecutive pairs of capitals (x,y) with x < y
consecutive_pairs_sum = 0
capitals_set = set(capitals)
for x in capitals:
    next_city = (x + 1) % n
    if next_city in capitals_set and x < next_city:
        consecutive_pairs_sum += c[x] * c[next_city]

# The sum of c[x] * c[y] for pairs of capitals with x < y and not consecutive in the cycle
pairs_between_capitals = S - consecutive_pairs_sum

# Calculate the sum of c[x] * (sum of c_y for y not adjacent to x and y != x)
capital_edge_sum = 0
for x in capitals:
    adjacent1 = (x - 1) % n
    adjacent2 = (x + 1) % n
    # Sum of c_y for all y except x and its adjacent cities
``` 

Submitted Code

View Extracted Code 
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Convert to 0-indexed
capitals = [x - 1 for x in capitals]

# Calculate cycle edge sum
cycle_sum = 0
for i in range(n):
    cycle_sum += c[i] * c[(i + 1) % n]

# Total sum of all beauty values
total_sum = sum(c)

# Sum of c[x] for x in capitals and sum of c[x]^2 for x in capitals
total_capitals_sum = sum(c[x] for x in capitals)
sum_of_squares = sum(c[x] * c[x] for x in capitals)
# The sum of c[x] * c[y] for all pairs of distinct capitals with x < y
S = (total_capitals_sum * total_capitals_sum - sum_of_squares) // 2

# Sum of c[x] * c[y] for consecutive pairs of capitals (x,y) with x < y
consecutive_pairs_sum = 0
capitals_set = set(capitals)
for x in capitals:
    next_city = (x + 1) % n
    if next_city in capitals_set and x < next_city:
        consecutive_pairs_sum += c[x] * c[next_city]

# The sum of c[x] * c[y] for pairs of capitals with x < y and not consecutive in the cycle
pairs_between_capitals = S - consecutive_pairs_sum

# Calculate the sum of c[x] * (sum of c_y for y not adjacent to x and y != x)
capital_edge_sum = 0
for x in capitals:
    adjacent1 = (x - 1) % n
    adjacent2 = (x + 1) % n
    # Sum of c_y for all y except x and its adjacent cities
    sum_c_not_adjacent = total_sum - c[x] - c[adjacent1] - c[adjacent2]
    capital_edge_sum += c[x] * sum_c_not_adjacent

# Subtract the pairs between two capitals that are not adjacent (because they were counted twice)
capital_edge_sum -= pairs_between_capitals

total = cycle_sum + capital_edge_sum
print(total)

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Little Mishka is a great traveller and she visited many countries. After thinking about where to travel this time, she chose XXX — beautiful, but little-known northern country.

Here are some interesting facts about XXX:

  1. XXX consists of n cities, k of whose (just imagine!) are capital cities. 
  2. All of cities in the country are beautiful, but each is beautiful in its own way. Beauty value of i-th city equals to ci. 
  3. All the cities are consecutively connected by the roads, including 1-st and n-th city, forming a cyclic route 1 — 2 — ... — n — 1. Formally, for every 1 ≤ i < n there is a road between i-th and i + 1-th city, and another one between 1-st and n-th city. 
  4. Each capital city is connected with each other city directly by the roads. Formally, if city x is a capital city, then for every 1 ≤ i ≤ n, i ≠ x, there is a road between cities x and i. 
  5. There is at most one road between any two cities. 
  6. Price of passing a road directly depends on beauty values of cities it connects. Thus if there is a road between cities i and j, price of passing it equals ci·cj.



Mishka started to gather her things for a trip, but didn't still decide which route to follow and thus she asked you to help her determine summary price of passing each of the roads in XXX. Formally, for every pair of cities a and b (a < b), such that there is a road between a and b you are to find sum of products ca·cb. Will you help her?

Input

The first line of the input contains two integers n and k (3 ≤ n ≤ 100 000, 1 ≤ k ≤ n) — the number of cities in XXX and the number of capital cities among them.

The second line of the input contains n integers c1, c2, ..., cn (1 ≤ ci ≤ 10 000) — beauty values of the cities.

The third line of the input contains k distinct integers id1, id2, ..., idk (1 ≤ idi ≤ n) — indices of capital cities. Indices are given in ascending order.

Output

Print the only integer — summary price of passing each of the roads in XXX.

Examples

Input

4 1
2 3 1 2
3


Output

17

Input

5 2
3 5 2 2 4
1 4


Output

71
