So the algorithm:
1. Read inputs
2. Calculate cycle sum: sum of c[i]*c[i+1] for i=0 to n-2, plus c[n-1]*c[0]
3. Initialize direct_sum = 0
4. For each capital city x (0-indexed), find its neighbors in cycle: (x-1)%n and (x+1)%n
5. For each city i not equal to x and not adjacent to x, add c[x]*c[i] to direct_sum
6. Initialize capital_pair_sum = 0
7. For each pair of distinct capital cities (x,y), check if they are adjacent in the cycle (i.e., |x-y| = 1 or |x-y| = n-1)
8. If they are not adjacent, add c[x]*c[y] to capital_pair_sum
9. Answer = cycle_sum + direct_sum - capital_pair_sum

Wait, step 7: we need to check adjacency properly. In a cycle, cities i and j are adjacent if |i-j| = 1 or |i-j| = n-1 (when n>2).

But since the capitals are given in sorted order, we can check for each pair.

However, we need to be careful: two cities are adjacent in the cycle if one is the next (i+1) or previous (i-1) in the cycle.

So for two capital cities x and y (x > y), they are adjacent if:
- y == (x-1) or y == (x+1) or (x == 0 and y == n-1) or (x == n-1 and y == 0)

Actually, in a circular arrangement, two cities i and j are adjacent if min(|i-j|, n-|i-j|) == 1.

But more precisely, they are adjacent if j == (i+1)%n or j == (i-1)%n.

So for each pair of capitals, we check if they are adjacent.

But since the input is small (k ≤ n ≤ 100,000), and k could be large, we need to be careful about complexity.

We need a more efficient approach.

Alternative approach: instead of iterating over all pairs, we can determine which capital cities are adjacent to each other.

For each capital city x, we can check which capital cities are adjacent to it in the cycle.

A capital city y is adjacent to x if y == (x+1)%n or y == (x-1)%n.

So in the list of capitals, we need to check if (x+1)%n or (x-1)%n is also a capital.

So we can:
- Store the capital indices in a set for O(1) lookup
- For each capital city x, if (x+1)%n is a capital, then mark that the edge between x and (x+1)%n exists and is adjacent
- Similarly for (x-1)%n

But we only need to count the edge between two capitals that are not adjacent in the cycle.

1. total_capital_pairs_sum = sum of c[x]*c[y] for all x < y
2. adjacent_capital_sum = 0
3. For each capital x, check if (x+1)%n is a capital. If yes, add c[x]*c[(x+1)%n]
4. Similarly check if (x-1)%n is a capital. If yes, add c[x]*c[(x-1)%n]

Therefore, the sum of c[x]*c[y] for non-adjacent capital pairs = total_capital_pairs_sum - adjacent_capital_sum

But in our direct_sum calculation, we already included every capital-to-non-adjacent-city edge, including capital-to-capital edges that are not adjacent.

So we need to subtract it once.

Therefore, final answer = cycle_sum + direct_sum - (total_capital_pairs_sum - adjacent_capital_sum)

Wait no: we want to remove the double-counting of the capital-capital edges that are not adjacent.

Each such edge is counted twice in direct_sum, so we need to subtract it once (to make it counted once).

So we subtract (total_capital_pairs_sum - adjacent_capital_sum) from direct_sum.

Therefore, final answer = cycle_sum + (direct_sum - (total_capital_pairs_sum - adjacent_capital_sum))

Let me verify with example 2:
- cycle_sum = 49
- direct_sum = 28
- total_capital_pairs_sum: capitals at 0(3) and 3(2) → sum = 3*2 = 6
- adjacent_capital_sum: check for each capital
  - capital 0: (0+1)%5=1, is 1 a capital? No. (0-1)%5=4, is 4 a capital? No.
  - capital 3: (3+1)%5=4, is 4 a capital? No. (3-1)%5=2, is 2 a capital? No.
  - so adjacent_capital_sum = 0
- non-adjacent capital sum = 6 - 0 = 6
- final = 49 + (28 - 6) = 49 + 22 = 71 ✓

Perfect.

Now let me code it step by step.

Note: cities are 1-indexed in input, but we'll use 0-indexed in code.

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Convert to 0-indexed
capitals = [x - 1 for x in capitals]

# Calculate cycle sum: edges (i, i+1) for i=0 to n-2, and (n-1, 0)
cycle_sum = 0
for i in range(n-1):
    cycle_sum += c[i] * c[i+1]
cycle_sum += c[n-1] * c[0]

# Calculate direct_sum: sum of c[x] * c[i] for each capital x and each city i not adjacent to x
direct_sum = 0
capital_set = set(capitals)

for x in capitals:
    # Find adjacent cities in cycle
    left_neighbor = (x - 1) % n
    right_neighbor = (x + 1) % n
    
    # For each city i not equal to x and not adjacent to x, add c[x]*c[i]
    for i in range(n):
        if i != x and i != left_neighbor and i != right_neighbor:
            direct_sum += c[x] * c[i]

# Calculate total capital pairs sum (all pairs of distinct capitals)
total_capital_pairs_sum = 0
capitals_sorted = sorted(capitals)
for i in range(len(capitals)):
    for j in range(i+1, len(capitals)):
        total_capital_pairs_sum += c[capitals[i]] * c[capitals[j]]

# Calculate adjacent capital pairs sum (sum of c[x]*c[y] for adjacent capital pairs)
adjacent_capital_sum = 0
for x in capitals:
    left_neighbor = (x - 1) % n
    right_neighbor = (x + 1) % n
    if left_neighbor in capital_set:
        adjacent_capital_sum += c[x] * c[left_neighbor]
    if right_neighbor in capital_set:
        adjacent_capital_sum += c[x] * c[right_neighbor]

# Each adjacent capital pair is counted twice (once from each end), so divide by 2
adjacent_capital_sum //= 2

# The sum we need to subtract is the sum of c[x]*c[y] for non-adjacent capital pairs
# Because each such edge is double-counted in direct_sum
non_adjacent_capital_sum = total_capital_pairs_sum - adjacent_capital_sum

# Final answer
result = cycle_sum + (direct_sum - non_adjacent_capital_sum)
print(result)
```

Wait, let me verify the example 1 with this:

Example 1: n=4, k=1, c=[2,3,1,2], capitals=[3] → 0-indexed: [2]

cycle_sum = c[0]*c[1] + c[1]*c[2] + c[2]*c[3] + c[3]*c[0] = 2*3 + 3*1 + 1*2 + 2*2 = 6+3+2+4 = 15

direct_sum: for x=2 (city 3)
- neighbors: (2-1)%4=1, (2+1)%4=3
- i not in {2,1,3} → only i=0
- add c[2]*c[0] = 1*2 = 2
- direct_sum = 2

total_capital_pairs_sum: only one capital, so 0

adjacent_capital_sum: 0

non_adjacent_capital_sum = 0

result = 15 + (2 - 0) = 17 ✓

Perfect!

But the time complexity: O(n*k) for direct_sum, and O(k²) for total_capital_pairs_sum. In worst case k=n=100,000, k² = 10^10 which is too slow.

We need to optimize.

For direct_sum: we can avoid the inner loop. For each capital x, we need to sum c[x]*c[i] for all i not x, not (x-1)%n, not (x+1)%n.

Sum of c[i] over all i is total_sum.

Sum of c[i] for i = (x-1)%n and (x+1)%n is c[(x-1)%n] + c[(x+1)%n].

So sum of c[i] for non-adjacent cities = total_sum - c[x] - c[(x-1)%n] - c[(x+1)%n]

Then direct_sum += c[x] * (total_sum - c[x] - c[(x-1)%n] - c[(x+1)%n])

For total_capital_pairs_sum: we can use the formula:

Sum of c[i]*c[j] for i<j = (sum c[i])² / 2 - sum c[i]² / 2

Actually: (sum c[i])² = sum c[i]² + 2*sum_{i<j} c[i]*c[j]

So sum_{i<j} c[i]*c[j] = (sum c[i])² - sum c[i]²) / 2

Similarly, for adjacent capital pairs: we can calculate it in O(k) by iterating over capitals.

So optimized version:

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Convert to 0-indexed
capitals = [x - 1 for x in capitals]

# Calculate cycle sum
cycle_sum = 0
for i in range(n-1):
    cycle_sum += c[i] * c[i+1]
cycle_sum += c[n-1] * c[0]

# Calculate total_sum of all c[i]
total_sum = sum(c)

# Calculate direct_sum using optimized formula
direct_sum = 0
for x in capitals:
    left_neighbor = (x - 1) % n
    right_neighbor = (x + 1) % n
    # Sum of c[i] for all i not x, not left_neighbor, not right_neighbor
    sum_other = total_sum - c[x] - c[left_neighbor] - c[right_neighbor]
    direct_sum += c[x] * sum_other

# Calculate total capital pairs sum (all pairs of distinct capitals)
sum_capitals = sum(c[x] for x in capitals)
sum_capitals_squared = sum(c[x] * c[x] for x in capitals)
total_capital_pairs_sum = (sum_capitals * sum_capitals - sum_capitals_squared) // 2

# Calculate adjacent capital pairs sum
adjacent_capital_sum = 0
capital_set = set(capitals)
for x in capitals:
    left_neighbor = (x - 1) % n
    right_neighbor = (x + 1) % n
    if left_neighbor in capital_set:
        adjacent_capital_sum += c[x] * c[left_neighbor]
    if right_neighbor in capital_set:
        adjacent_capital_sum += c[x] * c[right_neighbor]
adjacent_capital_sum //= 2

# Subtract non-adjacent capital pairs sum from direct_sum
non_adjacent_capital_sum = total_capital_pairs_sum - adjacent_capital_sum
result = cycle_sum + (direct_sum - non_adjacent_capital_sum)
print(result)
```

Let me verify with the examples.

Example 1: 
- total_sum = 2+3+1+2 = 8
- capitals = [2] (0-indexed)
- direct_sum: x=2, left=(1), right=(3), sum_other = 8 - c[2] - c[1] - c[3] = 8 - 1 - 3 - 2 = 2
- direct_sum += c[2] * 2 = 1 * 2 = 2 ✓
- sum_capitals = c[2] = 1
- sum_capitals_squared = 1
- total_capital_pairs_sum = (1*1 - 1)/2 = 0
- adjacent_capital_sum = 0 (since 1 and 3 are not capitals)
- non_adjacent_capital_sum = 0
- result = 15 + (2 - 0) = 17 ✓

Example 2:
- n=5, c=[3,5,2,2,4], capitals=[0,3] (0-indexed)
- total_sum = 3+5+2+2+4 = 16
- cycle_sum = 3*5 + 5*2 + 2*2 + 2*4 + 4*3 = 15+10+4+8+12 = 49
- direct_sum:
  - x=0: left=4, right=1
    - sum_other = 16 - c[0] - c[4] - c[1] = 16 - 3 - 4 - 5 = 4
    - add c[0] * 4 = 3*4 = 12
  - x=3: left=2, right=4
    - sum_other = 16 - c[3] - c[2] - c[4] = 16 - 2 - 2 - 4 = 8
    - add c[3] * 8 = 2*8 = 16
  - direct_sum = 12 + 16 = 28 ✓
- sum_capitals = c[0] + c[3] = 3 + 2 = 5
- sum_capitals_squared = 9 + 4 = 13
- total_capital_pairs_sum = (5*5 - 13)/2 = (25-13)/2 = 6
- adjacent_capital_sum: 
  - x=0: left=4 (not a capital), right=1 (not a capital) → 0
  - x=3: left=2 (not a capital), right=4 (not a capital) → 0
  - adjacent_capital_sum = 0
- non_adjacent_capital_sum = 6 - 0 = 6
- result = 49 + (28 - 6) = 71 ✓

Perfect!

Final code:

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Convert to 0-indexed
capitals = [x - 1 for x in capitals]

# Calculate cycle sum
cycle_sum = 0
for i in range(n-1):
    cycle_sum += c[i] * c[i+1]
cycle_sum += c[n-1] * c[0]

# Calculate total sum of all beauty values
total_sum = sum(c)

# Calculate direct_sum: sum of c[x] * c[i] for capital x and non-adjacent cities i
direct_sum = 0
for x in capitals:
    left_neighbor = (x - 1) % n
    right_neighbor = (x + 1) % n
    # Sum of c[i] for i not x, not left_neighbor, not right_neighbor
    sum_other = total_sum - c[x] - c[left_neighbor] - c[right_neighbor]
    direct_sum += c[x] * sum_other

# Calculate sum of products for all pairs of distinct capitals
sum_capitals = sum(c[x] for x in capitals)
sum_capitals_squared = sum(c[x] * c[x] for x in capitals)
total_capital_pairs_sum = (sum_capitals * sum_capitals - sum_capitals_squared) // 2

# Calculate sum of products for adjacent capital pairs
adjacent_capital_sum = 0
capital_set = set(capitals)
for x in capitals:
    left_neighbor = (x - 1) % n
    right_neighbor = (x + 1) % n
    if left_neighbor in capital_set:
        adjacent_capital_sum += c[x] * c[left_neighbor]
    if right_neighbor in capital_set:
        adjacent_capital_sum += c[x] * c[right_neighbor]
# Each adjacent pair is counted twice (once from each end)
adjacent_capital_sum //= 2

# The non-adjacent capital pairs sum is what we need to subtract from direct_sum
non_adjacent_capital_sum = total_capital_pairs_sum - adjacent_capital_sum
result = cycle_sum + (direct_sum - non_adjacent_capital_sum)
print(result)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Little Mishka is a great traveller and she visited many countries. After thinking about where to travel this time, she chose XXX — beautiful, but little-known northern country.

Here are some interesting facts about XXX:

  1. XXX consists of n cities, k of whose (just imagine!) are capital cities. 
  2. All of cities in the country are beautiful, but each is beautiful in its own way. Beauty value of i-th city equals to ci. 
  3. All the cities are consecutively connected by the roads, including 1-st and n-th city, forming a cyclic route 1 — 2 — ... — n — 1. Formally, for every 1 ≤ i < n there is a road between i-th and i + 1-th city, and another one between 1-st and n-th city. 
  4. Each capital city is connected with each other city directly by the roads. Formally, if city x is a capital city, then for every 1 ≤ i ≤ n, i ≠ x, there is a road between cities x and i. 
  5. There is at most one road between any two cities. 
  6. Price of passing a road directly depends on beauty values of cities it connects. Thus if there is a road between cities i and j, price of passing it equals ci·cj.



Mishka started to gather her things for a trip, but didn't still decide which route to follow and thus she asked you to help her determine summary price of passing each of the roads in XXX. Formally, for every pair of cities a and b (a < b), such that there is a road between a and b you are to find sum of products ca·cb. Will you help her?

Input

The first line of the input contains two integers n and k (3 ≤ n ≤ 100 000, 1 ≤ k ≤ n) — the number of cities in XXX and the number of capital cities among them.

The second line of the input contains n integers c1, c2, ..., cn (1 ≤ ci ≤ 10 000) — beauty values of the cities.

The third line of the input contains k distinct integers id1, id2, ..., idk (1 ≤ idi ≤ n) — indices of capital cities. Indices are given in ascending order.

Output

Print the only integer — summary price of passing each of the roads in XXX.

Examples

Input

4 1
2 3 1 2
3


Output

17

Input

5 2
3 5 2 2 4
1 4


Output

71

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` cities arranged in a cycle: 1-2-...-n-1
2. There are `k` capital cities
3. Each capital city is directly connected to every other city (including non-capitals)
4. The road between cities i and j has price = c_i × c_j
5. We need to find the sum of prices for all roads

## Key Insights

1. **Basic cycle connections**: Cities form a cycle, so we have roads: (1,2), (2,3), ..., (n-1,n), (n,1)
2. **Capital connections**: Each capital city is connected to every other city
3. **No duplicate roads**: The problem states at most one road between any two cities

## Approach

The total sum consists of two parts:

**Part 1: Cycle connections**
- Roads: (1,2), (2,3), ..., (n-1,n), (n,1)
- Sum = c₁c₂ + c₂c₃ + ... + cₙc₁

**Part 2: Capital connections**
- Each capital city is connected to every other city
- But we need to be careful not to double-count roads between capitals

Let me think about this more carefully:

For any two cities i and j (i < j):
- If they are adjacent in the cycle (|i-j| = 1 or |i-j| = n-1), they have a road with price c_i × c_j
- If one is a capital and the other is not, they have a road with price c_i × c_j
- If both are capitals, they have a road with price c_i × c_j

However, the capital connections include ALL pairs between a capital and any other city, including adjacent pairs in the cycle.

So we can calculate:
1. Sum of cycle edges: ∑(c_i × c_{i+1}) for i=1 to n-1, plus c_n × c_1
2. Sum of capital-to-all-other-city connections

But wait - the cycle edges are already part of the capital connections if the adjacent cities include capitals. So we need to be careful about double counting.

Actually, let's reread the problem: "each capital city is connected with each other city directly by the roads". This means:
- Every capital has direct connections to every city (including adjacent cities in the cycle)
- The cycle connections are also present

So the complete graph includes:
- All cycle edges
- All edges from capitals to all other cities

But since the cycle edges might also be between a capital and another city, we don't need to add them twice.

The key insight is that we need to sum up all unique roads.

Actually, the problem says "there is at most one road between any two cities", so we need to sum the product for each unique pair that has a road.

So the total sum = sum of products for all edges that exist.

The edges that exist are:
1. All adjacent pairs in the cycle: (1,2), (2,3), ..., (n-1,n), (n,1)
2. All pairs where one city is a capital and the other is not a capital

Wait, this is not right. The problem says "each capital city is connected with each other city directly". This means every capital is directly connected to every city.

But the cycle connections are also there.

So the complete set of roads is:
- All adjacent pairs in the cycle
- All pairs (capital, non-capital)

But if a capital is adjacent to another capital, that road appears in both the cycle and the capital connections. We need to make sure we don't double-count.

Actually, looking at the problem statement again: "There is at most one road between any two cities." This means each pair has at most one road.

So if a capital is connected to another city through both cycle and capital connection, we only count it once.

The problem says "each capital city is connected with each other city directly" - this suggests that capital-to-all connections exist, and the cycle connections exist.

But for a capital city and another city, if they are adjacent, there are two connections? That would violate "at most one road".

Therefore, I think the capital connections override or replace the cycle connections for capital pairs.

But the problem states "each capital city is connected with each other city directly" and "all cities are consecutively connected by roads" forming a cycle.

This suggests that both connections exist, but the key is that "at most one road" - so if both connections exist, there would be two roads, which is not allowed.

Therefore, the only interpretation is that the connections are:
1. The cycle edges: (1,2), (2,3), ..., (n-1,n), (n,1)
2. Additional edges from each capital to all other cities (but excluding the cycle edges if they already exist?)

This is confusing. Let me look at the examples.

Example 1: n=4, k=1, capitals=[3], values=[2,3,1,2]
- Cities: 1(2), 2(3), 3(1), 4(2)
- Cycle edges: (1,2)=2×3=6, (2,3)=3×1=3, (3,4)=1×2=2, (4,1)=2×2=4 → sum=6+3+2+4=15
- Capital city 3 is connected to all others: (3,1)=1×2=2, (3,2)=1×3=3, (3,4)=1×2=2
- But (2,3) and (3,4) are already in the cycle
- So if we add capital connections, we'd have duplicates

Looking at the answer: 17

15 + 2 + 3 + 2 = 22, too high.

Alternative interpretation: The capital connections exist, and the cycle connections exist, but when a capital connects to another city, it might replace the cycle connection.

But that seems arbitrary.

Another possibility: The cycle connections exist, and in addition, each capital has direct connections to every other city. But since the adjacent pairs are already in the cycle, we only add the non-adjacent capital connections.

But (3,1) in example 1: cities 3 and 1 are not adjacent (in cycle 1-2-3-4-1, 3 is adjacent to 2 and 4, not to 1). So (3,1) is not a cycle edge.

Cycle edges: (1,2), (2,3), (3,4), (4,1)
So (3,1) is not a cycle edge. So we should add it.

So total edges:
- Cycle edges: (1,2)=6, (2,3)=3, (3,4)=2, (4,1)=4 → sum=15
- Capital connections from city 3 to others: (3,1)=2, (3,2)=3, (3,4)=2
- But (3,2) and (3,4) are cycle edges, so we shouldn't add them again.

So we only add (3,1)=2.

Total sum = 15 + 2 = 17. This matches!

In example 2: n=5, k=2, capitals=[1,4], values=[3,5,2,2,4]
- Cycle edges: (1,2)=3×5=15, (2,3)=5×2=10, (3,4)=2×2=4, (4,5)=2×4=8, (5,1)=4×3=12 → sum=15+10+4+8+12=49
- Capital 1: connected to 2,3,4,5 (but (1,2) and (5,1) are cycle edges)

- Sum of non-cycle capital connections: 6+6+10 = 22
- Total sum = 49 + 22 = 71. This matches!

So the algorithm is:
1. Calculate sum of cycle edges: sum of c_i * c_{i+1} for i=1 to n-1, plus c_n * c_1
2. For each capital city, add connections to all non-adjacent cities in the cycle
3. For each capital city, only add the product if the other city is not adjacent to it in the cycle

For a capital city i, adjacent cities are (i-1) and (i+1) with modular arithmetic.
So non-adjacent cities are all other cities except (i-1) and (i+1).

But we don't want to double count when two capitals connect to each other.

- City 4 is adjacent to 3 and 5
- So (4,1) is not adjacent, so we would add it from capital 4.

This is a double-counting issue.

Actually, in the capital connections, we're adding every pair (capital, non-capital) with a direct road. But if we add it from both capitals, we're adding the product twice.

The problem says: "each capital city is connected with each other city directly by the roads" - this means each capital has a direct connection to every other city.

But since we're considering all pairs, and each pair (a,b) where a is capital and b is not capital should be included exactly once.

In my earlier analysis, I only added non-adjacent pairs. But the capital connections should include all pairs between capital and non-capital, regardless of adjacency.

The key is: the "cycle connections" are separate from the "capital connections". The problem says both exist, but "at most one road" means if there's a cycle edge between a capital and a non-capital, that's already covered, so we don't need to add it again.

- Cycle edges sum
- Plus sum of c_cap * c_other for each capital and each other city where they are not adjacent in the cycle

I think I need to reconsider the problem statement.

After careful analysis of the examples, let me try to derive the answer differently.

For example 1: n=4, k=1, c=[2,3,1,2], capital=[3]
- All pairs that have roads:
- Cycle pairs: (1,2)=6, (2,3)=3, (3,4)=2, (4,1)=4

- So we only add (3,1)=2

- capital-to-capital pairs where i < j

Alternative approach:

The total sum of all products for all pairs (i,j) with i < j is fixed, but we only want pairs that have roads.

The roads are:
- All adjacent pairs in the cycle
- All pairs (i,j) where i is a capital and j is any other city, and (i,j) are not adjacent in the cycle

But wait, in the cycle, if i and j are adjacent, they have a road. If they are not adjacent, and one is capital, they have a road from the capital rule.

But if both are capitals, and not adjacent, they have a road.

The total sum is:
- Sum of c_i * c_{i+1} for i=1 to n-1, plus c_n * c_1 (cycle edges)
- Plus sum of c_i * c_j for all pairs (i,j) where i is capital, j is any city, and (i,j) are not adjacent in the cycle

But in this, if i and j are both capitals and not adjacent, we add c_i * c_j once, and it's fine.

Perfect!

And there's no double counting because each pair (i,j) with i capital and j not adjacent to i in the cycle is added exactly once (when we process capital i).

We don't need to worry about j being capital - if j is capital, it's still one pair, and we add it once.

The only thing is that we must ensure we don't add the adjacent pairs.

So algorithm:
1. Read input
2. Calculate cycle_sum = sum of c_i * c_{i+1} for i=1 to n-1, plus c_n * c_1
3. additional_sum = 0
4. For each capital city i:
   - Find its adjacent cities in the cycle: (i-1) and (i+1), with modular arithmetic
   - For each city j from 1 to n:
     - if j != i and j is not in {adjacent cities}:
       - additional_sum += c[i-1] * c[j-1]
5. total_sum = cycle_sum + additional_sum

But careful: in the input, cities are 1-indexed, and we have c1, c2, ..., cn.

In code:
- c[0] is c1, c[1] is c2, etc.

For city i (1-indexed), adjacent cities are:
- (i-1) and (i+1), with wrap around
- So if i=1, adjacent to n and 2
- if i=n, adjacent to n-1 and 1
- if 1<i<n, adjacent to i-1 and i+1

So we can implement it.

But complexity: for each capital, we iterate over n cities, total O(k*n) which is 10^10 in worst case, too slow.

We need to optimize.

For a capital city i, we want to sum c[i] * c[j] for all j not adjacent to i.

This is equal to:
- (sum of all c[j] for j != adjacent) * c[i] 
- minus c[i] * c[adjacent1] - c[i] * c[adjacent2] 
- but only if adjacent cities are not i

Actually:
- Total sum of c[j] for j=1 to n = total_sum
- Sum of c[j] for j in adjacent cities = c[adj1] + c[adj2]
- So sum of c[j] for j not in adjacent = total_sum - c[adj1] - c[adj2]

Therefore, for capital i, additional contribution = c[i] * (total_sum - c[adj1] - c[adj2])

But wait, this includes j=i? We have j != i, but in our sum, we have j not in adjacent, and j could be i.

We need j != i.

In the adjacent cities, i is not included, so we are summing over j not in {adj1, adj2}, and j != i.

But if i is not in {adj1, adj2}, which it isn't, then we are summing over j not in {adj1, adj2}, which excludes i only if i is in {adj1, adj2}, which it isn't.

So actually, the sum is over j not in {adj1, adj2}, and j from 1 to n, which automatically excludes i and the adjacent cities.

So we want to exclude only the two adjacent cities.

Therefore, for each capital i:
- adjacent1, adjacent2 = the two adjacent cities
- sum_over_j = (sum of c[j] for all j) - c[adj1] - c[adj2]
- additional_sum += c[i] * sum_over_j

But this sum_over_j is for all j not in {adj1, adj2}, which is what we want.

Let's verify with example 1:
- Cities: c=[2,3,1,2] (c0=2, c1=3, c2=1, c3=2)
- total_sum = 2+3+1+2 = 8
- capital i=3 (index 2)
- adjacent1: i-1=2 (index 1), adjacent2: i+1=4 (index 3)
- sum_over_j = 8 - c[1] - c[3] = 8 - 3 - 2 = 3
- additional_sum += c[2] * 3 = 1 * 3 = 3
- cycle_sum = c0*c1 + c1*c2 + c2*c3 + c3*c0 = 2*3 + 3*1 + 1*2 + 2*2 = 6+3+2+4 = 15
- total = 15+3 = 18 ≠ 17

Oh no! We got 18.

Why? Because we want only j not adjacent to i, but in the formula, we have j not in {adj1, adj2}, which is correct.

- c[1]=3, c[3]=2, sum=5
- sum_over_j = 8-5=3
- we add 1*3=3

But manually, we should only add c3 * c1 = 1*2 = 2.

The issue is that in our sum, we are adding c[i] * c[j] for all j not adjacent to i, including j=i? No.

In our sum_over_j, j goes from 1 to n, not including adj1 and adj2.

So j=1,2,3,4 except 2 and 4 → j=1,3
- j=1: c1=3

- sum = 5

This is even worse.

I see the mistake.

We are not summing over j in the city indices with their values.

In the formula, sum_over_j means sum of c[j] for j not in {adj1, adj2}.

For city i=3 (index 2), adjacent are city 2 and city 4 (indices 1 and 3).

So cities not in {2,4} are: 1 and 3 (indices 0 and 2)

c[0]=2, c[2]=1, sum = 2+1 = 3

Then additional_sum += c[2] * 3 = 1 * 3 = 3

But manually, we only have one pair: (3,1) with value 1*2=2

Why the discrepancy?

Because when j=1 (city 1), the pair is (3,1), value c[2]*c[0] = 1*2 = 2
When j=3 (city 3), the pair is (3,3), which is invalid (same city)

In our iteration, we said j != i and j not in adjacent.

For j=3 (city 3), i=3, so j=i, so we should exclude j=i.

In the sum_over_j, we have a city j that might be equal to i.

In the adjacent set, we don't include i, so when we do j in all cities except adjacent, we still might have j=i.

In city i, we have city i itself, which is not in the adjacent set, so we include it.

But we shouldn't include j=i.

Therefore, we need to exclude j=i as well.

So sum_over_j = sum of c[j] for j not in {adj1, adj2, i}

So it's total_sum - c[adj1] - c[adj2] - c[i]

In example 1:
- total_sum = 8
- adj1 = city 2 (c[1]=3), adj2 = city 4 (c[3]=2), c[i]=c[2]=1
- sum_over_j = 8 - 3 - 2 - 1 = 2
- additional_sum += 1 * 2 = 2
- total = 15+2 = 17 ✓

In example 2:
- c = [3,5,2,2,4], total_sum = 3+5+2+2+4 = 16
- capitals: 1 and 4
- For capital 1 (index 0):
  - adjacent1: city 5 (index 4), adjacent2: city 2 (index 1)
  - c[adj1]=4, c[adj2]=5, c[i]=3
  - sum_over_j = 16 - 4 - 5 - 3 = 4
  - additional_sum += 3 * 4 = 12
- For capital 4 (index 3):
  - adjacent1: city 3 (index 2), adjacent2: city 5 (index 4)
  - c[adj1]=2, c[adj2]=4, c[i]=2
  - sum_over_j = 16 - 2 - 4 - 2 = 8
  - additional_sum += 2 * 8 = 16
- total additional = 12+16 = 28
- cycle_sum = 3*5 + 5*2 + 2*2 + 2*4 + 4*3 = 15+10+4+8+12 = 49
- total = 49+28 = 77 ≠ 71

Still not 71.

What's wrong?

In example 2, for capital 1, we are adding for j not in {5,2,1} → j not in {5,2,1} → j=3,4
- j=3: city 3, c[2]=2
- j=4: city 4, c[3]=2
- sum = 4, c[0]*4 = 3*4 = 12
- pairs: (1,3)=3*2=6, (1,4)=3*2=6 → sum=12 ✓

For capital 4, j not in {3,5,4} → j=1,2
- j=1: c[0]=3, j=2: c[1]=5
- sum = 3+5=8, c[3]*8 = 2*8 = 16
- pairs: (4,1)=2*3=6, (4,2)=2*5=10 → sum=16

But the pair (1,4) is counted in both: from capital 1 when j=4, and from capital 4 when j=1.

In our current method, we are counting each pair twice: once as (1,4) when i=1, j=4, and once as (4,1) when i=4, j=1.

But (1,4) is a single pair, and we should only count it once.

So we are double-counting the pair between two capitals.

In example 2, the pair (1,4) is between two capitals, and we are adding it twice: once in capital 1's loop and once in capital 4's loop.

We need to avoid this.

How to fix?

We can do:
- For each capital i, we add c[i] * (sum of c[j] for j not in {adj1, adj2, i})
- But then subtract the extra for each pair (i,j) where both i and j are capitals and j is not adjacent to i.

In example 2, only pair (1,4) is between two capitals and not adjacent.

For that pair, we have added c[0]*c[3] = 3*2 = 6 twice in the above sum (once in i=0, once in i=3)

So we need to subtract once the value 6.

But we have to do it for each such pair.

Specifically, for each pair (i,j) where i and j are both in capitals, and j is not adjacent to i, and i < j, we have added c[i]*c[j] twice (once in i's loop and once in j's loop), but should only add once.

So we need to subtract c[i]*c[j] once for each such pair.

In example 2, only (1,4) should be subtracted once with value 6.

So in the additional_sum, after the double loop, we do:
- for each pair (i,j) with i in capitals, j in capitals, i < j, and not adjacent in cycle:
  - additional_sum -= c[i] * c[j]

In example 2:
- additional_sum before adjustment = 12 + 16 = 28
- subtract c[0]*c[3] = 3*2 = 6
- additional_sum = 22
- total = 49+22 = 71 ✓

In example 1:
- only one capital, so no such pairs
- additional_sum = 2
- total = 15+2 = 17 ✓

Perfect.

So final algorithm:
1. Read input
2. Calculate cycle_sum = sum for i in 0 to n-2 of c[i] * c[i+1] + c[n-1] * c[0]
3. additional_sum = 0
4. total_sum = sum of all c[i]
5. For each capital city i (0-indexed):
   - adj1 = (i-1) % n, adj2 = (i+1) % n
   - sum_j = total_sum - c[adj1] - c[adj2] - c[i]
   - additional_sum += c[i] * sum_j
6. For each pair (i,j) with i < j, both in capital list, and not adjacent in cycle:
   - if (i, j) are not adjacent (i.e., (j-1) != (i+1) % n and (j-1) != (i-1) % n) - actually need to check if |i-j| or modular distance is 1
   - specifically, if (i and j are not adjacent in cycle) and i < j:
     - additional_sum -= c[i] * c[j]
7. total_sum = cycle_sum + additional_sum

But in step 6, we need to check if two capitals are adjacent in the cycle.

Two cities i and j are adjacent if |i-j| = 1 or |i-j| = n-1.

In indices: if (j - i) % n == 1 or (j - i) % n == n-1.

For example, in n=5, cities 0 and 4 are adjacent.

So in code:
- for each pair of capitals with i < j:
  - if (j - i) % n == 1 or (j - i) % n == n-1:
    - skip (adjacent, so should not be in the subtraction)
  - else:
    - additional_sum -= c[i] * c[j]

Let me verify with example 2:
- capitals: 0 (city1), 3 (city4)
- i=0, j=3: (3-0)%5 = 3, which is not 1 or 4, so not adjacent → subtract c[0]*c[3] = 3*2 = 6
- additional_sum becomes 28 - 6 = 22
- total = 49+22 = 71 ✓

Example 1: only one capital, no such pairs.

This should work.

Now complexity: step 5: O(k) * O(1) = O(k)
step 6: O(k^2) in worst case, since k up to 100,000, k^2 = 10^10, which is too slow.

We need to optimize step 6.

Instead of checking all pairs, we can calculate the sum of c[i]*c[j] for all i< j in capitals that are not adjacent.

We can do:
- total_cap_sum = sum of c[i] for i in capitals
- sum_of_squares = sum of c[i]^2 for i in capitals
- sum_of_products = sum over i< j of c[i]*c[j] = (total_cap_sum^2 - sum_of_squares) / 2

Then, for adjacent pairs in capitals, we want to subtract the sum of c[i]*c[j] for adjacent pairs.

Let adj_cap_sums = sum of c[i]*c[j] for adjacent pairs (i,j) with i< j and adjacent in cycle.

Then additional_sum = (additional_sum_before_subtraction) - (sum_of_products - adj_cap_sums) 
because we have double-counted the non-adjacent pairs.

Wait, in our double counting, for a pair (i,j) with i< j and not adjacent, we have added c[i]*c[j] twice (in i's loop and j's loop), but should only add once, so we need to subtract once.

So we need to subtract exactly the sum of c[i]*c[j] for all i< j in capitals that are not adjacent.

This is equal to: (sum of c[i]*c[j] for all i< j in capitals) minus (sum of c[i]*c[j] for adjacent pairs)

So:
- let all_pairs_sum = (total_cap_sum^2 - sum_of_squares) / 2
- let adjacent_pairs_sum = sum over adjacent pairs (i,j) in capitals with i< j of c[i]*c[j]
- then we need to subtract (all_pairs_sum - adjacent_pairs_sum) = (total_cap_sum^2 - sum_of_squares)/2 - adjacent_pairs_sum

But in our additional_sum, we have added for each capital i: c[i] * (sum of c[j] for j not in {adj1, adj2, i})

This includes for each pair (i,j) with both i,j in capitals and not adjacent: it is added twice (once in i's loop, once in j's loop)

For each pair (i,j) with i,j in capitals and adjacent in cycle: it is added twice in the loop (once in i's loop, once in j's loop), but in reality, for an adjacent pair (i,j), in i's loop, when we consider j, j is adjacent to i, so it is not in the sum (because we exclude adjacent cities), so it is not added.

Similarly in j's loop, i is adjacent to j, so not added.

Therefore, for adjacent pairs, the product is not added in the loop at all.

For non-adjacent pairs (i,j), the product is added twice: once in i's loop, once in j's loop.

Therefore, in additional_sum_after_loop, the value for non-adjacent pairs is 2 * (c[i]*c[j]), and we should have only 1 * (c[i]*c[j]).

So we need to subtract exactly (c[i]*c[j]) for each non-adjacent pair (i,j) with i< j and both in capitals.

In other words, subtract: sum_{i< j, both in capitals, not adjacent} c[i]*c[j]

This is equal to: (sum_{i< j} c[i]*c[j]) - (sum_{i< j, adjacent} c[i]*c[j])

So:
- let total_pair_sum = (total_cap_sum^2 - sum_of_squares) / 2
- let adjacent_pair_sum = 0
- for each pair of capitals with i< j and adjacent in cycle:
  - adjacent_pair_sum += c[i] * c[j]
- then subtract from additional_sum: (total_pair_sum - adjacent_pair_sum)

In example 2:
- capitals: [0,3], c=[3,5,2,2,4] → c[0]=3, c[3]=2
- total_cap_sum = 3+2 = 5
- sum_of_squares = 9 + 4 = 13
- total_pair_sum = (25 - 13)/2 = 6
- adjacent_pair_sum: i=0,j=3, (3-0)%5 = 3, not 1 or 4, so not adjacent → adjacent_pair_sum = 0
- so subtract = 6 - 0 = 6
- additional_sum becomes 28 - 6 = 22 ✓

In example 1: only one capital, so no pairs, adjacent_pair_sum=0, total_pair_sum=0, subtract 0.

Perfect.

Now, to compute adjacent_pair_sum, we can iterate over the capital list and check pairs.

Since k can be up to 100,000, but the number of adjacent pairs is at most k, and in practice, for a given capital, only a few are adjacent, but the number of pairs is C(k,2), which is O(k^2), too slow.

We need to be smarter.

Actually, for a capital city i, the adjacent cities in the cycle are (i-1) and (i+1) (mod n).

Among the capitals, only if (i-1) or (i+1) is also a capital, then it forms an adjacent pair.

So we can iterate over the capital list, and for each capital, check if its left or right neighbor is also a capital.

But we need to avoid double-counting.

For each capital i, if (i-1) is a capital, and i < (i-1) in index, then we have a pair (i, i-1) with i < (i-1) only if i < (i-1), which is not true.

We need to ensure i < j.

So for each capital i, consider its neighbors: left = (i-1) % n, right = (i+1) % n

If left is a capital and left > i, then the pair (i, left) with i < left, and they are adjacent? (i and left are adjacent only if |i - left| = 1 or |i - left| = n-1)

For example, if i=0, left= n-1, then if n-1 is a capital and i < n-1, then the pair (0, n-1) is adjacent.

Similarly, if right is a capital and i < right, then pair (i, right) is adjacent.

So we can do:
- adjacent_pair_sum = 0
- for each capital i:
  - left = (i-1) % n
  - right = (i+1) % n
  - if left is in the set of capitals and i < left:
    - adjacent_pair_sum += c[i] * c[left]
  - if right is in the set of capitals and i < right:
    - adjacent_pair_sum += c[i] * c[right]

But we must ensure that (i, left) with i < left is only counted once.

And since each adjacent pair will be counted exactly once (when we are at the smaller index), it should work.

In example 2: capitals at 0 and 3
- i=0: left=4, right=1
  - 4 is not a capital, 1 is not a capital → no addition
- i=3: left=2, right=4
  - 2 is not a capital, 4 is not a capital → no addition
- adjacent_pair_sum = 0 ✓

Another example: n=4, capitals at 0,1,3
- i=0: left=3, right=1
  - 3 is a capital, and 0 < 3 → add c[0]*c[3]
  - 1 is a capital, and 0 < 1 → add c[0]*c[1]
- i=1: left=0, right=2
  - 0 is a capital, but 1 > 0 → skip
  - 2 is not a capital → skip
- i=3: left=2, right=0
  - 2 not capital, 0 is capital, but 3 > 0 → skip
- adjacent_pair_sum = c[0]*c[3] + c[0]*c[1]

But the adjacent pairs are:
- (0,1): adjacent? in cycle 0-1-2-3-0, yes
- (0,3): adjacent? yes
- (1,2): not both capitals
- (3,0): adjacent? yes, but already included in (0,3)

So we have (0,1) and (0,3), both adjacent.

In the sum, we have c[0] * (c[1] + c[3]) which is correct.

And we are only counting each pair once (when the smaller index is processed).

Perfect.

So final algorithm:
1. Read n, k, c, and capitals (0-indexed)
2. cycle_sum = 0
   - for i in range(n-1): cycle_sum += c[i] * c[i+1]
   - cycle_sum += c[n-1] * c[0]
3. total_sum = sum(c)
4. additional_sum = 0
5. For each capital i:
   - adj1 = (i-1) % n, adj2 = (i+1) % n
   - sum_j = total_sum - c[adj1] - c[adj2] - c[i]
   - additional_sum += c[i] * sum_j
6. adjacent_pair_sum = 0
   - Create a set of capital indices for O(1) lookup
   - For each capital i in the list (in any order):
     - left = (i-1) % n
     - right = (i+1) % n
     - if left in capital_set and i < left:
       - adjacent_pair_sum += c[i] * c[left]
     - if right in capital_set and i < right:
       - adjacent_pair_sum += c[i] * c[right]
7. total_additional = additional_sum - adjacent_pair_sum
8. print(cycle_sum + total_additional)

Let's test with a small example.

Example: n=3, k=2, c=[1,2,3], capitals=[0,1] (cities 1,2)
- cycle_sum = c0*c1 + c1*c2 + c2*c0 = 1*2 + 2*3 + 3*1 = 2+6+3=11
- total_sum = 1+2+3=6
- additional_sum = 0
- For i=0:
  - adj1 = (0-1)%3=2, adj2=1
  - sum_j = 6 - c[2] - c[1] - c[0] = 6-3-2-1 = 0
  - additional_sum += 1*0 = 0
- For i=1:
  - adj1 = 0, adj2=2
  - sum_j = 6 - c[0] - c[2] - c[1] = 6-1-3-2 = 0
  - additional_sum += 2*0 = 0
- adjacent_pair_sum:
  - i=0: left=2 not in capitals, right=1 in capitals and 0<1 → add c[0]*c[1] = 1*2 = 2
  - i=1: left=0 in capitals and 1>0 → skip, right=2 not in capitals → skip
- total_additional = 0 - 2 = -2
- total = 11 + (-2) = 9

But manually:
- cycle edges: (1,2)=2, (2,3)=6, (3,1)=3 → sum=11
- capital connections: city1 (index0) to cities 2,3: (1,2)=2, (1,3)=3
- city2 (index1) to cities 1,3: (2,1)=2, (2,3)=6
- But adjacent pairs: (1,2) and (2,3) are already in cycle, so we only add non-adjacent:
  - (1,3): not adjacent in cycle (1 adjacent to 2 and 3, so in cycle: 1-2, 2-3, 3-1 → 1 and 3 are adjacent if n=3? In cycle 1-2-3-1, so 1 is adjacent to 2 and 3.
- So for city1: not adjacent to any other? it is adjacent to 2 and 3.
- For city2: adjacent to 1 and 3.
- So no non-adjacent pairs.
- So additional_sum should be 0.
- total = 11.

But in our calculation, we have total_additional = -2.

The issue is in the adjacent_pair_sum.

For i=0: right=1, and 0<1, so we add c[0]*c[1] = 1*2 = 2
But cities 0 and 1 are adjacent, so we should subtract it.

In the loop, we have added c[i] * sum_j = 0 for both, so additional_sum=0.
Then we subtract adjacent_pair_sum=2, so total_additional = -2.

This is wrong.

Why? Because in the loop, for city0, we should have added for j not in {2,1,0} → j not in {2,1,0} → no j, so 0.
For city1: j not in {0,2,1} → no j, so 0.
So additional_sum=0.
Then we subtract the adjacent_pair_sum=2, so -2.

But there are no non-adjacent pairs to be subtracted.

The non-adjacent pairs in the capital group are none.

So we should not subtract anything.

The adjacent_pair_sum is for adjacent pairs in the capital group, and we are to subtract the sum of c[i]*c[j] for non-adjacent pairs.

In this case, the only pair in capitals is (0,1), which is adjacent, so it should not be in the subtraction.

We only subtract for pairs that are not adjacent.

Therefore, we should only add to adjacent_pair_sum if the pair is adjacent.

In the algorithm, we are only adding when the pair is adjacent and i < j.

Then we subtract for non-adjacent pairs.

In this example, there is one pair (0,1) which is adjacent, so we add it to adjacent_pair_sum, then we subtract it, which is wrong because it should not be in the subtraction.

We need to subtract the sum of c[i]*c[j] for non-adjacent pairs.

In this example, there are no non-adjacent pairs, so we should subtract 0.

But in our adjacent_pair_sum, we have computed the sum for adjacent pairs, and we want to subtract the sum for non-adjacent pairs.

In the code, we have:
- total_pair_sum = (sum of c[i]^2 for i in capitals) / 2
- then subtract for adjacent pairs to get non-adjacent pairs sum = total_pair_sum - adjacent_pair_sum

Then we do: additional_sum -= (non_adjacent_pair_sum)

In the example:
- capitals: [0,1], c=[1,2]
- total_cap_sum = 3
- sum_of_squares = 1+4=5
- total_pair_sum = (9-5)/2 = 2
- adjacent_pair_sum = 1*2 = 2 (for pair (0,1))
- non_adjacent_pair_sum = 2 - 2 = 0
- additional_sum = 0 (before) - 0 = 0
- total = 11+0=11 ✓

Perfect.

So in step 6, we don't need to store adjacent_pair_sum, we can compute non_adjacent_pair_sum = total_pair_sum - adjacent_pair_sum

Then subtract from additional_sum.

Final code:

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = list(map(int, input().split()))
# Convert to 0-indexed
capitals = [x-1 for x in capitals]

# Calculate cycle sum
cycle_sum = 0
for i in range(n-1):
    cycle_sum += c[i] * c[i+1]
cycle_sum += c[n-1] * c[0]

# Total sum of beauty values
total_sum = sum(c)

# Additional sum from capital connections (for non-adjacent pairs)
additional_sum = 0

# For each capital city
for i in capitals:
    # Adjacent cities in cycle
    adj1 = (i - 1) % n
    adj2 = (i + 1) % n
    # Sum of c[j] for j not in {adj1, adj2, i}
    sum_j = total_sum - c[adj1] - c[adj2] - c[i]
    additional_sum += c[i] * sum_j

# Now, we need to subtract the sum of c[i]*c[j] for non-adjacent pairs among capitals
# First, compute total_pair_sum = sum_{i<j} c[i]*c[j] = (total_cap_sum^2 - sum_of_squares) / 2
total_cap_sum = sum(c[i] for i in capitals)
sum_of_squares = sum(c[i] * c[i] for i in capitals)
total_pair_sum = (total_cap_sum * total_cap_sum - sum_of_squares) // 2

# Compute adjacent_pair_sum = sum of c[i]*c[j] for adjacent pairs in capitals (i< j)
adjacent_pair_sum = 0
cap_set = set(capitals)
for i in capitals:
    left = (i - 1) % n
    right = (i + 1) % n
    if left in cap_set and i < left:
        adjacent_pair_sum += c[i] * c[left]
    if right in cap_set and i < right:
        adjacent_pair_sum += c[i] * c[right]

# non_adjacent_pair_sum = total_pair_sum - adjacent_pair_sum
non_adjacent_pair_sum = total_pair_sum - adjacent_pair_sum

# Subtract the non-adjacent pair sums (since they were double-counted)
additional_sum -= non_adjacent_pair_sum

result = cycle_sum + additional_sum
print(result)
```

Let me test with the examples.

Example 1: n=4, k=1, c=[2,3,1,2], capitals=[3-1=2] (0-indexed)
- cycle_sum = 2*3 + 3*1 + 1*2 + 2*2 = 6+3+2+4 = 15
- total_sum = 8
- for i=2: adj1=(1), adj2=(3)
  - sum_j = 8 - c[1] - c[3] - c[2] = 8-3-2-1 = 2
  - additional_sum += 1 * 2 = 2
- total_cap_sum = 1, sum_of_squares = 1, total_pair_sum = (1-1)//2 = 0
- adjacent_pair_sum = 0 (no other capitals)
- non_adjacent_pair_sum = 0
- additional_sum = 2
- result = 15+2 = 17 ✓

Example 2: n=5, k=2, c=[3,5,2,2,4], capitals=[0,3]
- cycle_sum = 3*5 + 5*2 + 2*2 + 2*4 + 4*3 = 15+10+4+8+12 = 49
- total_sum = 16
- for i=0: adj1=4, adj2=1
  - sum_j = 16 - c[4] - c[1] - c[0] = 16-4-5-3 = 4
  - additional_sum += 3 * 4 = 12
- for i=3: adj1=2, adj2=4
  - sum_j = 16 - c[2] - c[4] - c[3] = 16-2-4-2 = 8
  - additional_sum += 2 * 8 = 16
- additional_sum = 28
- total_cap_sum = 3+2=5, sum_of_squares=9+4=13, total_pair_sum=(25-13)//2=6
- adjacent_pair_sum: for i=0: left=4 not in capitals, right=1 not in capitals → 0; for i=3: left=2 not, right=4 not → 0
- non_adjacent_pair_sum = 6-0=6
- additional_sum = 28-6 = 22
- result = 49+22=71 ✓

Perfect.

Final code:

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = list(map(int, input().split()))
# Convert to 0-indexed
capitals = [x-1 for x in capitals]

# Calculate cycle sum
cycle_sum = 0
for i in range(n-1):
    cycle_sum += c[i] * c[i+1]
cycle_sum += c[n-1] * c[0]

# Total sum of beauty values
total_sum = sum(c)

# Additional sum from capital connections (for non-adjacent pairs)
additional_sum = 0

# For each capital city
for i in capitals:
    # Adjacent cities in cycle
    adj1 = (i - 1) % n
    adj2 = (i + 1) % n
    # Sum of c[j] for j not in {adj1, adj2, i}
    sum_j = total_sum - c[adj1] - c[adj2] - c[i]
    additional_sum += c[i] * sum_j

# Now, we need to subtract the sum of c[i]*c[j] for non-adjacent pairs among capitals
# First, compute total_pair_sum = sum_{i<j} c[i]*c[j] = (total_cap_sum^2 - sum_of_squares) / 2
total_cap_sum = sum(c[i] for i in capitals)
sum_of_squares = sum(c[i] * c[i] for i in capitals)
total_pair_sum = (total_cap_sum * total_cap_sum - sum_of_squares) // 2

# Compute adjacent_pair_sum = sum of c[i]*c[j] for adjacent pairs in capitals (i< j)
adjacent_pair_sum = 0
cap_set = set(capitals)
for i in capitals:
    left = (i - 1) % n
    right = (i + 1) % n
    if left in cap_set and i < left:
        adjacent_pair_sum += c[i] * c[left]
    if right in cap_set and i < right:
        adjacent_pair_sum += c[i] * c[right]

# non_adjacent_pair_sum = total_pair_sum - adjacent_pair_sum
non_adjacent_pair_sum = total_pair_sum - adjacent_pair_sum

# Subtract the non-adjacent pair sums (since they were double-counted)
additional_sum -= non_adjacent_pair_sum

result = cycle_sum + additional_sum
print(result)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Little Mishka is a great traveller and she visited many countries. After thinking about where to travel this time, she chose XXX — beautiful, but little-known northern country.

Here are some interesting facts about XXX:

  1. XXX consists of n cities, k of whose (just imagine!) are capital cities. 
  2. All of cities in the country are beautiful, but each is beautiful in its own way. Beauty value of i-th city equals to ci. 
  3. All the cities are consecutively connected by the roads, including 1-st and n-th city, forming a cyclic route 1 — 2 — ... — n — 1. Formally, for every 1 ≤ i < n there is a road between i-th and i + 1-th city, and another one between 1-st and n-th city. 
  4. Each capital city is connected with each other city directly by the roads. Formally, if city x is a capital city, then for every 1 ≤ i ≤ n, i ≠ x, there is a road between cities x and i. 
  5. There is at most one road between any two cities. 
  6. Price of passing a road directly depends on beauty values of cities it connects. Thus if there is a road between cities i and j, price of passing it equals ci·cj.



Mishka started to gather her things for a trip, but didn't still decide which route to follow and thus she asked you to help her determine summary price of passing each of the roads in XXX. Formally, for every pair of cities a and b (a < b), such that there is a road between a and b you are to find sum of products ca·cb. Will you help her?

Input

The first line of the input contains two integers n and k (3 ≤ n ≤ 100 000, 1 ≤ k ≤ n) — the number of cities in XXX and the number of capital cities among them.

The second line of the input contains n integers c1, c2, ..., cn (1 ≤ ci ≤ 10 000) — beauty values of the cities.

The third line of the input contains k distinct integers id1, id2, ..., idk (1 ≤ idi ≤ n) — indices of capital cities. Indices are given in ascending order.

Output

Print the only integer — summary price of passing each of the roads in XXX.

Examples

Input

4 1
2 3 1 2
3


Output

17

Input

5 2
3 5 2 2 4
1 4


Output

71

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` cities arranged in a cycle: 1-2-3-...-n-1
2. There are `k` capital cities
3. Between any two cities, there's at most one road
4. The roads are:
   - The cyclic connections: 1-2, 2-3, ..., (n-1)-n, n-1
   - Direct connections from each capital city to every other city
5. Price of a road between cities i and j is `ci * cj`
6. We need to find the sum of prices for all roads

## Key Insights

1. **Cyclic roads**: The cycle has exactly `n` roads: (1,2), (2,3), ..., (n-1,n), (n,1)
   - The cost of these roads is: `c1*c2 + c2*c3 + ... + cn*c1`

2. **Capital connections**: For each capital city `x`, it has direct connections to all other cities `i ≠ x`
   - The cost for capital `x` connecting to city `i` is `cx * ci`
   - Total cost for capital `x` is: `cx * (c1 + c2 + ... + cn - cx)` = `cx * (total_sum - cx)`

3. **Important constraint**: "There is at most one road between any two cities" - this means we don't double count any road.

4. **No double counting**: The cyclic roads and capital roads might overlap. However, since capital connections include all direct connections, and the cycle connection is between adjacent cities only, we need to be careful about overlap.

The problem says "all cities are consecutively connected" - so all adjacent pairs have a cycle edge.

Additionally, each capital city has direct edges to all other cities.

And "there is at most one road between any two cities" - this means that if a capital city x has a direct edge to city y, then that edge exists, and any cycle edge between x and y would be redundant.

So actually, for any pair of cities (i,j), there is a road between them if and only if:
- i and j are adjacent in the cycle, OR
- at least one of i or j is a capital city

But wait, if i and j are adjacent and only one is a capital, then both conditions are satisfied (adjacent AND one is capital). If both are capitals and adjacent, then both conditions are satisfied.

But if both are non-capitals and are not adjacent, then there's no road between them? 

However, if one of them is a capital, then even if they're not adjacent, there's a direct road.

But if both are non-capitals and not adjacent, then there's no road.

But the problem says "each capital city is connected with each other city directly" - so capital cities have connections to every city.

And the cycle has connections between consecutive cities.

So for any pair (i,j):
- If i and j are adjacent, there is a cycle edge
- If at least one of i or j is a capital, there is a capital edge

Since at most one road exists, if both edges exist, we only count one.

So the road between i and j exists if either:
1. i and j are adjacent in the cycle, OR
2. at least one of i or j is a capital

But wait, case 1 is always true for adjacent pairs, case 2 is true for pairs involving at least one capital.

But if a pair is adjacent and contains a capital, then both conditions are true, so we only count it once.

So the set of edges is: all edges between adjacent cities, plus all edges from capital cities to all other cities, minus the duplicate edges between a capital and an adjacent city.

Actually, no - if there's a capital at position x, and it connects to city y, this edge exists. But if y is adjacent to x, the cycle edge already exists. So we don't need to add both.

The total set of edges is the union of:
- All cycle edges between adjacent cities
- All edges from capital cities to other cities

And since we have at most one edge, we just take the union.

So the total cost is: sum of `ci * cj` for all pairs (i,j) where:
- |i - j| = 1 (mod n), OR
- at least one of i or j is a capital

But we can't just add the cycle edges and capital edges because of overlap.

Let me think of a better approach.

Actually, I realize there's a much simpler way:

Total cost = sum over all roads of `ci * cj`

The roads are:
1. The n cyclic edges: (1,2), (2,3), ..., (n-1,n), (n,1)
2. The edges from each capital to other cities that don't exist in the cycle

But for a capital city x, it needs to connect to all other cities except itself. However, if a city y is adjacent to x, then the edge (x,y) is already in the cycle. So we only add the edges from capital x to non-adjacent cities.

Wait, that's not right. If there's an edge from capital x to y, it's a direct edge. The cycle already has edges between adjacent cities. So if both exist for the same pair, we only count one.

But the capital edge (x,y) exists for any y ≠ x. So the direct connections from capitals cover all pairs where at least one city is a capital.

The cycle edges cover pairs that are adjacent.

So the final set of edges is:
- All adjacent pairs (i,i+1) 
- All pairs (x,y) where x is a capital and y ≠ x and (x,y) is not an adjacent pair

That is: for each capital x, it connects to all non-adjacent cities.

So total cost = sum of cycle edges + sum of capital edges to non-adjacent cities.

Let me verify with the first example:
n=4, k=1, capitals=[3], c=[2,3,1,2]

Cyclic edges:
(1,2): 2*3 = 6
(2,3): 3*1 = 3  
(3,4): 1*2 = 2
(4,1): 2*2 = 4
Sum = 6+3+2+4 = 15

Capital at 3: connects to cities 1,2,4
Adjacency for city 3: adjacent to 2 and 4
So non-adjacent: city 1
Edge (3,1): 1*2 = 2

Total cost = 15 + 2 = 17 ✓

Perfect! This matches.

So the algorithm:
1. Calculate the sum of cycle edges: sum(c[i]*c[(i+1)%n]) for i from 0 to n-1
2. For each capital city x (0-indexed), it has direct edges to all cities except adjacent ones
3. For each capital at position x, add the sum of c[x] * c[y] for all y that are not x and not (x-1) and not (x+1) (mod n)
4. Total cost = sum of cycle edges + sum of capital non-adjacent edges

Wait, actually in the cycle edges, we already have the edges between adjacent cities. When we add capital edges, we exclude adjacent ones to avoid double counting.

But in the capital edge addition, we're adding edges from capital to non-adjacent cities.

For a capital at x, it connects to all non-adjacent cities y (where y is not x, not (x-1), not (x+1) mod n).

The sum for capital x is: c[x] * (sum of c[y] for all y not x, not (x-1), not (x+1))

Let me write the code:

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Convert to 0-indexed
capitals = [x - 1 for x in capitals]

# Calculate sum of cycle edges
cycle_sum = 0
for i in range(n):
    j = (i + 1) % n
    cycle_sum += c[i] * c[j]

# Calculate sum of capital non-adjacent edges
capital_sum = 0
for capital in capitals:
    # Sum of beauty values of all cities except capital and its two neighbors
    total_sum = sum(c)
    # Subtract c[capital] and its adjacent cities
    neighbor1 = (capital - 1) % n
    neighbor2 = (capital + 1) % n
    adjacent_sum = c[neighbor1] + c[neighbor2]
    # Also subtract c[capital] itself
    excluded_sum = c[capital] + adjacent_sum
    remaining_sum = total_sum - excluded_sum
    capital_sum += c[capital] * remaining_sum

total_cost = cycle_sum + capital_sum
print(total_cost)
```

I think I misunderstood the problem. Let me reread.

"each capital city is connected with each other city directly by the roads" - this means every capital connects to every other city. 

But the cyclic roads already exist. 

And "at most one road between any two cities" - so if a capital connects to another city, and there's a cycle edge between them, we only count one.

- All cycle edges

This is way off.

I see the mistake! 

If a city is a capital, it connects to every other city. So for capital at city 1 (index 0), it connects to cities: 1,2,3,4 (0-indexed: 1,2,3,4)

For capital at city 4 (index 3), it connects to cities: 0,1,2,4 (0-indexed: 0,1,2,4)

But there are overlaps and also adjacents.

The key is that the total set of edges is the union of:
- All cycle edges 
- All edges from capital cities to other cities

And since at most one edge exists between any pair, we must take the union.

So for any pair (i,j), there is an edge if:
- i and j are adjacent in the cycle, OR
- at least one of i or j is a capital

But for a pair (i,j) where i and j are adjacent and one is a capital, the edge exists (either from the cycle or from the capital - but we count it once).

The total cost is the sum of c[i]*c[j] over all pairs (i,j) that satisfy the above condition.

We can compute this as:
sum over all pairs (i,j) where i < j and (i,j) is an adjacent pair + sum over all pairs (i,j) where i < j, at least one is capital, and (i,j) is not an adjacent pair.

But this is complex.

Alternative approach:
Total cost = sum over all pairs (i,j) with i < j of c[i]*c[j] for all edges that exist.

The edges that exist are:
- All adjacent pairs (i,i+1) 
- All pairs (capital, non-capital) where the non-capital is not adjacent to the capital

Wait no. If there's a capital, it connects to every other city, including non-adjacent ones.

For any pair (x,y) where x is a capital and y is any other city, there is an edge between x and y.

For any adjacent pair (i,i+1), there is an edge between them.

But if both x and y are capitals and adjacent, then the edge exists (covered by either).

So the set of edges is:
- All edges between any capital and any other city (including capital-capital edges)
- All edges between non-capital adjacent cities

But wait, if two non-capitals are adjacent, there's a cycle edge between them.

If a non-capital is adjacent to a capital, there's a capital edge between them (which covers it).

If two non-capitals are not adjacent, there's no edge between them.

If two capitals are adjacent, there's a capital edge between them.

So finally, the total set of edges is:
1. All pairs (x,y) where x is a capital and y is any city ≠ x
2. All pairs (i,j) where i and j are adjacent and both are non-capitals

This makes sense because:
- For capital connections: every capital connects to every other city
- For non-capital adjacent pairs: if two non-capitals are adjacent, there's a cycle edge between them that is not covered by any capital connection (since neither is a capital)

For a pair of non-capitals that are not adjacent, or for a capital-non-capital pair where they are not adjacent, the capital connection covers it.

For adjacent non-capital pair, only the cycle edge covers it.

So total cost = 
sum of (c[x] * c[y] for each capital x and each city y ≠ x) + 
sum of (c[i] * c[j] for each adjacent pair (i,j) where both i and j are non-capitals)

- (2,3): 2 is capital, 3 is non-capital → skip
- (3,0): 3 is non-capital, 0 is non-capital → 2*2 = 4
Sum = 6+4 = 10

Total = 7 + 10 = 17 ✓

Perfect!

Wait, let me check the input again.

Input: "5 2\n3 5 2 2 4\n1 4"

Capitals are 1 and 4 (1-indexed), so in 0-indexed: 0 and 3 (cities 1 and 4)

c = [3,5,2,2,4]

I think I made a mistake in the non-capital cities.

Non-capitals are cities 2,3 (0-indexed: indices 2 and 3) only? 

Capitals are 1 and 4 (1-indexed) → indices 0 and 3 (0-indexed)

So non-capitals: indices 1,2,4

Adjacent pairs:
- (0,1): 0=capital, 1=non-capital → skip
- (1,2): 1=non-capital, 2=non-capital → 5*2 = 10
- (2,3): 2=non-capital, 3=capital → skip
- (3,4): 3=capital, 4=non-capital → skip
- (4,0): 4=non-capital, 0=capital → skip

Sum = 10

Capital edges: 
Capital at 0: connects to 1,2,3,4 (indices 1,2,3,4)
- (0,1): 3*5 = 15
- (0,2): 3*2 = 6
- (0,3): 3*2 = 6
- (0,4): 3*4 = 12
Sum = 39

Capital at 3: connects to 0,1,2,4 (indices 0,1,2,4)
- (3,0): 2*3 = 6
- (3,1): 2*5 = 10
- (3,2): 2*2 = 4
- (3,4): 2*4 = 8
Sum = 28

Total capital edges = 39+28 = 67

Total = 67+10 = 77

Expected is 71. 

I see the issue - the capital at 0 and capital at 3 are both capitals, so their edges might be counted twice? 

No, each capital connects to every other city, including each other.

But when we say "sum of c[x]*c[y] for capital x and y ≠ x", we are summing over all pairs, including (0,3).

In our calculation, for capital at 0: includes edge (0,3)
For capital at 3: includes edge (3,0) which is the same edge.

So we are double-counting the edge between two capitals.

Yes! That's the mistake.

In the first part, when we sum over all capital x and city y ≠ x, we are counting each edge between a capital and any other city, including between two capitals.

But each such edge is unique between two vertices, so when we have two capitals, the edge between them is counted twice: once from each capital.

We should only count it once.

For any pair of cities (x,y), if at least one is a capital, and if they are not adjacent (or they are adjacent), we have an edge.

But in our current approach, for non-adjacent pairs involving a capital, we count it once (from the capital), which is correct.

For adjacent pairs involving a capital, it's covered by the capital edge.

For the edge between two capitals, it should be counted only once.

In our sum over capital-x and y≠x, we are counting the edge (x,y) twice if both x and y are capitals.

So we need to adjust.

The correct sum for capital connections (without double counting) is:
Sum over all pairs (x,y) where at least one of x or y is a capital, and (x,y) is not an adjacent pair, plus the edges between capital and other cities that are adjacent.

Actually, simpler: the total cost of edges from capitals to other cities, with no double counting, is:
- Sum over all capital cities x of (c[x] * (sum of c[y] for all y ≠ x and y is not adjacent to x)) 
- Plus sum over all capital cities x of (c[x] * c[y] for all y adjacent to x) 
- But this is not right.

Actually, the edge between a capital x and any other city y exists, and we should count it once.

So the cost from capital edges is the sum over all pairs (x,y) where x is a capital and y is any city ≠ x, of c[x]*c[y].

This includes the edge between two capitals.

And since each such edge is between two specific cities, and we are summing over x (capital) and y (any other), each edge is counted once for each capital in the pair.

For an edge between two capitals, it is counted twice: once from each capital.

For an edge between a capital and a non-capital, it is counted once.

So the total cost from capital edges as we sum over x and y is:
- For capital-capital edge (x,y): counted twice
- For capital-non-capital edge (x,y): counted once

But in the final set, each edge should be counted once.

So we need to subtract the extra count of capital-capital edges.

Let me denote:
A = sum over all capital cities x, and all cities y ≠ x, of c[x]*c[y]
B = sum over all pairs of capital cities (x,y) where x < y of c[x]*c[y] (the edges between capital cities)

Then the actual cost from capital edges is A - B (because we double-counted the capital-capital edges)

Plus the cost from non-capital adjacent pairs: sum of c[i]*c[j] for adjacent non-capital pairs.

Total cost = (A - B) + C, where C is the sum for non-capital adjacent pairs.

Let me verify with example 1:
n=4, k=1, capitals=[2], c=[2,3,1,2]

A = capital at 2 connects to 0,1,3: 1*2 + 1*3 + 1*2 = 2+3+2 = 7
B = 0 (only one capital)
C = non-capital adjacent pairs: (0,1): 2*3=6, (3,0): 2*2=4 → sum=10
Total = 7 - 0 + 10 = 17 ✓

Example 2:
n=5, k=2, capitals=[0,3], c=[3,5,2,2,4]

A = sum over capital x, y≠x of c[x]*c[y]
Capital at 0: connects to 1,2,3,4: 3*5 + 3*2 + 3*2 + 3*4 = 15+6+6+12 = 39
Capital at 3: connects to 0,1,2,4: 2*3 + 2*5 + 2*2 + 2*4 = 6+10+4+8 = 28
A = 39+28 = 67

B = edge between capital 0 and 3: c[0]*c[3] = 3*2 = 6

C = non-capital adjacent pairs:
Non-capitals: 1,2,4
Adjacent pairs: 
- (0,1): 0=capital → skip
- (1,2): both non-capitals → 5*2 = 10
- (2,3): 3=capital → skip
- (3,4): 3=capital → skip
- (4,0): 0=capital → skip
C = 10

Total = (67 - 6) + 10 = 61 + 10 = 71 ✓

Perfect!

So the algorithm:
1. Calculate A = sum for each capital x of (c[x] * sum of c[y] for all y ≠ x)
2. Calculate B = sum of c[x]*c[y] for all pairs of capitals (x,y) with x < y
3. Calculate C = sum of c[i]*c[j] for all adjacent pairs (i,j) where both i and j are non-capitals
4. Answer = (A - B) + C

We can optimize A:
A = sum over all capital x of (c[x] * (total_sum - c[x]))
A = total_sum * (sum of c[x] for all capitals) - sum of (c[x]^2) for all capitals

And B = sum of c[i]*c[j] for all pairs of capitals (i,j) with i < j
B = (sum of c[x] for capitals)^2 - sum of (c[x]^2) for capitals) / 2

And C can be computed by iterating through adjacent pairs.

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = [x - 1 for x in capitals]

# Total sum of all beauty values
total_sum = sum(c)

# Sum of beauty values of all capital cities
cap_sum = sum(c[i] for i in capitals)

# Sum of squares of beauty values of capital cities
cap_sq_sum = sum(c[i] * c[i] for i in capitals)

# Calculate A = sum over all capital x of (c[x] * (total_sum - c[x]))
A = total_sum * cap_sum - cap_sq_sum

# Calculate B = sum of c[i]*c[j] for all pairs of capitals i < j
# B = (cap_sum^2 - cap_sq_sum) / 2
B = (cap_sum * cap_sum - cap_sq_sum) // 2

# Calculate C = sum of c[i]*c[j] for adjacent pairs where both i and j are non-capitals
C = 0
non_capitals = [i for i in range(n) if i not in capitals]
for i in range(n):
    j = (i + 1) % n
    if i not in capitals and j not in capitals:
        C += c[i] * c[j]

answer = A - B + C
print(answer)
```

Let me verify the examples.

Example 1: n=4, k=1, capitals=[2], c=[2,3,1,2]
total_sum = 8
cap_sum = 1
cap_sq_sum = 1
A = 8*1 - 1 = 7
B = (1*1 - 1)//2 = 0
C: non-capitals at 0,1,3
adjacent pairs: (0,1): both non-capitals → 2*3=6; (3,0): both non-capitals → 2*2=4; (1,2): 2 is capital; (2,3): 2 is capital
So (0,1) and (3,0) → C = 6+4 = 10
Answer = 7 - 0 + 10 = 17 ✓

Example 2: n=5, k=2, capitals=[0,3], c=[3,5,2,2,4]
total_sum = 3+5+2+2+4 = 16
cap_sum = 3+2 = 5
cap_sq_sum = 9+4 = 13
A = 16*5 - 13 = 80-13 = 67
B = (25 - 13)//2 = 12//2 = 6
C: non-capitals at 1,2,4
adjacent pairs: 
(0,1): 0 is capital → skip
(1,2): both non-capitals → 5*2 = 10
(2,3): 3 is capital → skip
(3,4): 3 is capital → skip
(4,0): 0 is capital → skip
C = 10
Answer = 67 - 6 + 10 = 71 ✓

Perfect!

Final code:

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = [x - 1 for x in capitals]

# Total sum of all beauty values
total_sum = sum(c)

# Sum of beauty values of all capital cities
cap_sum = sum(c[i] for i in capitals)

# Sum of squares of beauty values of capital cities
cap_sq_sum = sum(c[i] * c[i] for i in capitals)

# Calculate A = sum over all capital x of (c[x] * (total_sum - c[x]))
A = total_sum * cap_sum - cap_sq_sum

# Calculate B = sum of c[i]*c[j] for all pairs of capitals i < j
B = (cap_sum * cap_sum - cap_sq_sum) // 2

# Calculate C = sum of c[i]*c[j] for adjacent pairs where both i and j are non-capitals
C = 0
for i in range(n):
    j = (i + 1) % n
    if i not in capitals and j not in capitals:
        C += c[i] * c[j]

answer = A - B + C
print(answer)
```

Submitted Code

View Extracted Code 
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = [x - 1 for x in capitals]

# Total sum of all beauty values
total_sum = sum(c)

# Sum of beauty values of all capital cities
cap_sum = sum(c[i] for i in capitals)

# Sum of squares of beauty values of capital cities
cap_sq_sum = sum(c[i] * c[i] for i in capitals)

# Calculate A = sum over all capital x of (c[x] * (total_sum - c[x]))
A = total_sum * cap_sum - cap_sq_sum

# Calculate B = sum of c[i]*c[j] for all pairs of capitals i < j
B = (cap_sum * cap_sum - cap_sq_sum) // 2

# Calculate C = sum of c[i]*c[j] for adjacent pairs where both i and j are non-capitals
C = 0
for i in range(n):
    j = (i + 1) % n
    if i not in capitals and j not in capitals:
        C += c[i] * c[j]

answer = A - B + C
print(answer)

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Little Mishka is a great traveller and she visited many countries. After thinking about where to travel this time, she chose XXX — beautiful, but little-known northern country.

Here are some interesting facts about XXX:

  1. XXX consists of n cities, k of whose (just imagine!) are capital cities. 
  2. All of cities in the country are beautiful, but each is beautiful in its own way. Beauty value of i-th city equals to ci. 
  3. All the cities are consecutively connected by the roads, including 1-st and n-th city, forming a cyclic route 1 — 2 — ... — n — 1. Formally, for every 1 ≤ i < n there is a road between i-th and i + 1-th city, and another one between 1-st and n-th city. 
  4. Each capital city is connected with each other city directly by the roads. Formally, if city x is a capital city, then for every 1 ≤ i ≤ n, i ≠ x, there is a road between cities x and i. 
  5. There is at most one road between any two cities. 
  6. Price of passing a road directly depends on beauty values of cities it connects. Thus if there is a road between cities i and j, price of passing it equals ci·cj.



Mishka started to gather her things for a trip, but didn't still decide which route to follow and thus she asked you to help her determine summary price of passing each of the roads in XXX. Formally, for every pair of cities a and b (a < b), such that there is a road between a and b you are to find sum of products ca·cb. Will you help her?

Input

The first line of the input contains two integers n and k (3 ≤ n ≤ 100 000, 1 ≤ k ≤ n) — the number of cities in XXX and the number of capital cities among them.

The second line of the input contains n integers c1, c2, ..., cn (1 ≤ ci ≤ 10 000) — beauty values of the cities.

The third line of the input contains k distinct integers id1, id2, ..., idk (1 ≤ idi ≤ n) — indices of capital cities. Indices are given in ascending order.

Output

Print the only integer — summary price of passing each of the roads in XXX.

Examples

Input

4 1
2 3 1 2
3


Output

17

Input

5 2
3 5 2 2 4
1 4


Output

71

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` cities arranged in a cycle: 1-2-...-n-1 (so city 1 is connected to city n)
2. There are `k` capital cities. Each capital city is directly connected to ALL other cities
3. The price of passing a road between cities i and j is `ci * cj`
4. We need to find the sum of prices for all roads in the network

## Key Insights

1. **Base cyclic connections**: For any cycle of `n` cities, there are exactly `n` edges: (1,2), (2,3), ..., (n-1,n), (n,1)

2. **Capital connections**: Each capital city is connected to every other city. So if city `x` is a capital, it has connections to all cities `i` where `i ≠ x`.

3. **No duplicate edges**: The problem states there's at most one road between any two cities, so we don't double count.

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Identify all roads
The roads come from two sources:
- The cyclic connections: (i, i+1) for i from 1 to n-1, and (n, 1)
- The capital connections: for each capital city x, it connects to every city i where i ≠ x

### Step 2: Calculate the total price
We need to sum `ci * cj` for all pairs (i,j) that have a road between them.

### Step 3: Handle the capital connections
For each capital city at position `x`, it connects to all other cities. The contribution to the total price is:
- For each other city `i`: `cx * ci`

So the total contribution from capital city `x` is: `cx * (sum of all ci where i ≠ x)`

### Step 4: Handle the cyclic connections
The cyclic connections contribute: `c1*c2 + c2*c3 + ... + cn*c1`

### Step 5: Combine both contributions

Let's work through the first example to verify:
- n=4, k=1, cities have beauty [2,3,1,2], capital at city 3 (index 3, beauty=1)
- Cyclic connections: (1,2): 2*3=6, (2,3): 3*1=3, (3,4): 1*2=2, (4,1): 2*2=4 → sum = 6+3+2+4 = 15
- Capital connections: city 3 connects to cities 1,2,4: 1*2 + 1*3 + 1*2 = 2+3+2 = 7
- Total: 15 + 7 = 22... but expected is 17.

Wait, let me reconsider. The capital connections are already part of the network. Let me analyze the problem again.

Looking at the problem statement more carefully: "each capital city is connected with each other city directly by the roads". This means we add additional roads.

But in the cyclic structure, there's already a road between consecutive cities and between 1 and n. The capital connections add additional roads.

Let me recalculate the first example:
- Cities: 1(2), 2(3), 3(1), 4(2)
- Cyclic connections: (1,2): 2*3=6, (2,3): 3*1=3, (3,4): 1*2=2, (4,1): 2*2=4 → sum = 15
- Capital at city 3: connects to cities 1,2,4 → roads: (3,1): 1*2=2, (3,2): 1*3=3, (3,4): 1*2=2
- But (3,4) is already in the cyclic connections? Actually, the cyclic connection (3,4) already exists, so we don't add it again.

The problem says "there is at most one road between any two cities", so we shouldn't double count.

So if city 3 is a capital, it connects to all other cities, BUT if that road already exists in the cyclic structure, we only count it once.

In our cyclic structure: (1,2), (2,3), (3,4), (4,1)
The capital connection to city 3 would add: (3,1), (3,2), (3,4). But (3,4) already exists.

So we need to be careful about duplicates.

Actually, let me read the problem again: "each capital city is connected with each other city directly by the roads" - this suggests we add these connections regardless.

But condition 5 says "there is at most one road between any two cities", so if a road already exists, it's just one instance.

Therefore, the total roads are:
- The n cyclic edges
- Plus additional edges from capital city to all others, EXCEPT for edges that already exist in the cyclic structure.

This is getting complex. Let me think differently.

Actually, the problem says: "formally, for every 1 ≤ i < n there is a road between i-th and i+1-th city, and another one between 1-st and n-th city" - so basic cycle.

"Each capital city is connected with each other city directly by the roads" - meaning we add direct roads from each capital to every other city.

But condition 5 says "there is at most one road between any two cities", so if a road already exists in the cycle, we don't create a duplicate.

So the total roads are:
- All edges in the cycle
- All edges from capitals to other cities that are NOT already in the cycle

But wait - the capital city connections might create edges that are already in the cycle (like between consecutive cities or between city and n).

In the cycle, city i is connected to i+1 and to 1 (if i=n).

So for a capital city at position x, it would be connected to:
- x-1 (if x>1)
- x+1 (if x<n)
- 1 (if x≠1) 
- n (if x≠n)

These connections are already in the cycle.

Therefore, when we add capital connections, we only add edges to cities that are not adjacent to x in the cycle.

But this seems overly complex. Let me verify with the first example:

n=4, k=1, c=[2,3,1,2], capital at city 3 (index 3, beauty 1)

Cyclic connections: (1,2): 2*3=6, (2,3): 3*1=3, (3,4): 1*2=2, (4,1): 2*2=4 → sum = 15

Capital at city 3: should connect to cities 1,2,4.

Price of (3,1): 1*2 = 2

Total: 15 + 2 = 17 ✓

This matches!

So algorithm:
1. Calculate sum from cyclic edges
2. For each capital city x, find all cities that it is NOT adjacent to in the cycle, and add the products of their beauty values

For city x in a cycle of n cities:
- Adjacent cities: (x-1) mod n and (x+1) mod n
- So city x is NOT adjacent to all other cities except those two
- So it adds edges to (n - 2) other cities

But we need to be careful about indices (1-based).

For a capital at position x (1-based):
- Adjacent positions: (x-1) if x>1 else n, and (x+1) if x<n else 1
- So non-adjacent: all other cities except these two

So for capital at x, sum of beauty products = cx * (sum of ci for all i except the two adjacent ones)

Wait, no - the road is between x and i, so price is cx * ci.

So total contribution = cx * (sum of ci for all cities i that are not adjacent to x)

The cities adjacent to x are:
- (x-1) if x > 1, else n
- (x+1) if x < n, else 1

So we need to exclude these two when summing.

Therefore:
Total sum = (sum of cyclic edges) + (sum over all capitals of cx * (sum of ci for all i not adjacent to x))

Let me implement this.

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Convert to 0-based indexing
capitals = [x - 1 for x in capitals]

# Calculate sum from cyclic edges
cyclic_sum = 0
for i in range(n):
    j = (i + 1) % n
    cyclic_sum += c[i] * c[j]

# Calculate additional edges from capitals
additional_sum = 0
total_sum = sum(c)

for capital in capitals:
    # Find adjacent cities to this capital (0-based)
    # Left neighbor
    left = (capital - 1) % n
    # Right neighbor
    right = (capital + 1) % n
    
    # Sum of beauties of non-adjacent cities
    non_adjacent_sum = total_sum - c[capital] - c[left] - c[right]
    
    # Add cx * (sum of ci for non-adjacent i)
    additional_sum += c[capital] * non_adjacent_sum

result = cyclic_sum + additional_sum
print(result)
```

n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Convert to 0-based indexing
capitals = [x - 1 for x in capitals]

# Calculate sum from cyclic edges
cyclic_sum = 0
for i in range(n):
    j = (i + 1) % n
    cyclic_sum += c[i] * c[j]

# Calculate additional edges from capitals
additional_sum = 0
total_sum = sum(c)

for capital in capitals:
    # Find adjacent cities to this capital (0-based)
    left = (capital - 1) % n
    right = (capital + 1) % n
    
    # Sum of beauties of non-adjacent cities (excluding self, left, right)
    non_adjacent_sum = total_sum - c[capital] - c[left] - c[right]
    
    # Add cx * (sum of ci for non-adjacent i)
    additional_sum += c[capital] * non_adjacent_sum

result = cyclic_sum + additional_sum
print(result)
