You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Little Mishka is a great traveller and she visited many countries. After thinking about where to travel this time, she chose XXX — beautiful, but little-known northern country.

Here are some interesting facts about XXX:

  1. XXX consists of n cities, k of whose (just imagine!) are capital cities. 
  2. All of cities in the country are beautiful, but each is beautiful in its own way. Beauty value of i-th city equals to ci. 
  3. All the cities are consecutively connected by the roads, including 1-st and n-th city, forming a cyclic route 1 — 2 — ... — n — 1. Formally, for every 1 ≤ i < n there is a road between i-th and i + 1-th city, and another one between 1-st and n-th city. 
  4. Each capital city is connected with each other city directly by the roads. Formally, if city x is a capital city, then for every 1 ≤ i ≤ n, i ≠ x, there is a road between cities x and i. 
  5. There is at most one road between any two cities. 
  6. Price of passing a road directly depends on beauty values of cities it connects. Thus if there is a road between cities i and j, price of passing it equals ci·cj.



Mishka started to gather her things for a trip, but didn't still decide which route to follow and thus she asked you to help her determine summary price of passing each of the roads in XXX. Formally, for every pair of cities a and b (a < b), such that there is a road between a and b you are to find sum of products ca·cb. Will you help her?

Input

The first line of the input contains two integers n and k (3 ≤ n ≤ 100 000, 1 ≤ k ≤ n) — the number of cities in XXX and the number of capital cities among them.

The second line of the input contains n integers c1, c2, ..., cn (1 ≤ ci ≤ 10 000) — beauty values of the cities.

The third line of the input contains k distinct integers id1, id2, ..., idk (1 ≤ idi ≤ n) — indices of capital cities. Indices are given in ascending order.

Output

Print the only integer — summary price of passing each of the roads in XXX.

Examples

Input

4 1
2 3 1 2
3


Output

17

Input

3 5 2 2 4
1 4


Output

71

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` cities arranged in a cycle: 1-2-...-n-1
2. There are `k` capital cities
3. Every capital city is directly connected to every other city (including non-capitals)
4. Additionally, there are the cyclic connections: (1,2), (2,3), ..., (n-1,n), (n,1)
5. The price of passing a road between cities i and j is `c[i] * c[j]`
6. We need to find the sum of prices for all roads

## Analysis

The roads consist of two types:
1. **Cyclic roads**: (1,2), (2,3), ..., (n-1,n), (n,1)
2. **Capital connections**: For each capital city x, it has direct connections to all other cities (except itself)

## Important Observations

1. The cyclic roads are fixed: we have exactly n roads in the cycle
2. For capital connections: if we have capital cities, each capital city connects to all other n-1 cities
3. However, we need to be careful about double counting - a road between two capital cities would be counted twice if we just sum all capital connections

Let me think about this more systematically:

The total sum = sum of all cyclic roads + sum of all capital-to-other roads

But wait - the problem states: "each capital city is connected with each other city directly" - this means for each capital city x, there's a direct road from x to every other city i (i ≠ x).

So the total sum should be:
- Sum of products for all adjacent pairs in the cycle: (c[1]×c[2] + c[2]×c[3] + ... + c[n-1]×c[n] + c[n]×c[1])
- Sum of products for all capital-to-other connections: for each capital city x, sum of c[x]×c[i] for all i ≠ x

However, if two cities are both capitals and we have a direct road between them, this will be counted in both the capital connections and potentially in the cycle if they're adjacent.

But actually, the problem says "each capital city is connected with each other city directly" - this means we have direct roads between every capital and every other city. This includes connections between capitals.

And we also have the cyclic connections.

So the total sum is:
- Sum of prices for all cyclic edges (edges between consecutive cities in the cycle)
- Sum of prices for all direct edges between capital cities and other cities (including edges between capitals)

But we have to be careful not to double count or miss anything.

Since each edge is defined by a pair (a,b) where a < b, and we're summing c[a]×c[b] for every such edge that exists.

The edges that exist are:
1. The cyclic edges: (1,2), (2,3), ..., (n-1,n), (n,1)
2. The capital edges: for each capital city x, edges (x,i) for all i ≠ x

For the capital edges, since we're summing over pairs (a,b) where a < b, we need to be careful about ordering.

## Better Approach

Let's calculate the total sum as:

Total = (sum of products of adjacent cities in cycle) + (sum of products of capital city with every other city excluding itself)

For the capital part: for each capital city x, we add c[x] × c[i] for all i ≠ x.

But if we just sum this for all capitals, we'll be counting each capital-to-other edge exactly once (when we process the capital city).

So:
- Cyclic sum: sum_{i=1 to n} c[i] × c[(i mod n) + 1] (with proper indexing)
- Capital sum: for each capital city x, sum_{j ≠ x} c[x] × c[j]

Actually, the capital sum can be rewritten as: for each capital city x, c[x] × (sum of all c[j] for j ≠ x)

So capital sum = sum_{x in capitals} c[x] × (total_sum - c[x])

where total_sum = sum of all c[i]

5. Add both sums together

Alternative: since each edge (x,y) where x is capital and y is not adjacent to x in the cycle, and we want to sum c[x]*c[y] for all such edges, but each edge is counted once for each capital that has a connection to it, and we want it only once.

But wait, for an edge (x,y) where x and y are both capitals and not adjacent, this edge is counted in both x's sum and y's sum, so it appears twice.

This is the problem.

For an edge between two capitals that are not adjacent in the cycle, it will be counted twice in the above sum (once from each capital).

But in reality, there should be only one such edge.

Therefore, I need to subtract the double-counted edges.

The double-counted edges are edges (x,y) where:
- x and y are both capitals
- x and y are not adjacent in the cycle
- and the edge (x,y) is not present in the cycle

For such an edge, it appears twice in the sum sum_{x in capitals} c[x] * (sum_{y not adjacent to x} c[y]), but should appear only once.

So I need to subtract the sum of c[x]*c[y] for all pairs of capitals (x,y) where x and y are not adjacent in the cycle.

Therefore, the correct additional capital connection sum is:

sum_{x in capitals} c[x] * (total_sum - c[x] - sum of c[adjacent cities of x]) - sum_{x<y, both capital, not adjacent in cycle} c[x]*c[y]

But this is complicated.

Let me define:
- Let S1 = sum_{x in capitals} c[x] * (total_sum - c[x] - sum of c[adjacent cities of x])
- Let S2 = sum_{x<y, x,y in capitals, and x,y not adjacent in cycle} c[x]*c[y]

Then the actual capital connection sum is S1 - S2

And total sum = cyclic_sum + (S1 - S2)

Let me verify with example 1:
- n=4, c=[2,3,1,2], capital=[3] (index 2)
- total_sum = 8
- For x=2 (value 1): adjacent cities = c[1]=3, c[3]=2, sum = 5

- Capital connection sum = 2
- Cyclic sum = 15
- Total = 17 ✓

  - So (0,3): city 0 and 3 are not adjacent (adjacent pairs are (0,1), (0,4), (1,2), (2,3), (3,4))
  - So (0,3) is not adjacent, so include
- S2 = c[0]*c[3] = 3*2 = 6
- Capital connection sum = S1 - S2 = 28 - 6 = 22
- Cyclic sum = 49
- Total = 49 + 22 = 71 ✓

So the algorithm is:

1. Read input
2. Calculate total_sum
3. Calculate cyclic_sum = sum of c[i] * c[(i+1)%n] for i in 0 to n-1
4. For each capital city x (0-indexed):
   - Calculate sum_adj = c[(x-1)%n] + c[(x+1)%n]
   - Add c[x] * (total_sum - c[x] - sum_adj) to S1
5. For each pair of distinct capital cities (i,j) with i < j:
   - Check if they are adjacent in the cycle (i.e., |i-j| == 1 or (i=0 and j=n-1) or (j=0 and i=n-1))
   - If not adjacent, add c[i] * c[j] to S2
6. Capital connection sum = S1 - S2
7. Total sum = cyclic_sum + (S1 - S2)

Note: two cities are adjacent in the cycle if they are consecutive indices or (0 and n-1).

For i and j (0-indexed), they are adjacent if:
- |i - j| == 1, or
- (i == 0 and j == n-1), or
- (j == 0 and i == n-1)

But since we're checking i < j, and we only do it once per pair, we can check:
- if (i+1 == j) or (j+1 == i) or (i == 0 and j == n-1)

Actually, for indices i and j with i < j:
- They are adjacent if (j == i+1) or (j == n-1 and i == 0)

So in code:

```python
# Read input
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Convert to 0-indexed
capitals = [x - 1 for x in capitals]

# Calculate total sum
total_sum = sum(c)

# Calculate cyclic sum
cyclic_sum = 0
for i in range(n):
    cyclic_sum += c[i] * c[(i + 1) % n]

# S1: sum over capitals of c[x] * (total_sum - c[x] - sum of adjacent beauty values)
S1 = 0
for x in capitals:
    # adjacent indices: (x-1) % n and (x+1) % n
    left = (x - 1) % n
    right = (x + 1) % n
    sum_adj = c[left] + c[right]
    S1 += c[x] * (total_sum - c[x] - sum_adj)

# S2: sum of c[i]*c[j] for all pairs of capitals (i,j) with i < j and not adjacent
S2 = 0
capital_list = sorted(capitals)  # already sorted, but just in case
m = len(capitals)
for i in range(m):
    for j in range(i + 1, m):
        # Check if i and j are adjacent in cycle
        if (j == i + 1) or (i == 0 and j == n - 1) or (j == 0 and i == n - 1):
            # actually, since i < j, and j >= i+1
            # they are adjacent if j == i+1 or (i==0 and j==n-1)
            if j == i + 1 or (i == 0 and j == n - 1):
                continue
            else:
                S2 += c[capital_list[i]] * c[capital_list[j]]
        else:
            S2 += c[capital_list[i]] * c[capital_list[j]]

# Wait, I think I made a mistake here.
# If they are not adjacent, we add the product.
# So if they are adjacent, we skip (don't add)
# If they are not adjacent, we add.

# Actually, I want to add c[i]*c[j] only if they are not adjacent.

# So:
# S2 = sum for all pairs (i,j) with i<j, both in capitals, and not adjacent
# So: for each pair, if not adjacent, add c[i]*c[j]

S2 = 0
capital_list = capitals
m = len(capital_list)
for i in range(m):
    for j in range(i + 1, m):
        # Check if they are adjacent in cycle
        # i and j are adjacent if |i-j| == 1 or (i==0 and j==n-1) or (j==0 and i==n-1)
        # with i < j
        if j == i + 1 or (i == 0 and j == n - 1):
            # adjacent, skip
            continue
        else:
            S2 += c[capital_list[i]] * c[capital_list[j]]

# Capital connection sum = S1 - S2
capital_sum = S1 - S2

total_sum = cyclic_sum + capital_sum

print(total_sum)
```

But note: the adjacent check for i and j is: they are adjacent if they are consecutive in the cycle, specifically if they are next to each other.

For example, city 0 and city 1 are adjacent, city n-1 and city 0 are adjacent.

So for indices i and j with i < j:
- They are adjacent if j == i + 1 OR (i == 0 and j == n-1)

Yes.

Let me verify with example 2:
- capitals: [0,3] (0-indexed)
- pairs: only (0,3)
- are they adjacent? 3 == 0+1? 3==1? no. i=0, j=3, so (i==0 and j==n-1)= (0 and 4)? n=5, n-1=4, j=3≠4, so no
- So they are not adjacent, so add c[0]*c[3] = 3*2 = 6
- S2 = 6
- S1 = 28
- capital_sum = 28 - 6 = 22
- cyclic_sum = 49
- total = 49 + 22 = 71 ✓

Perfect.

The time complexity is O(k^2) which is acceptable since k ≤ n ≤ 100,000, but worst case k=100,000, then k^2 = 10^10 which is too slow.

We need to optimize.

We can compute S2 without O(k^2).

Total sum of products for all pairs of capitals = (sum of c[x] for x in capitals)^2 - sum of c[x]^2) / 2

But we want only the pairs that are not adjacent.

So S2 = (sum of c[x]^2 for x in capitals) / 2 + something? No.

Let me define:
- Let A = sum of c[x] for x in capitals
- Let B = sum of c[x]^2 for x in capitals
- Let C = sum of c[i]*c[j] for i < j, i,j in capitals = (A^2 - B) / 2

Then S2 = C - (sum of c[i]*c[j] for adjacent pairs of capitals)

So we can compute:
- total_pair_sum = (A^2 - B) / 2
- adjacent_pair_sum = sum of c[i]*c[j] for adjacent pairs (i,j) with i<j and adjacent in cycle
- S2 = total_pair_sum - adjacent_pair_sum

This is better.

Steps:
1. Calculate A = sum of c[x] for x in capitals
2. Calculate B = sum of c[x]^2 for x in capitals
3. total_pair_sum = (A*A - B) // 2
4. adjacent_pair_sum = 0
   - For each adjacent pair of capitals (i,j) with i < j and adjacent in cycle:
     - if j == i+1 or (i == 0 and j == n-1), then add c[i]*c[j]
5. S2 = total_pair_sum - adjacent_pair_sum

This is O(k), which is acceptable.

Let me verify with example 2:
- capitals: [0,3], values: [3,2]
- A = 3+2 = 5
- B = 3^2 + 2^2 = 9+4 = 13
- total_pair_sum = (25 - 13) // 2 = 12 // 2 = 6
- adjacent_pair_sum: pair (0,3)
  - j=3, i=0: is 3 == 0+1? 3==1? no. i==0 and j==n-1? n-1=4, j=3≠4? no. So not adjacent → adjacent_pair_sum = 0
- S2 = 6 - 0 = 6 ✓

Example 1: only one capital, so no pairs → S2=0 ✓

So the final code:

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = [x - 1 for x in capitals]  # 0-indexed

total_sum = sum(c)

# Cyclic sum: sum of c[i] * c[(i+1) % n] for i in range(n)
cyclic_sum = 0
for i in range(n):
    cyclic_sum += c[i] * c[(i + 1) % n]

# S1: sum over capitals of c[x] * (total_sum - c[x] - sum of adjacent beauty values)
S1 = 0
for x in capitals:
    left = (x - 1) % n
    right = (x + 1) % n
    sum_adj = c[left] + c[right]
    S1 += c[x] * (total_sum - c[x] - sum_adj)

# S2: sum of c[i]*c[j] for all pairs of capitals (i,j) with i < j and not adjacent
# First, total sum of c[i]*c[j] for all pairs i<j in capitals
A = sum(c[x] for x in capitals)
B = sum(c[x] * c[x] for x in capitals)
total_pair_sum = (A * A - B) // 2

# Now subtract the sum for adjacent pairs in capitals
adjacent_pair_sum = 0
capital_set = set(capitals)
# Generate all pairs of capitals with i < j
capital_list = sorted(capitals)
m = len(capital_list)
for i in range(m):
    for j in range(i + 1, m):
        # Check if they are adjacent in cycle
        if j == i + 1 or (i == 0 and j == n - 1):
            adjacent_pair_sum += c[capital_list[i]] * c[capital_list[j]]

S2 = total_pair_sum - adjacent_pair_sum

capital_sum = S1 - S2
result = cyclic_sum + capital_sum
print(result)
```

But still O(k^2) in the worst case.

Can we avoid the nested loop for adjacent pairs?

Actually, since k can be large, and we only have at most O(k) pairs, but in worst case k=100,000, then k^2 is 10^10, which is too slow in Python.

We need to find adjacent pairs without nested loops.

The adjacent pairs in the capital set are:
- Consecutive indices in the cycle
- The pair (0, n-1)

So we need to check if any two capitals are at distance 1 in the cycle or are (0 and n-1).

For each capital, we can check if its adjacent cities are also capitals.

For example, for city x, its adjacent cities are (x-1) and (x+1). If either of them is also a capital, then we have a pair.

But we only want pairs (i,j) with i < j.

We can:
- For each capital x, for each adjacent city y in the cycle, if y is also a capital and y > x, then add c[x]*c[y]
- Also check if (0, n-1) are both capitals and 0 < n-1

But note: (x-1, x) is not covered if we only go in one direction.

Actually, the adjacent pairs are:
- (i, i+1) for i in 0 to n-2
- (n-1, 0)

So we can iterate over the cycle and check if both i and i+1 are in the capital set, and then add c[i]*c[i+1].

Also, if both 0 and n-1 are in the capital set, add c[0]*c[n-1].

But note: if both i and i+1 are capitals, then for i < i+1, we have a pair (i, i+1) which we want to exclude.

So algorithm for adjacent_pair_sum:
1. Create a set of capitals for O(1) lookup
2. adjacent_pair_sum = 0
3. For i in range(n):
   - j = (i+1) % n
   - if i in capitals and j in capitals:
     - if i < j, then add c[i] * c[j]
4. Also, if 0 and n-1 are in capitals, then add c[0] * c[n-1]

But in step 3, when i goes from 0 to n-1, and j = (i+1)%n, then (i,j) with i<j only when j > i or (j=0 and i=n-1).

For consecutive pairs: (i, i+1) with i < i+1, which is always true for i < n-1.
For (n-1, 0): when i=n-1, j=0, and i > j (since n-1 > 0), so we won't add when i < j.

So we need to handle (n-1,0) separately.

So:
- adjacent_pair_sum = 0
- For i in range(n):
  - j = (i+1) % n
  - if i in capitals and j in capitals:
    - if i < j:
      - adjacent_pair_sum += c[i] * c[j]
- Also, if 0 in capitals and (n-1) in capitals and 0 < n-1:
  - adjacent_pair_sum += c[0] * c[n-1]

Actually, the pair (n-1,0) is not included in the above when i=n-1 and j=0, because i=n-1 and j=0, and n-1 > 0 (if n>2), so i>j.

So we need to add it manually.

But when i=0, j=1, we add if 0 and 1 in capitals, and 0<1.
When i=1, j=2, add if both in capitals and 1<2.
...
When i=n-2, j=n-1, add if both in capitals and n-2 < n-1.
When i=n-1, j=0, we skip because n-1 > 0.

So we only miss the pair (n-1,0) when both are capitals.

Therefore, we add:
- if 0 in capitals and (n-1) in capitals, then add c[0] * c[n-1] (since 0 < n-1 is true for n>2)

But wait, in the input n>=3, so n-1>0.

So code:

```python
adjacent_pair_sum = 0
capital_set = set(capitals)

# Check consecutive pairs in cycle
for i in range(n):
    j = (i + 1) % n
    if i in capital_set and j in capital_set:
        if i < j:
            adjacent_pair_sum += c[i] * c[j]

# Add the pair (0, n-1) if both are capitals
if 0 in capital_set and (n-1) in capital_set:
    adjacent_pair_sum += c[0] * c[n-1]
```

Let me verify with example 2:
- capitals: [0,3]
- i=0, j=1: 0 in set, 1 not in set → skip
- i=1, j=2: 1 not in set → skip
- i=2, j=3: 2 not in set → skip
- i=3, j=4: 3 in set, 4 not in set → skip
- i=4, j=0: 4 not in set → skip
- Then add (0,4)? n-1=4, capital_set has 0 and 3, 4 not in set → skip
- So adjacent_pair_sum = 0 ✓

Example: suppose capitals are 0,1,4 (n=5)
- Then pairs: (0,1) and (4,0) which is (4,0)
- (0,1): i=0, j=1, both in set, i<j → add c[0]*c[1]
- (4,0): i=4, j=0, i>j (4>0), so skip in loop
- Then add (0,4): 0 and 4 in set → add c[0]*c[4]
- So adjacent_pair_sum = c[0]*c[1] + c[0]*c[4]

Is that correct? The adjacent pairs are:
- (0,1): consecutive
- (4,0): consecutive (since 4-1=3, 3+1=4? No, in cycle, 4 is adjacent to 3 and 0)
- So (4,0) is an adjacent pair with 4<0? No, 4>0
- So we should have (0,1) and (4,0) with 4 and 0.

Since (4,0) has i=4, j=0, i>j, so we skip in the loop, and then we add (0,4) at the end.

But when we add (0,4), we are adding the pair where i=0, j=4, and 0<4 is true, so we should add it.

Yes, and the pair (0,4) is the same as (4,0).

So this is correct.

But note: in the loop, when i=4, j=0, we have i=4, j=0, and 4>0, so we skip.

Then we add (0,4) separately.

But 0 is in set and 4 is in set, and 0<4, so we add c[0]*c[4].

The pair (4,0) is the same as (0,4) in the sum.

So it's correct.

Therefore, final code:

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals_input = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = [x - 1 for x in capitals_input]  # convert to 0-indexed

total_sum = sum(c)

# Cyclic sum: edges between consecutive cities
cyclic_sum = 0
for i in range(n):
    cyclic_sum += c[i] * c[(i + 1) % n]

# S1: contribution from capital connections for each capital, excluding adjacent cities
S1 = 0
for x in capitals:
    left = (x - 1) % n
    right = (x + 1) % n
    sum_adj = c[left] + c[right]
    S1 += c[x] * (total_sum - c[x] - sum_adj)

# S2: sum of c[i]*c[j] for pairs of capitals that are adjacent in the cycle
# Total sum of products for all pairs of capitals
A = sum(c[x] for x in capitals)
B = sum(c[x] * c[x] for x in capitals)
total_pair_sum = (A * A - B) // 2

# Adjacent pairs in the cycle
capital_set = set(capitals)
adjacent_pair_sum = 0

# Check all consecutive pairs in cycle (i, (i+1)%n)
for i in range(n):
    j = (i + 1) % n
    if i in capital_set and j in capital_set:
        if i < j:
            adjacent_pair_sum += c[i] * c[j]

# Add the pair (0, n-1) if both are capitals
if 0 in capital_set and (n - 1) in capital_set:
    adjacent_pair_sum += c[0] * c[n - 1]

# The actual capital connection sum is S1 - adjacent_pair_sum
capital_sum = S1 - adjacent_pair_sum
result = cyclic_sum + capital_sum
print(result)
```

This should be efficient with O(n + k).

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Little Mishka is a great traveller and she visited many countries. After thinking about where to travel this time, she chose XXX — beautiful, but little-known northern country.

Here are some interesting facts about XXX:

  1. XXX consists of n cities, k of whose (just imagine!) are capital cities. 
  2. All of cities in the country are beautiful, but each is beautiful in its own way. Beauty value of i-th city equals to ci. 
  3. All the cities are consecutively connected by the roads, including 1-st and n-th city, forming a cyclic route 1 — 2 — ... — n — 1. Formally, for every 1 ≤ i < n there is a road between i-th and i + 1-th city, and another one between 1-st and n-th city. 
  4. Each capital city is connected with each other city directly by the roads. Formally, if city x is a capital city, then for every 1 ≤ i ≤ n, i ≠ x, there is a road between cities x and i. 
  5. There is at most one road between any two cities. 
  6. Price of passing a road directly depends on beauty values of cities it connects. Thus if there is a road between cities i and j, price of passing it equals ci·cj.



Mishka started to gather her things for a trip, but didn't still decide which route to follow and thus she asked you to help her determine summary price of passing each of the roads in XXX. Formally, for every pair of cities a and b (a < b), such that there is a road between a and b you are to find sum of products ca·cb. Will you help her?

Input

The first line of the input contains two integers n and k (3 ≤ n ≤ 100 000, 1 ≤ k ≤ n) — the number of cities in XXX and the number of capital cities among them.

The second line of the input contains n integers c1, c2, ..., cn (1 ≤ ci ≤ 10 000) — beauty values of the cities.

The third line of the input contains k distinct integers id1, id2, ..., idk (1 ≤ idi ≤ n) — indices of capital cities. Indices are given in ascending order.

Output

Print the only integer — summary price of passing each of the roads in XXX.

Examples

Input

4 1
2 3 1 2
3


Output

17

Input

5 2
3 5 2 2 4
1 4


Output

71

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` cities arranged in a cycle: 1-2-...-n-1
2. There are `k` capital cities
3. Every capital city is directly connected to EVERY other city (including non-capitals)
4. The cycle roads exist between consecutive cities (i and i+1) and between city n and city 1
5. The price of passing a road between cities i and j is ci × cj
6. We need to find the total sum of prices for all roads

## Road Types

There are two types of roads:
1. **Cycle roads**: Between consecutive cities (i, i+1) and (n, 1)
2. **Capital connections**: Each capital city connects to every other city

## Key Insight

We need to be careful about double counting. When we add all connections from capital cities, we might be counting some roads multiple times.

Let's analyze the connections:

1. **Cycle roads**: We have exactly n cycle roads: (1,2), (2,3), ..., (n-1,n), (n,1)
2. **Capital connections**: Each capital city connects to every other city, but we need to avoid double counting

Actually, rereading the problem: "All the cities are consecutively connected by the roads, including 1-st and n-th city" and "Each capital city is connected with each other city directly".

- The standard cycle connections (consecutive cities)

## Approach

1. First, add all cycle roads: between (i, i+1) for i=1 to n-1, and (n,1)
2. For each capital city x, add roads from x to every other city y where y ≠ x, but only if that road doesn't already exist in the cycle

In fact, in the base cycle, only adjacent pairs have roads. So for any non-adjacent pair (x,y), we have a direct road from capital x to y.

So we can think of it as:
- Sum of products for all adjacent pairs in the cycle (i, i+1) and (n,1)
- Plus sum of products for all pairs (capital x, city y) where y ≠ x and (x,y) are not adjacent in the cycle

But there's "at most one road between any two cities", so if there's already a road between x and y in the cycle, we shouldn't add it again.

So we need:
1. Base cycle roads: (1,2), (2,3), ..., (n-1,n), (n,1)
2. Additional roads from capital cities to non-adjacent cities

- If they are adjacent in the cycle (i and i+1, or n and 1), there's a road with price c_i * c_j
- If i is a capital and j is any other city, and (i,j) are not adjacent in the cycle, there's an additional road with price c_i * c_j

But wait - if j is adjacent to i in the cycle, then the cycle already has that road, so we don't add it again.

Actually, let me think about what exactly the roads are:
- The cycle defines roads between consecutive cities: (1,2), (2,3), ..., (n-1,n), (n,1)
- For each capital city x, there are roads from x to every other city y ≠ x

Since "at most one road", if (x,y) is already a cycle road, we don't add it again.

But the problem says "each capital city is connected with each other city directly" - this suggests that additional roads exist. Given the constraint, it means that for each capital x, for every y ≠ x, there is a road x-y, unless x-y is already in the cycle (but that would be adjacent pairs).

Actually, I think we have all edges from a capital to all other vertices, AND the cycle edges. But since edges are unique, we only have each edge once.

So total edges = cycle edges + (edges from capital to non-adjacent vertices)

But more precisely: for each capital city x, we add edges (x,y) for all y ≠ x, except when (x,y) is already in the cycle (i.e., y = x±1 mod n).

However, the cycle edges already exist, so when we add capital connections, we only add edges that are not already in the cycle.

But actually, the problem might be simpler: the problem states that there are the cycle roads and the capital connections, and we need the sum of products for all roads.

Let me verify with the first example:
n=4, k=1, c=[2,3,1,2], capital at city 3 (index 3)

Cycle edges:
- (1,2): 2*3 = 6
- (2,3): 3*1 = 3  
- (3,4): 1*2 = 2
- (4,1): 2*2 = 4
Sum of cycle = 6+3+2+4 = 15

Capital at city 3 (index 3), which has value 1. Capital connections:
- To city 1 (already has cycle edge (4,1), and (3,4) exists, but (3,1)? 3 and 1 are not adjacent in 4-cycle: adjacency is (1,2), (2,3), (3,4), (4,1)
- So (3,1) is not adjacent, so add 1*2 = 2
- To city 2: (3,2) is adjacent (since (2,3) exists in cycle), so don't add
- To city 4: (3,4) is adjacent, so don't add

So capital connections add 2.

Total = 15 + 2 = 17 ✓

This matches!

So the algorithm:
1. Calculate sum of products for all cycle edges
2. For each capital city x, for each other city y, if (x,y) are not adjacent in the cycle, add c[x] * c[y] 

But we need to be efficient since n can be up to 100,000.

Cycle adjacency: two cities i and j are adjacent if |i-j| = 1 or |i-j| = n-1 (considering 1-based indexing).

For a capital at position x, it has neighbors x-1 and x+1 (with wraparound). So we don't want to add edges to these neighbors.

So for each capital x, we add sum of (c[x] * c[y]) for all y ≠ x and y is not adjacent to x.

We can rewrite this as:
For each capital x: c[x] * (sum of c[y] for all y ≠ x and y not adjacent to x)

Total sum = (sum of cycle edges) + (sum over all capitals x of c[x] * (sum of c[y] for y ≠ x and not adjacent to x))

We can calculate the sum of c[y] for all y first, then subtract the contributions of adjacent cities.

For each capital x:
- Total sum of all c[y] = S
- Sum of c[y] for adjacent y = c[x-1] + c[x+1] (with wraparound)
- So sum of c[y] for non-adjacent y = S - c[x] - (c[x-1] + c[x+1])

Therefore, contribution from capital x = c[x] * (S - c[x] - c[x-1] - c[x+1])

But we need to handle indexing carefully (1-based).

Let me implement this:

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Convert to 0-based indexing
capitals = [x - 1 for x in capitals]

# Calculate sum of all beauty values
total_sum = sum(c)

# Calculate cycle edge contributions
cycle_sum = 0
for i in range(n):
    j = (i + 1) % n
    cycle_sum += c[i] * c[j]

# Calculate capital connection contributions
capital_sum = 0
for capital in capitals:
    # Find adjacent cities (with wraparound)
    prev = (capital - 1) % n
    next_val = (capital + 1) % n
    
    # Sum of beauty values of adjacent cities to capital
    adjacent_sum = c[prev] + c[next_val]
    
    # Sum of beauty values of non-adjacent cities to capital
    non_adjacent_sum = total_sum - c[capital] - adjacent_sum
    
    # Contribution from capital to non-adjacent cities
    capital_sum += c[capital] * non_adjacent_sum

# Total result
result = cycle_sum + capital_sum
print(result)
```

Let me read again: "All of cities in the country are beautiful, but each is beautiful in its own way. Beauty value of i-th city equals to ci. All the cities are consecutively connected by the roads, including 1-st and n-th city, forming a cyclic route 1 — 2 — ... — n — 1. Formally, for every 1 ≤ i < n there is a road between i-th and i + 1-th city, and another one between 1-st and n-th city. Each capital city is connected with each other city directly by the roads."

So both exist. And "there is at most one road between any two cities", so if a road exists in both, we count it once.

So for each pair (i,j), we include c[i]*c[j] if there is a road between them.

The roads are:
- The cycle edges
- The capital edges (from each capital to every other city)

But for a non-adjacent pair (i,j), if i is capital, there is a road, and if it's in the cycle, it's already there.

So the set of roads is the union of:
1. All adjacent pairs in the cycle
2. All pairs (capital i, city j) for j ≠ i

For a pair (i,j), it is present if:
- (i,j) is adjacent in cycle, OR
- i is a capital and j is any city (including when (i,j) might be adjacent)

But since we only want to count each road once, if (i,j) is both in cycle and (i,j) is between capital i and j, we count it once.

In the example, for cities 1 and 4 (1-based):
- (1,4) is not in cycle, so if 1 is capital, there is a direct road.

In 1-based indexing for n=5:
- Cycle edges: (1,2), (2,3), (3,4), (4,5), (5,1)
- Capital 1: has roads to (1,2), (1,3), (1,4), (1,5)
- But (1,2) and (5,1) are in cycle, so in the union, we have (1,3), (1,4)
- Capital 4: has roads to (4,1), (4,2), (4,3), (4,5)
- (4,5) and (3,4) are in cycle, so in the union, we have (4,1), (4,2)

So the final roads are:
- Cycle: (1,2), (2,3), (3,4), (4,5), (5,1)
- Additional: (1,3), (1,4), (4,1), (4,2)

But (4,1) is the same as (1,4), so we don't duplicate.

So we have:
- (1,2): 3*5 = 15

Ah! So I see the mistake. When I said "non-adjacent", I was only considering one direction, but in the capital connections, for a capital at i, it has a road to every j ≠ i, but if (i,j) is in the cycle, it's already included, so we only add the non-adjacent ones.

But in the above, for capital 1: non-adjacent cities are 3 and 4 (since 2 and 5 are adjacent)
For capital 4: non-adjacent cities are 1 and 2 (since 3 and 5 are adjacent)

In 1-based, for city 1: adjacent to 2 and 5 (since (1,2), (5,1))
So non-adjacent: 3,4

For city 4: adjacent to 3 and 5 (since (3,4), (4,5))
So non-adjacent: 1,2

So the additional roads are:
- (1,3): 3*2 = 6
- (1,4): 3*2 = 6
- (4,1): 2*3 = 6 (same as (1,4)) 
- (4,2): 2*5 = 10

But (4,1) is the same as (1,4), so we should not add it again.

Ah! I see the issue. In my algorithm, when I said "for each capital x, add to non-adjacent cities", I am adding for every pair (x,y) with x capital, y not adjacent to x. But when I do that, I am adding (x,y) and (y,x), but since we are summing c[x]*c[y], and when I do for x, I add c[x]*c[y], and when I do for y if y is also a capital, I might add c[y]*c[x], which is the same.

But in the example, city 1 and 4 are both capitals, and they are not adjacent, so when I process capital 1, I add for y=3,4: c[1]*c[3], c[1]*c[4]
When I process capital 4, I add for y=1,2: c[4]*c[1], c[4]*c[2]

So the pair (1,4) is added twice: once from capital 1 to city 4, once from capital 4 to city 1.

But in reality, there is only one road between them.

So I am double-counting the pairs between two capitals.

In the example:
- (1,3): only from capital 1
- (1,4): from both capital 1 and capital 4
- (4,2): from capital 4
- (1,2): already in cycle
- etc.

The pair (1,4) is between two capitals, and is counted twice in my algorithm.

So I need to avoid double-counting.

The solution is: for each capital x, add c[x] * (sum of c[y] for y ≠ x and y not adjacent to x) only for y that are not capitals? No, because the road exists regardless.

Actually, the road between a capital and a non-capital city should be added only once, and between two capitals should be added only once.

In the pair (x,y) where x and y are both capitals and not adjacent, this road should be counted only once.

In my current algorithm, when I process capital x, I add c[x] * c[y] for each y not adjacent to x.
When I process capital y, I add c[y] * c[x] for each x not adjacent to y.

Since c[x]*c[y] = c[y]*c[x], I am double-counting.

So I need to ensure that each edge is counted only once.

Therefore, I should only consider edges between a capital and a city, and count each edge once.

So the additional roads are:
- For each pair (x,y) where x is capital, y is not adjacent to x, and (x,y) is not between two capitals

But this is messy.

Better: when we add the capital connections, we are adding for each capital x to each non-adjacent city y, but we must avoid double-counting between two capitals.

Since the road between x and y with x,y both capitals and not adjacent should only be counted once.

So in our sum, for the capital connections, we should only add edges (x,y) where either x is capital and y is not a capital, or x and y are both capitals but only once.

Actually, we can simply not double-count by only including edges where the destination city is not a capital, and then separately handle edges between two capitals.

But that's complicated.

Alternatively, we can add the sum for each capital x of c[x] * (sum of c[y] for y not adjacent to x), and then subtract the double-counted edges.

When we do this, each edge (x,y) where x is capital and y is not adjacent to x is added once (when processing x), and each edge (x,y) where x and y are both capitals and y is not adjacent to x is added twice (once when processing x, once when processing y).

For an edge between two capitals x and y that are not adjacent, it is added twice: once in capital x's sum, once in capital y's sum.

So we have overcounted by exactly one time for each such edge.

Therefore, we can do:
- First calculate the sum of capital connections as sum over all capitals x of c[x] * (sum of c[y] for all y not adjacent to x)
- Then subtract the sum of c[i]*c[j] for each pair of capitals (i,j) where i and j are not adjacent in the cycle

Because for each such pair, it was counted twice, and should be counted once.

In the example:
First, sum over capitals:
For capital 0 (value 3): not adjacent to 2,3 → c[2]=2, c[3]=2, sum = 4, contribution = 3*4 = 12
For capital 3 (value 2): not adjacent to 0,1 → c[0]=3, c[1]=5, sum = 8, contribution = 2*8 = 16
Total = 28

But this counts (0,2), (0,3), (3,0), (3,1) - so (0,3) is counted twice.

The double-counted edge is (0,3): value 3*2 = 6

So we need to subtract this once: 28 - 6 = 22

Then total = cycle_sum + 22 = 49 + 22 = 71 ✓

Perfect!

So the algorithm is:

1. Calculate cycle_sum (sum of c[i] * c[(i+1)%n] for i in range(n))
2. Calculate capital_sum = sum over each capital x of c[x] * (sum of c[y] for y not adjacent to x)
3. For each pair of capitals (i,j) where i < j and (i,j) are not adjacent in the cycle, subtract c[i]*c[j] once
4. Result = cycle_sum + capital_sum - double_count

In step 2, when we say "not adjacent", we mean in the cycle, not in value.

For a capital at x, adjacent cities are (x-1)%n and (x+1)%n.

For the double-counting, we need to find pairs of capitals that are not adjacent in the cycle.

So in code:

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Convert to 0-based indexing
capitals = [x - 1 for x in capitals]

# Calculate sum of all beauty values
total_sum = sum(c)

# Calculate cycle edge contributions
cycle_sum = 0
for i in range(n):
    j = (i + 1) % n
    cycle_sum += c[i] * c[j]

# Calculate capital connection contributions without double-counting
capital_sum = 0
capital_set = set(capitals)

for capital in capitals:
    # Find adjacent cities to this capital
    prev = (capital - 1) % n
    next_val = (capital + 1) % n
    
    # Sum of beauty values of adjacent cities to capital
    adjacent_sum = c[prev] + c[next_val]
    
    # Sum of beauty values of non-adjacent cities to capital
    non_adjacent_sum = total_sum - c[capital] - adjacent_sum
    
    # Contribution from capital to non-adjacent cities
    capital_sum += c[capital] * non_adjacent_sum

# Now subtract the double-counted edges between two capitals
# Double-counted edges: when two capitals i and j are not adjacent in cycle
double_count = 0
capital_list = list(capitals)
for i in range(len(capital_list)):
    for j in range(i + 1, len(capital_list)):
        a = capital_list[i]
        b = capital_list[j]
        # Check if a and b are adjacent in cycle
        # They are adjacent if |a-b| = 1 or |a-b| = n-1 (considering modulo)
        if abs(a - b) == 1 or abs(a - b) == n - 1:
            # They are adjacent, so no double-counting for this edge
            continue
        else:
            # They are not adjacent, and both are capitals, so edge (a,b) was counted twice
            double_count += c[a] * c[b]

# Total result
result = cycle_sum + capital_sum - double_count
print(result)
```

But this double loop is O(k²) and k can be up to 100,000, which might be too slow.

We need to optimize.

For the double-counting part: we need to sum c[i]*c[j] for all pairs of capitals i,j where i < j and |i-j| ≠ 1 and |i-j| ≠ n-1 (with wraparound).

The total sum of c[i]*c[j] for all pairs i<j is (sum of c[i]) * (sum of c[j] for j>i) / 2, but we need to exclude adjacent pairs.

Total pairwise sum = (1/2) * (sum of c[i])^2 - (1/2) * sum of c[i]^2

Then subtract the sum of c[i]*c[j] for adjacent pairs of capitals.

So:
total_pair_sum = (total_sum * total_sum - sum_of_squares) // 2
adjacent_pair_sum = sum of c[i]*c[j] for each pair of capitals (i,j) that are adjacent in the cycle

Then double_count = total_pair_sum - adjacent_pair_sum

But this only works if we consider all pairs, including adjacent ones.

We want to exclude only the non-adjacent pairs from the total_pair_sum, and then the double_count is the sum of c[i]*c[j] for non-adjacent pairs of capitals.

So double_count = (sum over all pairs i<j of c[i]*c[j]) - (sum over adjacent pairs of (c[i]*c[j]))

Where sum over all pairs i<j of c[i]*c[j] = (total_sum^2 - sum_sq) / 2

And sum over adjacent pairs of (c[i]*c[j]) = sum over each adjacent pair of capitals of c[i]*c[j]

So double_count = (total_sum^2 - sum_sq) // 2 - adjacent_pair_sum

But only for pairs that are adjacent in the cycle and both are capitals.

In the example: capitals at 0 and 3 (0-based), n=5
total_sum = 16, sum_sq = 3^2 + 5^2 + 2^2 + 2^2 + 4^2 = 9+25+4+4+16 = 58
total_pair_sum = (16*16 - 58) // 2 = (256-58)//2 = 198//2 = 99
adjacent_pair_sum: are 0 and 3 adjacent? |0-3|=3, n-1=4, so 3≠4, not adjacent. So adjacent_pair_sum = 0
double_count = 99 - 0 = 99

But we only want the non-adjacent pairs of capitals. And there is only one pair: (0,3), which is not adjacent, so double_count should be 3*2 = 6.

The total_pair_sum includes all pairs of values, not just between capitals.

I see the mistake. The expression (total_sum^2 - sum_sq)//2 is the sum of c[i]*c[j] for all pairs i < j of all cities, not just between capitals.

We want the sum of c[i]*c[j] for all pairs of capitals i < j.

Let S_cap = sum of c[i] for i in capitals
S_cap_sq = sum of c[i]^2 for i in capitals

Then sum of c[i]*c[j] for all pairs i<j of capitals = (S_cap^2 - S_cap_sq) // 2

And then we need to subtract the sum of c[i]*c[j] for adjacent pairs of capitals.

So double_count = (S_cap^2 - S_cap_sq) // 2 - adjacent_pair_sum

In example: S_cap = c[0] + c[3] = 3 + 2 = 5
S_cap_sq = 9 + 4 = 13
sum_pairs = (25 - 13)//2 = 12//2 = 6
adjacent_pair_sum = 0 (since not adjacent)
double_count = 6 - 0 = 6 ✓

Perfect.

So now the code:

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Convert to 0-based indexing
capitals = [x - 1 for x in capitals]

# Calculate sum of all beauty values
total_sum = sum(c)

# Calculate cycle edge contributions
cycle_sum = 0
for i in range(n):
    j = (i + 1) % n
    cycle_sum += c[i] * c[j]

# Calculate capital connection contributions without double-counting
capital_sum = 0
capital_set = set(capitals)

for capital in capitals:
    # Find adjacent cities to this capital
    prev = (capital - 1) % n
    next_val = (capital + 1) % n
    
    # Sum of beauty values of adjacent cities to capital
    adjacent_sum = c[prev] + c[next_val]
    
    # Sum of beauty values of non-adjacent cities to capital
    non_adjacent_sum = total_sum - c[capital] - adjacent_sum
    
    # Contribution from capital to non-adjacent cities
    capital_sum += c[capital] * non_adjacent_sum

# Calculate sum of c[i]*c[j] for all pairs of capitals i<j
S_cap = sum(c[capital] for capital in capitals)
S_cap_sq = sum(c[capital] ** 2 for capital in capitals)
total_cap_pair_sum = (S_cap * S_cap - S_cap_sq) // 2

# Calculate sum of c[i]*c[j] for adjacent pairs of capitals
adjacent_cap_pair_sum = 0
capital_list = list(capitals)
for i in range(len(capital_list)):
    for j in range(i + 1, len(capital_list)):
        a = capital_list[i]
        b = capital_list[j]
        # Check if a and b are adjacent in cycle
        if abs(a - b) == 1 or abs(a - b) == n - 1:
            adjacent_cap_pair_sum += c[a] * c[b]

# Double-counted edges between two capitals (counted twice in capital_sum, should be counted once)
double_count = total_cap_pair_sum - adjacent_cap_pair_sum

# Total result
result = cycle_sum + capital_sum - double_count
print(result)
```

But still O(k²) for the adjacent pair sum. We can optimize this.

We can precompute the adjacent pairs of capitals without nested loops.

For each capital, its adjacent cities in the cycle are (capital-1)%n and (capital+1)%n.

For two capitals a and b to be adjacent, they must have |a-b| = 1 or |a-b| = n-1.

We can iterate through the capital list and for each capital, check if its neighbors are also in the capital list.

For each capital a, check if (a-1)%n is in capitals and (a+1)%n is in capitals, and if so, add c[a] * c[neighbor] to adjacent_cap_pair_sum.

But we need to avoid double-counting (i,j) and (j,i).

So for each capital a, if (a-1)%n is in capitals and (a-1)%n != a, then add c[a] * c[(a-1)%n]
Similarly for (a+1)%n.

But this will count each adjacent pair twice.

So we can do:

adjacent_cap_pair_sum = 0
capital_set = set(capitals)
for capital in capitals:
    # Check left neighbor
    left = (capital - 1) % n
    if left in capital_set and left != capital:  # left is a capital and not itself
        adjacent_cap_pair_sum += c[capital] * c[left]
    # Check right neighbor
    right = (capital + 1) % n
    if right in capital_set and right != capital:
        adjacent_cap_pair_sum += c[capital] * c[right]

But this counts each edge twice (once from each end).

So we need to divide by 2.

adjacent_cap_pair_sum //= 2

In the example: capitals [0,3]
For capital 0: left = (0-1)%5 = 4, not in capitals (capitals are 0,3), right = (0+1)%5 = 1, not in capitals. So adjacent_cap_pair_sum = 0
Then divide by 2: 0.

Correct.

But in a different example, say capitals at 0,1,2 in n=5.
For capital 0: left=4, not in capitals; right=1, in capitals → add c[0]*c[1]
For capital 1: left=0, in capitals → add c[1]*c[0]; right=2, in capitals → add c[1]*c[2]
For capital 2: left=1, in capitals → add c[2]*c[1]; right=3, not in capitals
So total before divide = c[0]c[1] + c[1]c[0] + c[1]c[2] + c[2]c[1] = 2*c[0]c[1] + 2*c[1]c[2]
After divide = c[0]c[1] + c[1]c[2], which is correct for the adjacent pairs (0,1) and (1,2).

Yes.

So final code:

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Convert to 0-based indexing
capitals = [x - 1 for x in capitals]

# Calculate sum of all beauty values
total_sum = sum(c)

# Calculate cycle edge contributions
cycle_sum = 0
for i in range(n):
    j = (i + 1) % n
    cycle_sum += c[i] * c[j]

# Calculate capital connection contributions without double-counting
capital_sum = 0
capital_set = set(capitals)

for capital in capitals:
    # Find adjacent cities to this capital
    prev = (capital - 1) % n
    next_val = (capital + 1) % n
    
    # Sum of beauty values of adjacent cities to capital
    adjacent_sum = c[prev] + c[next_val]
    
    # Sum of beauty values of non-adjacent cities to capital
    non_adjacent_sum = total_sum - c[capital] - adjacent_sum
    
    # Contribution from capital to non-adjacent cities
    capital_sum += c[capital] * non_adjacent_sum

# Calculate sum of c[i]*c[j] for all pairs of capitals i<j
S_cap = sum(c[capital] for capital in capitals)
S_cap_sq = sum(c[capital] ** 2 for capital in capitals)
total_cap_pair_sum = (S_cap * S_cap - S_cap_sq) // 2

# Calculate sum of c[i]*c[j] for adjacent pairs of capitals
adjacent_cap_pair_sum = 0
capital_set = set(capitals)
for capital in capitals:
    # Check left neighbor
    left = (capital - 1) % n
    if left in capital_set:
        adjacent_cap_pair_sum += c[capital] * c[left]
    # Check right neighbor
    right = (capital + 1) % n
    if right in capital_set:
        adjacent_cap_pair_sum += c[capital] * c[right]

# Since each adjacent pair is counted twice, divide by 2
adjacent_cap_pair_sum //= 2

# Double-counted edges between two capitals (counted twice in capital_sum, should be counted once)
double_count = total_cap_pair_sum - adjacent_cap_pair_sum

# Total result
result = cycle_sum + capital_sum - double_count
print(result)
```

This should be efficient: O(n + k) total.

Let me test with the examples.

Example 1: n=4, k=1, c=[2,3,1,2], capitals=[2] (0-based)

total_sum = 8

cycle_sum = 2*3 + 3*1 + 1*2 + 2*2 = 6+3+2+4 = 15

capital_sum: capital at 2
prev = 1, next_val = 3
adjacent_sum = c[1] + c[3] = 3 + 2 = 5
non_adjacent_sum = 8 - 1 - 5 = 2
contribution = 1 * 2 = 2

S_cap = 1
S_cap_sq = 1
total_cap_pair_sum = (1 - 1)//2 = 0
adjacent_cap_pair_sum: capital at 2, left=1, right=3. Are they in capitals? capitals={2}, so no. So adjacent_cap_pair_sum=0, then //=2 =0
double_count = 0
result = 15 + 2 - 0 = 17 ✓

Example 2: n=5, k=2, c=[3,5,2,2,4], capitals=[0,3]

total_sum = 16

cycle_sum = 3*5 + 5*2 + 2*2 + 2*4 + 4*3 = 15+10+4+8+12 = 49

capital_sum:
capital at 0: prev=4, next=1. adjacent_sum = c[4]+c[1]=4+5=9
non_adjacent_sum = 16 - 3 - 9 = 4
contribution = 3*4 = 12
capital at 3: prev=2, next=4. adjacent_sum = c[2]+c[4]=2+4=6
non_adjacent_sum = 16 - 2 - 6 = 8
contribution = 2*8 = 16
total capital_sum = 12+16 = 28

S_cap = 3+2 = 5
S_cap_sq = 9+4 = 13
total_cap_pair_sum = (25-13)//2 = 12//2 = 6

adjacent_cap_pair_sum: 
capital 0: left=4 (not in capitals), right=1 (not in capitals) → 0
capital 3: left=2 (not in capitals), right=4 (not in capitals) → 0
adjacent_cap_pair_sum = 0, then //=2 = 0
double_count = 6 - 0 = 6
result = 49 + 28 - 6 = 71 ✓

Perfect.

Final code:

c = list(map(int, input().split()))
capitals = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Convert to 0-based indexing

# Calculate sum of all beauty values
total_sum = sum(c)

# Calculate cycle edge contributions
cycle_sum = 0
for i in range(n):

    cycle_sum += c[i] * c[j]

# Calculate capital connection contributions without double-counting

capital_set = set(capitals)

for capital in capitals:
    # Find adjacent cities to this capital
    prev = (capital - 1) % n
    next_val = (capital + 1) % n
    
    # Sum of beauty values of adjacent cities to capital
    adjacent_sum = c[prev] + c[next_val]
    
    # Sum of beauty values of non-adjacent cities to capital
    non_adjacent_sum = total_sum - c[capital] - adjacent_sum
    
    # Contribution from capital to non-adjacent cities
    capital_sum += c[capital] * non_adjacent_sum

# Calculate sum of c[i]*c[j] for all pairs of capitals i<j
S_cap = sum(c[capital] for capital in capitals)
S_cap_sq = sum(c[capital] ** 2 for capital in capitals)

    left = (capital - 1) % n
    if left in capital_set:
        adjacent_cap_pair_sum += c[capital] * c[left]
    # Check right neighbor

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Vasya plays one very well-known and extremely popular MMORPG game. His game character has k skill; currently the i-th of them equals to ai. Also this game has a common rating table in which the participants are ranked according to the product of all the skills of a hero in the descending order.

Vasya decided to 'upgrade' his character via the game store. This store offers n possible ways to improve the hero's skills; Each of these ways belongs to one of three types:

  1. assign the i-th skill to b; 
  2. add b to the i-th skill; 
  3. multiply the i-th skill by b. 



Unfortunately, a) every improvement can only be used once; b) the money on Vasya's card is enough only to purchase not more than m of the n improvements. Help Vasya to reach the highest ranking in the game. To do this tell Vasya which of improvements he has to purchase and in what order he should use them to make his rating become as high as possible. If there are several ways to achieve it, print any of them.

Input

The first line contains three numbers — k, n, m (1 ≤ k ≤ 105, 0 ≤ m ≤ n ≤ 105) — the number of skills, the number of improvements on sale and the number of them Vasya can afford.

The second line contains k space-separated numbers ai (1 ≤ ai ≤ 106), the initial values of skills.

Next n lines contain 3 space-separated numbers tj, ij, bj (1 ≤ tj ≤ 3, 1 ≤ ij ≤ k, 1 ≤ bj ≤ 106) — the type of the j-th improvement (1 for assigning, 2 for adding, 3 for multiplying), the skill to which it can be applied and the value of b for this improvement.

Output

The first line should contain a number l (0 ≤ l ≤ m) — the number of improvements you should use.

The second line should contain l distinct space-separated numbers vi (1 ≤ vi ≤ n) — the indices of improvements in the order in which they should be applied. The improvements are numbered starting from 1, in the order in which they appear in the input. 

Examples

Input

2 4 3
13 20
1 1 14
1 2 30
2 1 6
3 2 2


Output

3
2 3 4

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Now solve the problem by providing the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have k skills with initial values a₁, a₂, ..., aₖ
2. We have n improvement options that can be applied to any skill
3. Each improvement can be used only once
4. We can afford at most m improvements
5. The ranking is based on the product of all skills (larger product = better ranking)
6. We need to find the optimal sequence of improvements to maximize the product

## Key Insights

1. Since we want to maximize the product of all skills, we need to apply operations that increase the product as much as possible
2. For each improvement type:
   - Type 1 (assign): sets skill i to b. This might be better than other operations if b is large
   - Type 2 (add): adds b to skill i. This increases the skill by b
   - Type 3 (multiply): multiplies skill i by b. This has a much bigger impact on the product than addition
3. The product is maximized when we prioritize operations that have the most significant impact on increasing the product

## Strategy

1. For each improvement, calculate its potential impact on the product
2. Since multiplication has the most significant impact, we should prioritize multiplication operations
3. For addition, we should apply them to skills that are already large (since adding to larger values has more impact)
4. For assignment, we should consider if assigning a large value would be better than other operations

However, there's a crucial insight: when considering the product, multiplying by b when the skill is already x gives us a factor of x × b, while adding b gives us a factor of x + b. We need to compare these.

But actually, since we're dealing with products, we should consider the relative impact. For multiplication, the relative increase is b. For addition, the relative increase is b/x.

So for multiplication: relative impact = b
For addition: relative impact = b/x

Therefore, multiplication operations have a more significant impact when the current skill is small, but addition might have more impact when the skill is already large.

However, let me reconsider. The product grows as we increase individual skills. For a skill currently at value v, multiplying by b gives us v×b, while adding b gives us v+b.

The ratio of the new product to old product is:
- For multiplication: b
- For addition: (v+b)/v = 1 + b/v

So multiplication gives a ratio of b, addition gives a ratio of 1 + b/v.

Since b ≥ 1, multiplication always gives at least a ratio of 1 (same as adding b when v → ∞), but multiplication gives a much larger ratio when v is small.

Actually, when v is small, b > 1 + b/v, so multiplication is better. When v is large, 1 + b/v might be larger than b.

Wait, let me check: is b > 1 + b/v for all v?
b > 1 + b/v → b - b/v > 1 → b(1 - 1/v) > 1 → b(v-1)/v > 1

This depends on v and b. For v = 1: b(0)/1 = 0 < 1, so addition is better when v=1.
For v = 2: b(1)/2 > 1 → b > 2

So when v=1 and b ≤ 2, addition is better than multiplication.

This is getting complex. Let's think differently.

Since we want to maximize the product, and the product is multiplicative, we should prioritize operations that give the highest multiplicative increase.

For each improvement, the multiplicative factor it provides is:
- Type 1 (assign): the factor is (b / current_value) - this is only meaningful if we're replacing
- Type 2 (add): the factor is (current_value + b) / current_value = 1 + b/current_value
- Type 3 (multiply): the factor is b

Since we want to maximize the product, we should consider the multiplicative increase factor from each operation.

For multiplication operations, the factor is b.
For addition operations, the factor is 1 + b/current_value.
For assignment operations, the factor is (b/current_value) when replacing, or b when the current value is 0 (but skills are at least 1).

So the best strategy is to:
1. Prioritize multiplication operations (highest multiplicative increase)
2. Among those, prioritize operations where b is large
3. For addition, consider adding to skills that have a high current value (so 1 + b/current_value is large)
4. For assignment, only consider if b is very large compared to current values

But there's a simpler approach: since we have limited operations (m), we should greedily pick the operations that give the highest multiplicative increase.

Actually, let me think of a simpler greedy approach:
- We want to maximize the final product
- The product is multiplicative, so we want operations that multiply a skill by a large factor
- Multiplication operations give a factor of b
- Addition operations give a factor of 1 + b/current_value
- Assignment operations give a factor of b/current_value (when replacing)

Since b always ≥ 1, multiplication operations always give at least a factor of 1, while addition operations give at least 1.1 if b ≥ 1 and current_value ≤ b.

But the key insight is that multiplication operations typically give larger improvements than addition when b is moderate or large.

Let me compare two operations:
- Operation 1: multiply skill by b → factor = b
- Operation 2: add b to skill → factor = 1 + b/v

When is b > 1 + b/v?
b - b/v > 1 → b(1 - 1/v) > 1 → b(v-1)/v > 1

So when b(v-1)/v > 1, multiplication is better.
This happens when b > v/(v-1).

For v > 1: v/(v-1) > 1, so if b > v/(v-1), multiplication is better.
For v = 1: v/(v-1) is undefined, but when v=1, multiplication gives factor b, addition gives factor 1 + b/1 = b+1.
So when v=1, addition is always better.

Therefore:
- When current skill = 1: addition is always better than multiplication (since 1 + b > b when b > 0)
- When current skill > 1: multiplication is better than addition when b > v/(v-1)

For v=2: b > 2/1 = 2
For v=3: b > 3/2 = 1.5
For v=4: b > 4/3 ≈ 1.33

So for larger v, the threshold decreases.

Since b ≥ 1, for v ≥ 3, b > v/(v-1) is often true (especially for large v).

But we also have assignment operations.

For assignment, if we assign b to a skill that has value v, the multiplicative factor is b/v.

This is only good when b/v > 1, i.e., b > v.

So assignment is only good when b > v.

In practice, we should prioritize:
1. All multiplication operations with large b values
2. Addition operations to skills with high current values (where 1 + b/v is large)
3. Assignment operations only when b is much larger than current values

But since we're limited by m, and we want the maximum product, we can use a greedy approach:

Sort all operations by their potential multiplicative increase, and apply the top m operations (or until we run out of applicable operations).

However, we need to be careful about the order. Since operations affect each other, we need to consider the sequence.

Actually, the order of operations matters! If we multiply first then add, it's different from adding first then multiplying.

But multiplication is commutative in a way - multiplying by b and then by c is the same as multiplying by c and then by b.

For addition, the order matters. Adding to a small value might be better than adding to a large value.

But multiplication operations on different skills are independent and commutative.

So we can think of it as: we're going to apply some operations, and we want to maximize the final product.

The key insight is that for any operation, we can calculate the expected multiplicative increase it would provide if applied to a skill with current value v.

However, since we can choose the order, and multiplication operations are independent, we should prioritize them.

Let me implement a greedy solution:

1. For each operation, calculate its multiplicative impact factor if applied to the current skill value
2. Among all operations, sort by this factor in descending order
3. Select the top m operations (or all operations if less than m)

For the general case, I will implement the sorting by multiplicative factor.

So final solution:

1. Read input
2. For each operation, calculate initial multiplicative factor
3. Sort operations by factor in descending order
4. Take first min(m, n) operations
5. Output the indices in the order of the sorted list

```python
k, n, m = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

operations = []
for i in range(n):
    t, idx, b = map(int, input().split())
    idx -= 1  # convert to 0-indexed
    if t == 1:  # assign
        factor = b / a[idx]
    elif t == 2:  # add
        factor = 1 + b / a[idx]
    else:  # multiply
        factor = b
    operations.append((factor, i+1))  # (factor, original_index)

# Sort by factor in descending order
operations.sort(key=lambda x: x[0], reverse=True)

# Take at most m operations
selected = operations[:m]

# Output
print(len(selected))
if selected:
    print(' '.join(str(idx) for factor, idx in selected))
else:
    print()
```

```python
k, n, m = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

operations = []
for i in range(n):
    t, idx, b = map(int, input().split())
    idx -= 1  # convert to 0-indexed
    if t == 1:  # assign
        factor = b / a[idx]
    elif t == 2:  # add
        factor = 1 + b / a[idx]
    else:  # multiply
        factor = b
    operations.append((factor, i+1))  # (factor, original_index)

# Sort by factor in descending order
operations.sort(key=lambda x: x[0], reverse=True)

# Take at most m operations
l = min(m, len(operations))
selected = operations[:l]

# Output
print(l)
if l > 0:
    print(' '.join(str(idx) for factor, idx in selected))
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Vasya plays one very well-known and extremely popular MMORPG game. His game character has k skill; currently the i-th of them equals to ai. Also this game has a common rating table in which the participants are ranked according to the product of all the skills of a hero in the descending order.

Vasya decided to 'upgrade' his character via the game store. This store offers n possible ways to improve the hero's skills; Each of these ways belongs to one of three types:

  1. assign the i-th skill to b; 
  2. add b to the i-th skill; 
  3. multiply the i-th skill by b. 



Unfortunately, a) every improvement can only be used once; b) the money on Vasya's card is enough only to purchase not more than m of the n improvements. Help Vasya to reach the highest ranking in the game. To do this tell Vasya which of improvements he has to purchase and in what order he should use them to make his rating become as high as possible. If there are several ways to achieve it, print any of them.

Input

The first line contains three numbers — k, n, m (1 ≤ k ≤ 105, 0 ≤ m ≤ n ≤ 105) — the number of skills, the number of improvements on sale and the number of them Vasya can afford.

The second line contains k space-separated numbers ai (1 ≤ ai ≤ 106), the initial values of skills.

Next n lines contain 3 space-separated numbers tj, ij, bj (1 ≤ tj ≤ 3, 1 ≤ ij ≤ k, 1 ≤ bj ≤ 106) — the type of the j-th improvement (1 for assigning, 2 for adding, 3 for multiplying), the skill to which it can be applied and the value of b for this improvement.

Output

The first line should contain a number l (0 ≤ l ≤ m) — the number of improvements you should use.

The second line should contain l distinct space-separated numbers vi (1 ≤ vi ≤ n) — the indices of improvements in the order in which they should be applied. The improvements are numbered starting from 1, in the order in which they appear in the input. 

Examples

Input

2 4 3
13 20
1 1 14
1 2 30
2 1 6
3 2 2


Output

3
2 3 4

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

# YOUR CODE HERE

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have k skills with initial values a₁, a₂, ..., aₖ
2. We have n improvement options of 3 types:
   - Type 1: Assign skill i to value b (replace current value)
   - Type 2: Add b to skill i (add to current value)
   - Type 3: Multiply skill i by b (multiply current value)
3. We can only use m improvements (m ≤ n)
4. The rating is the product of all skills
5. Goal: maximize the product and output which improvements to use in what order

## Key Insights

1. Since we want to maximize the product, we should prioritize operations that give the largest multiplicative increase
2. For a given skill, which operation gives the biggest boost?
   - Type 1 (assignment): sets the skill to a fixed value
   - Type 2 (addition): adds b to current value
   - Type 3 (multiplication): multiplies current value by b

3. For any skill, we want to use the operation that gives the maximum product increase. However, we need to consider:
   - The current value of the skill
   - The multiplier effect of each operation
   - The fact that we have limited operations (m)

4. In general, multiplication is more powerful than addition for large values. For small values, assignment might be better.

5. Strategy:
   - For each operation, calculate its potential impact on the final product
   - Sort operations by their impact (descending)
   - Apply the top m operations (or fewer if we run out of operations)

   - Multiplication should generally be applied after addition if possible
   - Assignment should be applied last since it completely resets the value

    - Multiplication by b on value v gives final value v*b
    - Addition of b gives final value v+b
    - Assignment gives final value b
    
    For v > b, multiplication is better than addition when v*b > v+b, which simplifies to v*(b-1) > b, or v > b/(b-1)
    Since b ≥ 1, b/(b-1) is at most 2 (when b=2, it's 2; when b>2, it's <2), so if v ≥ 3, multiplication is better for b>1.

3. Instead, I'll rank operations by their effectiveness: for multiplication, the gain is multiplied by current value; for addition, gain is added; for assignment, it's a complete reset

Since we can only use m operations, and we want to maximize the product, I'll:
- Sort operations by the relative increase they provide, considering the current skill value
- But the current value changes with operations

This is computationally complex. Since the constraints are up to 10^5, we need an O(n log n) solution.

Simpler insight: Pre-calculate the potential final value for each operation applied in optimal order, but this is still complex.

Actually, the problem says "if there are several ways to achieve it, print any of them". So we don't need the absolutely optimal one, just one that maximizes the product.

Given that, and since multiplication grows faster than addition, I'll prioritize multiplication operations, then addition, then assignment, and ensure that for the same skill, multiplication comes before addition.

But still, the order matters. 

Another approach: since we want maximum product, and with multiplicative operations, we should apply operations that give the highest multiplicative factor. So for each operation, the "quality" might be roughly the logarithm of the result.

Let me define a score for each operation: for a skill with current value v, and operation with value b:
- Type 1 (assign): score = log(b/v) 
- Type 2 (add): score = log((v+b)/v) = log(1 + b/v)
- Type 3 (multiply): score = log(b) 

But since we're dealing with logarithms of products, the total log product is sum of logs of each skill's final value.

So the total log product is: sum over skills of log(final_value)

So we want to maximize this sum.

For an operation on skill i:
- Assignment: becomes log(b) - log(current_value)
- Addition: becomes log(1 + b/current_value)
- Multiplication: becomes log(b) + log(current_value) - log(current_value) = log(b) 

Wait, no:
- Current value: v
- After operation: new_value
- Change in log product: log(new_value) - log(v)

So:
- Assignment: log(b) - log(v)
- Addition: log(v + b) - log(v) = log(1 + b/v)
- Multiplication: log(v * b) - log(v) = log(b)

So the gain from operation is:
- Assignment: log(b) - log(v)
- Addition: log(1 + b/v)
- Multiplication: log(b)

Since log(b) is always positive for b > 1, and log(1 + b/v) is also positive, we should compare these.

For multiplication: gain = log(b)
For addition: gain = log(1 + b/v)
For assignment: gain = log(b) - log(v)

Since we want maximum gain, and log(b) is the maximum for multiplication (since log(1 + b/v) ≤ log(1 + b) ≤ log(b) when v ≥ 1, b ≥ 1), multiplication generally gives the highest gain when v is large.

For small v, addition might be better? Let's check:
If v = 1, b = 2:
- Multiplication: log(2) ≈ 0.693
- Addition: log(3) - log(1) = log(3) ≈ 1.10
- Assignment: log(2) - log(1) = log(2) ≈ 0.693

So when v = 1, addition gives higher gain than multiplication.

This suggests that for small skills, addition might be better.

But let's recalculate:
- For v = 1, b = 2:
  - Multiplication: log(1*2) - log(1) = log(2) ≈ 0.693
  - Addition: log(1+2) - log(1) = log(3) ≈ 1.10
  - Assignment: log(2) - log(1) = log(2) ≈ 0.693

So addition gives the highest gain when v is small.

For v = 10, b = 2:
- Multiplication: log(20) - log(10) = log(2) ≈ 0.693
- Addition: log(12) - log(10) ≈ log(1.2) ≈ 0.182
- Assignment: log(2) - log(10) ≈ -log(5) ≈ -1.609

So multiplication gives the highest gain when v is large.

Therefore, the gain depends on v and b.

The optimal strategy might be:
1. For each operation, calculate the gain: log(new_value / current_value)
2. Sort operations by gain in descending order
3. Apply the first m operations in this order

But the value of v depends on previous operations, so the gain depends on order.

However, since we're using logarithms, and the gain is a sum of positive terms, and for any sequence of operations, the total log gain is the sum of individual gains (which depend on the current value), we need to apply operations in order of highest gain first.

This is complex to compute optimally due to dependencies.

Given the constraints and that we only need any optimal solution, I'll use the following heuristic:

1. Create a list of operations with their gain function
2. Sort operations by gain (initially based on current values)
3. Apply operations in that order

But since the gain depends on the current state, we'll simulate the process to evaluate the gain of each operation.

Actually, given the complexity, and since the problem says "if there are several ways, print any", I'll use this approach:

1. For each operation, calculate its potential gain assuming it's applied from the current state
2. Sort operations by gain in descending order
3. Apply the top m operations in this order

We'll simulate the application:

```python
import math

k, n, m = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

operations = []
for i in range(n):
    t, idx, b = map(int, input().split())
    # idx is 1-indexed, convert to 0-indexed
    operations.append((t, idx-1, b, i+1))  # (type, skill_index, value, original_index)

# For each operation, calculate gain in log space
# But we need to consider that the gain depends on current value
# We'll sort by gain at current state
# Since we don't know the future state, and we want maximum product,
# we'll use the gain based on current state

# Create list with gain calculation
operation_gains = []
for t, skill, b, orig_idx in operations:
    current_val = a[skill]
    if t == 1:  # assign
        gain = math.log(b / current_val)
    elif t == 2:  # add
        gain = math.log((current_val + b) / current_val)
    else:  # multiply
        gain = math.log(b)
    operation_gains.append((gain, t, skill, b, orig_idx))

# Sort by gain (descending)
operation_gains.sort(reverse=True)

# Apply the top m operations
result = []
current_a = a[:]

for gain, t, skill, b, orig_idx in operation_gains:
    if len(result) >= m:
        break
    if t == 1:  # assign
        current_a[skill] = b
    elif t == 2:  # add
        current_a[skill] += b
    else:  # multiply
        current_a[skill] *= b
    result.append(orig_idx)

# Output
print(len(result))
if result:
    print(' '.join(map(str, result)))
else:
    print()
```

Wait, this approach has a problem: the gain is calculated based on the initial values, but after applying operations, the value changes. And when we sort, we're using the initial values, which might not be optimal.

But since we're applying operations in the order of highest gain based on current state, and we're sorting by the gain at the current state, this should work.

The issue is that we calculate initial gain, but the current state changes.

To fix this, we need to simulate the operations in the order of highest "potential gain" based on current state.

But that requires knowing the future state.

Given the complexity and the fact that the constraints are up to 10^5, and that we only need any solution that maximizes the product, I think the simpler approach of sorting by the initial gain might be sufficient, especially since addition on large values has low gain.

Our method of sorting by initial gain doesn't work because the gain depends on the current value at the time of application.

Why? Because after assignment skill 2 becomes 30, then multiplication doubles it to 60.

If they applied multiplication first, skill 2 becomes 40, then assignment would set it to 30, which is bad.

So the order matters.

For a skill, if we have both assignment and multiplication, we should:
- First apply operations that build up the value (addition)
- Then apply multiplication (to maximize the final value)
- Then apply assignment (only if it gives a better value)

But in this case, assignment gives value 30, multiplication on 20 gives 40, so multiplication is better.

For a given skill, if we have both assignment and multiplication, and the assignment value is high, then assignment followed by multiplication might be better.

So if we assign to x and then multiply by y, final value is x * y.

If we multiply by y first (giving v*y), then assign to x, final value is x.

So we want x*y > x, which is true if y > 1.

So assignment then multiplication gives final value x*y, while multiplication then assignment gives final value x.

So assignment then multiplication is better when x*y > x, i.e., y > 1.

In our case, y=2>1, so assignment then multiplication is better.

Therefore, the strategy should be:

For each skill, consider the operations:
- If we have assignment to value x, and multiplication by y, then sequence: assignment then multiplication gives final value x*y
- This is better than any other sequence for this skill.

Also, for addition, if we add b, then multiply by y, final value is (v + b)*y, which is better than (v + b) or v*y.

So the best for a skill is to build it up with addition, then multiply.

But assignment might be better if it gives a good value.

Given the complexity, and since the problem only requires any solution that maximizes the product, here's a simple strategy:

1. For each skill, find the best possible final value among all operations on that skill
2. For operations of type 3 (multiply), they scale the current value
3. For type 2 (add), they increase the value
4. For type 1 (assign), they set to a fixed value

For each skill, the final value can be:
- By assignment: b
- By addition: v + b
- By multiplication: v * b
- By combination: e.g., add then multiply: (v + b) * c

For a given skill, the best final value is the maximum over all sequences.

But this is too complex.

Given the constraints and that we only need m operations, and m might be small, I'll try a greedy approach:

Sort operations by the final value they would give if applied in isolation to the initial state, in descending order.

For each operation:
- Type 1: final value = b
- Type 2: final value = a[i] + b
- Type 3: final value = a[i] * b

Sort by final value in descending order.

Apply the top m operations in this order.

But this ignores the order dependence.

In the example:
Skill 1: 
- Type 1: 14
- Type 2: 13+6 = 19
- Type 3: 13*2 = 26

Skill 2:
- Type 1: 30
- Type 2: 20+2 = 22
- Type 3: 20*2 = 40

- op1 (type1 skill1): 14
- op2 (type1 skill2): 30
- op3 (type2 skill1): 19
- op4 (type3 skill2): 40

1. op4: multiply skill2 by 2: [13, 40]

But expected is 1140.

So this doesn't work.

The expected solution is: 2 (assign skill2 to 30), 3 (add 6 to skill1), 4 (multiply skill2 by 2) -> [19, 60] -> product 1140.

So the final value of skill2 is 60 = 30 * 2.

The operations are applied in sequence, and the order matters.

In this sequence, after assignment, skill2 is 30, then multiplication by 2 gives 60.

If we apply multiplication first on skill2, we get 40, then assignment to 30 gives 30, which is worse.

So for a skill with both assignment and multiplication, assignment followed by multiplication is better.

Therefore, the strategy should be:

For each skill, if it has both assignment and multiplication operations, and if multiplication is by a factor >1, then we should prefer the assignment operation and then the multiplication operation on the same skill, in that order.

But we only have one of each improvement.

Since each improvement is unique, we can only use each once.

Given the complexity, and since the problem only requires any solution, I'll implement a simulated greedy with a priority queue.

But given time, I'll go with the following:

1. For each operation, calculate its "impact" as the potential increase in log product, based on current value
2. We'll simulate the process, and at each step, choose the operation that gives the highest gain
3. We do this for at most m steps

This is a greedy algorithm with correct order.

Since the gain depends on current state, we can compute it.

```python
import math

k, n, m = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

operations = []
for i in range(n):
    t, idx, b = map(int, input().split())
    operations.append((t, idx-1, b, i+1))

# We'll use a greedy approach: at each step, choose the operation that gives the highest log gain
# represented as (gain, type, skill, value, original_index)
result = []
current_a = a[:]

# Create list of all operations
# For each time, we'll evaluate all available operations
for step in range(m):
    best_gain = -10**18
    best_op = None
    best_type = None
    best_skill = None
    best_b = None
    best_idx = None
    
    for (t, skill, b, orig_idx) in operations:
        if orig_idx in result:
            continue  # already used
        current_val = current_a[skill]
        if t == 1:  # assign
            gain = math.log(b / current_val) if current_val > 0 else math.log(b)
        elif t == 2:  # add
            gain = math.log((current_val + b) / current_val)
        else:  # multiply
            gain = math.log(b)
        if gain > best_gain:
            best_gain = gain
            best_op = (t, skill, b, orig_idx)
    
    if best_op is None:
        break
        
    t, skill, b, orig_idx = best_op
    result.append(orig_idx)
    
    if t == 1:
        current_a[skill] = b
    elif t == 2:
        current_a[skill] += b
    else:  # multiply
        current_a[skill] *= b

print(len(result))
if result:
    print(' '.join(map(str, result)))
else:
    print()
```
